## How to read this document

The kernel is organized into seven parts. Each Part answers one question:

| Part | Question |
|---|---|
| I — Substrate | What exists? |
| II — Instruments | How is the substrate measured, visualized, transformed, or read? |
| III — Atlases and Structural Classes | What kinds of atoms or regimes appear across the substrate? |
| IV — Dynamics and Walk Grammar | How do walks move? |
| V — The 720 Factorial-Shell and 1001 Complement Instance | What survives as the local 720/1001 worked instance once the atlas is primary? |
| VI — Applications, Tooling, and AI | What is the framework for? |
| VII — Status, Scope, Open Problems, Notation, Bibliography | What is proved, what is conditional, what is open? |

Every claim carries a status label from the frozen v2.2 vocabulary. The status partition is not annotative — it is the integrity contract that makes an atlas-first architecture survivable.

Theorem content is preserved where the audit leaves it active. Universe-of-discourse is reframed: theorems framed as "about the substrate" in v2.7.x are reframed as "atom-local," "instrument-local," or "atlas-internal" in v3.0, and v3.2.0 further corrects statements whose prior status exceeded the supplied evidence.

**Dual-reference convention (v3.0).** Every subsection in Parts I–VII carries a three-line header block — `**Source:**` (original v2.7.14 / v3.0-alpha label), `**v3.0 location:**` (Part-number address), `**Status:**` — to keep the full fold auditable against v2.7.14. Original theorem / remark / note labels are preserved both in the per-subsection header block and inline within each moved block. New Part-number addresses are *added*, not substitutions: original v2.7.14 numbering remains visible for audit throughout. The structured *forward* inverse of these `Source:` headers — resolving a v2.7.x §-label to its v3.0 Part address(es), with a dedicated §9.7 cluster detail table — is the fold concordance at Part VII / VII.2a.

**Roots-of-unity register discipline (v3.1.18).** Phase claims in the helix family, the atlas, and the §9.7 embedding are stated first in roots-of-unity form; degrees and decimals are display annotations only — labels for human sight, not the object. A "30° rotation" is, in kernel language, multiplication by $\zeta_{12}$; a visual zero is an algebraic cancellation among roots of unity (e.g. $1 + \omega_6^3 = 0$). For the $\Omega = 2$ slice of a constant six-prime crystal, the outer hexagon and inner star are the two $\mu_6$-cosets of $\mu_{12}$ — $\langle\omega_6\rangle$ (outer, even $\zeta_{12}$ powers) and $\zeta_{12}\langle\omega_6\rangle$ (inner star, odd powers) — and the inner star's two triangles are the $\mu_3$-cosets $\zeta_{12}\langle\zeta_3\rangle$ and $i\langle\zeta_3\rangle = \zeta_{12}^{3}\langle\zeta_3\rangle$. Metric magnitudes such as $\sqrt{3}/3$ are *not* roots of unity; they live in the conductor-enlarged real subfield under the $\sqrt{\cdot}$ clause of Lemma 9.7q (Part II / II.15). This is a representation discipline, not a theorem: no kernel claim is expanded. *Degrees are labels for human sight; roots of unity are the object.*

The Lucretian governing principle — *naturae species ratioque*, the look and logic of nature — frames the document. Full statement: Part VII / VII.3.


**Register separation contract (v3.2.0).** The following registers are distinct even when they share divisor labels:

1. **Atlas/Hasse register:** $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}} = \{\mathrm{Div}(N) : N \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\}$, with nodes $d \mid N$, Hasse covers, gcd/lcm operations, product decompositions, and atlas morphisms.
2. **Vernier/phasor register:** finite six-parameter phasor instruments over $(n_1, \varphi_3, s, t_1, t_2, n_6, \alpha)$; the general real-axis contact rule is $n_3 \mid t_2 s$ within the finite instrument range.
3. **Harmonic/exact-conductor register:** signals $x \in \ell^2(\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z})$, Ramanujan/supercharacter channels, exact-conductor subspaces $V_q$, and conductor projectors $P_q$.
4. **Hasse Hilbert register:** $H_{\mathrm{Hasse}}(N) = \ell^2(\mathrm{Div}(N)) \cong \bigotimes_i \mathbb{C}^{a_i+1}$ for $N=\prod_i p_i^{a_i}$, with basis $|d\rangle$ or $|e_1,\ldots,e_m\rangle$, inner product making that basis orthonormal, dimension $\tau(N)$, and prime-direction raising/lowering along Hasse covers after an operator convention is declared.
5. **Harmonic Hilbert register:** $H_{\mathrm{harm}}(N) = \ell^2(\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}) \cong \mathbb{C}^N = \bigoplus_{q\mid N} V_q$, with sample/Fourier bases, dimension $N$, and orthogonal conductor projectors $P_q$.
6. **Application/tooling evidence register:** HTML programs, viewers, trainers, comments, UI labels, and staged notices are implementation evidence only; they are not theorem authority.

Shared divisor labels do not identify these spaces and do not imply a canonical unitary map. At $N=720$, $\dim H_{\mathrm{Hasse}}(720)=\tau(720)=30$, while $\dim H_{\mathrm{harm}}(720)=720$. The valid Hasse-harmonic bridge is a divisor-indexed measurement map such as $x \mapsto (E_q^{\mathrm{full}}(x))_{q\mid N}$; it is generally nonlinear, loses phase, and is not Hasse-edge energy dynamics.

ASCII aliases used in release notes and validators: `H_Hasse(N)` denotes $H_{\mathrm{Hasse}}(N)$ and `H_harm(N)` denotes $H_{\mathrm{harm}}(N)$.

**Energy notation contract (v3.2.0).** Before any Parseval/Plancherel statement, distinguish
$$
E_q^{\mathrm{line}}(x) = \frac{|\langle x,c_q\rangle|^2}{\|c_q\|^2},
\qquad
E_q^{\mathrm{full}}(x) = \|P_q x\|^2.
$$
$E_q^{\mathrm{line}}$ is the coherent scalar / Ramanujan-line / Wintner statistic. $E_q^{\mathrm{full}}$ is the full exact-conductor subspace energy and is the object used in active Parseval/Plancherel identities. Windowed forms are written $E_{q,W}^{\mathrm{line}}$ and $E_{q,W}^{\mathrm{full}}$ after declaring support, absolute or reanchored indexing, normalization, and taper policy. An unqualified $E_q$ is allowed only when a local section declares which object it means.

# Part I — Substrate

## I.1 Prime factorization and divisor atoms

**Source:** §2 preamble (v2.7.14); Def A.1 (v3.0-alpha).  
**v3.0 location:** Part I / I.1.  
**Status:** definitional.

For $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$, the *atom indexed by $n$* is the divisor lattice $\mathrm{Div}(n)$ with its canonical Hasse structure: nodes are positive divisors of $n$; covers are single-prime multiplications $d \to d \cdot p$ for prime $p$ with $d \cdot p \mid n$. The atom carries intrinsic geometry — covering relations, factorial grading $\Omega$ (Part I / I.6), fibered decomposition (Part I / I.8), prime-exponent coordinates, and where applicable basis/complement structure — entirely from its index $n$.

Each atom is a finite, ranked, distributive lattice with bottom $1$ and top $n$. The trivial atom is $\mathrm{Div}(1) = \{1\}$; the simplest non-trivial atoms are $\mathrm{Div}(p)$ for prime $p$, each a two-element chain.

The substrate is the family of atoms, not any one atom: see Part I / I.2.

---

## I.2 The positive atlas $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$

**Source:** Def A.2 (v3.0-alpha).  
**v3.0 location:** Part I / I.2.  
**Status:** definitional.

The *positive atlas* is the family
$$
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}} \;:=\; \{\mathrm{Div}(n) : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\}.
$$

$\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$ is the FVLT substrate. Specific atoms enter the framework as *instruments* or worked instances — selected by named criteria such as closure exhaustion, loop topology threshold, and basis/complement architecture (Part V / V.2 for the $N_0 = 720$ factorial-shell selection theorem via Thm 8.20h.3) — or as *atlases in the macro-section sense* (Part III) — regimes collected per topic.

The positive atlas does not single out any atom. Local selections, including the $720$ factorial-shell / $1001$ complement instance, are *instrumentation choices* justified by named criteria, not substrate selections. The substrate is plural; the instruments are specific.

---

## I.3 Signed completion $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}$

**Source:** §1.1 signed-completion clause (v3.0-alpha); §C companion-spine (Phase 1.a complete).  
**v3.0 location:** Part I / I.3.  
**Status:** definitional / structural.

The *signed completion* of the atlas is
$$
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times} \;:=\; \{\mathrm{Div}^{+}(n),\, \mathrm{Div}^{-}(n) : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\},
$$
with $\mathrm{Div}^{+}(n) = \mathrm{Div}(n)$ (the positive sheet, identical to the atom of $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$) and $\mathrm{Div}^{-}(n)$ the oppositely oriented copy indexed by $-n$.

The two sheets are **lattice-congruent but orientation-distinguished**: divisor combinatorics, Hasse structure, polygon multiset, $\Omega_{\max}$, and the Rmk 9.7k local-comb cardinality (Part II / II.18) are identical between $\mathrm{Div}^{+}(n)$ and $\mathrm{Div}^{-}(n)$; what distinguishes them is the chirality / helix-orientation convention installed in Part II / II.13.

**Same cardinality, different structure.** Although $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$ and $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}$ are both countably infinite, they are not the same structured object: $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}$ is an oriented two-sheeted completion of the positive atlas, with $0$ retained only as a non-atomic carrier hub (Part I / I.4).

**FVLT signed completion is chirality-sensitive** by definitional choice (companion §C body, Rmk C.2). Negative atoms carry reversed helix orientation relative to their positive counterparts ($\theta \mapsto -\theta$ in the Part II / II.14 embedding); this convention is what makes sign geometrically visible in phase and winding invariants. Without it, signed completion is bare index relabeling and the clinamen reading (Part II / II.6) dissolves.

The three orthogonal $\mathbb{Z}/2$ involutions on the geometric realization — chirality flip $\chi$, Weyl reflection $w$, signed completion — are declared distinct in v3.0; their coupling is post-v3.0 scope. See companion §C / §G routing notes (Part II / II.17, Part IV / IV.12).

---

## I.4 Zero as carrier hub, not atom

**Source:** Rmk 9.3e zero-clause (v2.7.7); Rmk 9.3f (v2.7.8); v3.0-alpha §1.1.  
**v3.0 location:** Part I / I.4.  
**Status:** definitional.

The integer $0$ is excluded from the atlas index set for a **structural** reason: $\mathrm{Div}_{\mathrm{literal}}(0)$ has infinitely many divisors (every nonzero integer divides $0$), so $0$ does not give a finite atom. The exclusion is structural, not policy.

$0$ is retained as the *additive carrier hub* through which signed atlas walks may pass — handled by the carrier-event grammar of Part IV / IV.4 (Rmk 9.3f atom / jump / zero typology, v2.7.8, preserved) and Rmk 9.3e (zero-clause, v2.7.7, preserved).

**Identity symmetry (Rmk 9.3e, preserved).** $1$ is the multiplicative root of every $\mathrm{Div}(n)$; $0$ is the additive hub of the carrier layer. Two roots, two layers, not interchangeable.

> *Zero is singular relative to positive divisor-lattice measurement, not singular absolutely.* In the carrier, $0$ is the additive origin / launch hub; in the atlas, $0$ is excluded.

---

## I.5 Atlas-as-lattice: gcd/lcm structure

**Source:** Rmk A.3 + Lem A.3.1 + Cor A.3.2 (v3.0-alpha); Prop 2.2b.2 (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part I / I.5.  
**Status:** unconditional.

Order $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$ by divisor-set inclusion, equivalently by induced sublattice inclusion: $\mathrm{Div}(m) \leq_{\mathcal{A}} \mathrm{Div}(n)$ iff $\mathrm{Div}(m) \subseteq \mathrm{Div}(n)$. When this holds, $\mathrm{Div}(m)$ is a sublattice of $\mathrm{Div}(n)$ in the technical sense — closed under the gcd and lcm of $\mathrm{Div}(n)$, with cover relations preserved.

**Lemma I.5.1** (corresponds to v3.0-alpha Lemma A.3.1, unconditional). *$\mathrm{Div}(m) \subseteq \mathrm{Div}(n) \iff m \mid n$.*

*Proof.* If $m \mid n$, every divisor of $m$ divides $n$ by transitivity. Conversely, $m \in \mathrm{Div}(m)$ always; if $\mathrm{Div}(m) \subseteq \mathrm{Div}(n)$ then $m \in \mathrm{Div}(n)$, i.e., $m \mid n$. $\square$

**Corollary I.5.2** (corresponds to v3.0-alpha Corollary A.3.2). The map $\mathrm{Div}(n) \mapsto n$ gives an order isomorphism
$$
(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}, \leq_{\mathcal{A}}) \;\cong\; (\mathbb{N}_{>0}, \mid).
$$

$(\mathbb{N}_{>0}, \mid)$ is a lattice with $\gcd$ as meet, $\mathrm{lcm}$ as join, $1$ as bottom, and no top. Therefore $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$ is a lattice of the same structural kind as each of its atoms. Its meet and join operations correspond to arithmetic operations on indices:
$$
\mathrm{Div}(m) \wedge_{\mathcal{A}} \mathrm{Div}(n) = \mathrm{Div}(\gcd(m, n)),
$$
$$
\mathrm{Div}(m) \vee_{\mathcal{A}} \mathrm{Div}(n) = \mathrm{Div}(\mathrm{lcm}(m, n)).
$$

The atlas-meet equals the *atom intersection* (Proposition 2.2b.2, v2.7.14, preserved): $\mathrm{Div}(m) \cap \mathrm{Div}(n) = \mathrm{Div}(\gcd(m, n))$. The atlas-join equals the *smallest atom containing both*. Rmk 2.2e (Part I / I.8) supplies the further structural relation: for coprime $m, n$, atlas-join factors as direct product of atoms.

**Atom-internal counterpart** (*interpretive cross-reference; depends Rmk 9.7h*). The atlas-level gcd/lcm structure has an atom-internal geometric counterpart in the Part II / II.16 polygonal fiber assembly: atoms decompose into prime-power fibers just as the atlas composes atoms by divisibility, gcd/lcm, and coprime product structure. The same compositional logic operates at two scales — within an atom, divisors factor into prime-power positions; across the atlas, atoms compose by coprime product. Atlas-meet trivial ↔ atom-level coprimality; atlas-join factors as direct product ↔ fibered decomposition makes the product geometric.

**Recursion hinge of v3.0.** The FVLT substrate is self-similar in a precise, not merely evocative, sense: the atlas, like each of its atoms, is a divisibility-ordered lattice. The same arithmetic operations that organize the divisors inside an atom organize the atoms inside the atlas.

<!-- id: LEM-I.5.3-DIV-AUT-SQUAREFREE | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: classical | depends: I.5 -->
**Lemma I.5.3 (Automorphism group of squarefree divisor lattices, v3.1.9, new, unconditional).** For a squarefree integer $N = p_1 p_2 \cdots p_m$ with $m$ distinct prime factors, the automorphism group of the graded poset $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ is the symmetric group on the prime indices:
$$
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{Div}(N)) \;\cong\; S_m.
$$
Each automorphism acts by permuting the $m$ primes; the action preserves Hasse covers, the $\Omega$-grading, and the gcd/lcm structure. Specifically:

- $\mathrm{Div}(30) = \mathrm{Div}(2 \cdot 3 \cdot 5)$ has $\mathrm{Aut} \cong S_3$, order 6.
- $\mathrm{Div}(1001) = \mathrm{Div}(7 \cdot 11 \cdot 13)$ has $\mathrm{Aut} \cong S_3$, order 6.
- $\mathrm{Div}(30030) = \mathrm{Div}(2 \cdot 3 \cdot 5 \cdot 7 \cdot 11 \cdot 13)$ has $\mathrm{Aut} \cong S_6$, order 720.

The $S_6$ realization on $\mathrm{Div}(30030)$ is the faithful structural setting for $S_6$ group theory in the FVLT framework, distinct from $|S_6| = 720 = |\mathrm{Div}(720)|$ as a numerical coincidence (which lives in the integer-cardinality register, not the poset-automorphism register — see Part V / V.16, Open Problem 18).

## I.6 Factorial grading, shells, and containment

**Source:** §2.1, §2.3 (v2.7.14); Def 2.2d (v2.7.14); Note 2.2a.2 two-senses (v2.7.9).  
**v3.0 location:** Part I / I.6.  
**Status:** definitional / structural.

For $d \in \mathrm{Div}(N)$ with $d = \prod_i p_i^{e_i}$, the *factorial grading* is
$$
\Omega(d) \;=\; \sum_i e_i.
$$
$\Omega$ measures the total prime-multiplicity weight; $\Omega(1) = 0$, $\Omega(N) = \Omega_{\max}(N) = \sum_i a_i$ where $N = \prod_i p_i^{a_i}$.

**Factorial shells (§2.1, preserved).** The factorial filtration $N_k = k!$ supplies the canonical sequence of shells nested by divisibility:
$$
N_1 = 1 \mid N_2 = 2 \mid N_3 = 6 \mid N_4 = 24 \mid N_5 = 120 \mid N_6 = 720 \mid N_7 = 5040 \mid \cdots
$$

The factorial filtration is constructively detected by single-phasor zero-scanning at integer factorial spin in the canonical Vernier gearbox; see Algorithm Note ALG-1 (Single-Phasor Divisor Gate for Hasse Atom Construction). The Q-residual $|\Im(z_1+z_2)|$ records antipodal-gearbox symmetry at integer spin and is not the divisor detector; the single-phasor imaginary part $\Im(z_1) = 0$ is.

Above the contact condition, the integer-$N$ off-axis phasor stratifies the operating-shell space by $\gcd(N, k!)$, with each gcd stratum $S_d = \{N : \gcd(N, k!) = d\}$ indexed by a divisor $d \mid k!$. The operating value $N$ here is the factorial-strip output $N = k! - n_7 = k! + 1 - \varphi_3$ ($n_7 = \varphi_3 - 1$ the walk position; reference $\varphi_3 = 1 \Rightarrow N = k!$), read on the $t_1 = t_2 = 1$ reference gearbox tooth — the strip and gearbox parameters are upstream of the stratification (see ALG-2 cross-reference at Part II / II.2 for the full reduction chain). The ALG-1 contact readout is not the single stratum $S_{k!}$; it is the contact-normalized condition $m_1 = N/\gcd(N, k!) = 1$, equivalently $N \mid k!$. Thus ALG-1 is recovered as the union of divisor contact points across the gcd-stratified scan, while off-contact samples $m_1 > 1$ carry gcd information that the contact-only readout discards. See Algorithm Note ALG-2 (Cyclotomic Stratification of the Single-Phasor Curve at Integer Spin).

Between adjacent factorial shells $N_k = k!$ and $N_{k+1} = (k+1)!$, the angular winding of the continuous-$N$ phasor at spin $s = (k+1)!$ recovers the shell ratio $r = N_{k+1}/N_k = k+1$ via $\Delta\theta = \pi\ell(r-1)$ with $\ell = s/N_{k+1} = 1$. The integer-valued shell ratio is geometric content; the combinatorial atomicity of $r$ in $\mathrm{Div}((k+1)!)$ identifies whether the shell step is a Hasse cover ($k+1$ prime) or a composite divisibility step ($k+1$ composite, with the ratio decomposing into its prime factorization inside the larger lattice). See Algorithm Note ALG-3 (Continuous-Phasor Winding Recovery of Divisor Lattice Ratios).

The shell function (Definition 2.2d, preserved): $N_{\mathrm{base}} \mid N_t^* \mid N_t$ is the canonical nesting of base lens, operative lens, and current lens (Part IV / IV.1). The nesting is preserved under lens contraction; lens contraction is a view change, not erasure.

**The $k$-rough spectrum (§2.3, preserved).** For atom $\mathrm{Div}(N)$, the $k$-rough spectrum classifies divisors by their smallest prime factor at threshold $k$. The spectrum partitions $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ along the "rough" axis complementary to the "smooth" axis indexed by $\Omega$.

**Two senses of shell (Note 2.2a.2, v2.7.9, interpretive, preserved).** The factorial filtration $N_k = k!$ has two operationally distinct readings:

- **Lattice shell** — $\mathrm{Div}(N_0)$ hosting nodes up to $\Omega = \Omega_{\max}(N_0)$. Reads prime-support and multiplicity growth in the visited trajectory.
- **Polynomial shell** — factorial filtration rung $N_k = k!$ that the descent-through-zero walk's signature requires to standardize, $k \leq \Omega(n) + 2$. Reads denominator clearance in the polynomial readback (Part IV / IV.7).

The two senses are independent. A node $n = 720$ sits comfortably in the lattice shell at $\Omega = 7$, while its descent-through-zero walk would require polynomial shell $k = 9$ — overflowing $S_6$ in the polynomial register. Both index into $N_k = k!$, but they measure different objects.

## I.7 Lattice metric and geodesic distance

**Source:** §2.2, Cor 2.2b.1 (v2.7.14) — substrate-geometry half of SPLIT.  
**v3.0 location:** Part I / I.7.  
**Status:** unconditional.

Within atom $\mathrm{Div}(N)$, the *lattice metric* $d(m, n)$ is the length of the shortest path in the Hasse diagram between divisors $m, n$ of $N$. Cover edges are unit-length; the metric is geodesic on the underlying graph.

**Corollary 2.2b.1 (v2.7.14, preserved).** *$d(n, 1) = \Omega(n)$.*

That is: the distance from a divisor to the unit equals the prime-multiplicity weight of the divisor. Monotone descent through Hasse covers from $n$ to $1$ has exactly $\Omega(n)$ steps; no shorter path exists because each cover step changes $\Omega$ by exactly $\pm 1$.

The lattice metric is a substrate-geometry fact about atoms. Its relationship to polynomial-signature degree of descent-through-zero walks lives in the dynamics register (Part IV / IV.7).

---

## I.8 Coprime products and fibered decomposition

**Source:** Rmk 2.2e (v2.7.10).  
**v3.0 location:** Part I / I.8.  
**Status:** unconditional.

<!-- id: RMK-2.2e-FIBERED-DECOMPOSITION | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 2.2b -->
**Remark 2.2e (Fibered Decomposition of Coprime Products, v2.7.10, preserved).** For $N = A \cdot B$ with $\gcd(A, B) = 1$, the map
$$
\varphi: \mathrm{Div}(N) \to \mathrm{Div}(A) \times \mathrm{Div}(B), \quad n \mapsto (\gcd(n, A), \gcd(n, B))
$$
is an order isomorphism with inverse $(a, b) \mapsto a \cdot b$. The divisor lattice decomposes as the Cartesian product of partial orders with corresponding Cartesian graph product on Hasse diagrams; cover edges are inherited from factors with no mixed-direction covers.

**720/1001 bridge instance.** $N_{\mathrm{FUSED}} = N_{\mathrm{BASIS}} \cdot N_{\mathrm{COMPLEMENT}} = 720 \cdot 1001 = 720720$ gives
$$
\mathrm{Div}(720720) \cong \mathrm{Div}(720) \times \mathrm{Div}(1001)
$$
with $|\mathrm{Div}(720720)| = 30 \cdot 8 = 240$. The trainer's fibered embedding renders this directly (Part VI / VI.8): each divisor $n = b \cdot c$ at world coordinates determined by $(e_2, e_3, e_5)$ within a basis-cell, the cell indexed by $(e_7, e_{11}, e_{13})$ across a $2 \times 2 \times 2$ super-arrangement. Every divisor occupies a unique geometric position; no projection from higher dimension is performed.

General statement extends to any pairwise coprime decomposition $N = \prod_i N_i$. Rmk 2.2e is the substrate-level theorem; its instrument-realization geometry is Part II / II.16 (CRT fibered presentation, Rmk 9.7h).

---

## I.9 Factorization regimes and construction costs

**Source:** Rmk 1.6a (v2.7.10) — substrate-construction half of SPLIT.  
**v3.0 location:** Part I / I.9.  
**Status:** unconditional.

<!-- id: RMK-1.6a-FACTORIZATION-REGIMES | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 1.4, 2.2 -->
**Remark 1.6a (Factorization Regime, v2.7.10, preserved).** Three operationally distinct factorization regimes:

- **Cold** — factor new ambient $N$ from scratch, $O(\sqrt{N})$ trial division for direct rendering range; the trainer does not assume cold factorization is available for large unstructured $N$.
- **Warm** — within known lens $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ with prime support $\pi(N)$, divisibility against $\pi(N)$ only, $O(r \log m)$ where $r = |\pi(N)|$.
- **Walk-relative** — atom step $n \mapsto n \cdot p^{\varepsilon}$ updates one exponent coordinate, $O(1)$.

The trainer's operational cost is dominated by warm and walk-relative regimes; cold factorization is invoked only at lattice construction or when the user supplies a target outside the current ambient's prime support.

**Rendering cap discipline.** The rendering cap on $N$ is a *node-count* cap $\prod_i (a_i + 1) \leq N_{\mathrm{render}}$ — **not** a factorization-cost cap. Lattices with structurally tractable construction sequences (factorial, lcm-of-range, axis-add expansions) reach values far past direct-rendering cap without invoking cold factorization. The regime distinction is structural, not algorithmic.

The observation-context partner of this remark — Rmk 1.6b (three observation contexts: node / visit / transition) — lives at the readout register, Part II / II.19.

---

## I.10 Coordinate substrate vs closure substrate

**Source:** Rmk 1.5b substrate-half (v2.7.10); §1.1 (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part I / I.10.  
**Status:** interpretive.

The Lucretian governing principle of the framework — *naturae species ratioque*, the look and logic of nature — frames the substrate question at first principles. Full statement of the principle: Part VII / VII.3. The substrate is what FVLT operates *on*; the closure structure is what FVLT *finds* once observation begins.

<!-- id: RMK-1.5b-SUBSTRATE-DUALITY | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 1.4, 2.2 -->
**Remark 1.5b (Coordinate Substrate vs Closure-of-Description Substrate, v2.7.10, preserved — substrate half).** Two lattices arise over the trainer's primitives:

- The *coordinate substrate* $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ — navigation, geometry, prime-exponent position. The substrate of Parts I.1 through I.9 above.
- The *closure-of-description substrate* $\Lambda_{\mathrm{desc}}(O, \Sigma, I)$ — closure questions from a Galois connection on derivation operators. The closure substrate's machinery lives in Part II / II.19 as a readout; here we name only the distinction.

**Load-bearing sentence:** *the two lattices share the same primitive objects but answer different questions.*

The canonical case ($\Sigma$ = prime-power divisibility predicates over the join-irreducibles of $\mathrm{Div}(N)$) recovers $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ up to isomorphism via the order-ideal representation theorem [Bir40]. When $\Sigma$ is observation-derived (walker history, lens state, atom/jump/zero classification), $\Lambda_{\mathrm{desc}}$ diverges from $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ and provides genuinely new structure. Naming follows the framework's principle of description over prescription.

This distinction is what separates Parts I from Parts II–VI: Part I is coordinate substrate; the readouts of Part II include the closure-of-description substrate as one instrument among many.

---

# Part II — Instruments

The substrate of Part I becomes legible through *instruments* — readouts that measure, visualize, transform, or address the atoms. Part II inventories the instruments; theorems live atom-locally within each instrument's canonical exemplar (Part V) or atlas-locally within each instrument's regime (Part III). The instruments are not ranked: each reads structure the others cannot. Hasse and harmonic register the divisor as both a coordinate and a frequency (II.3, *load-bearing*). Helix and crystal-graph give the divisor as both a geometric bead and a representation-theoretic basis vertex (II.13, II.17). Each instrument has its scope; together they triangulate the substrate.

## II.1 Hasse readout

**Source:** §1.4 (v2.7.14); Rmk 1.2, Rmk 1.2a (v2.7.14); Rmk 9.3d residue-fan half.  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.1.  
**Status:** definitional / interpretive (per-block).

The first instrument is the Hasse diagram of an atom: a navigational coordinate substrate.

<!-- id: RMK-1.2-DIVISOR-LATTICE-AS-MEASUREMENT-BASIS | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: none -->
**Remark 1.2 (Divisor Lattice as Measurement Basis, preserved).** In the standard mathematical literature, Hasse diagrams illustrate partial orders — the divisor lattice of $n$, the subgroup lattice of a finite group, Boolean algebras, concept lattices. In every case, the diagram is *descriptive* — a picture of a structure already understood.

In the FVLT, the divisor lattice $\mathrm{Div}(N_0)$ serves as the *measurement apparatus itself*:

(i) Each node $q \in \mathrm{Div}(N_0)$ is a frequency channel carrying the Ramanujan basis function $c_q$.

(ii) The covering edges ($q \to pq$ for prime $p \in B$) are energy flow paths between resolution scales.

(iii) The $\Omega$-stratification (number of prime factors with multiplicity) is the tensor rank of each channel.

(iv) The CRT decomposition assigns each node a coordinate in the product torus $T^{|B|}$.

(v) The dual lattice $\mathrm{Div}(\mathrm{Pr})$ extends the instrument to a complementary set of prime channels.

Prior applications of Ramanujan sums to signal processing (Bruns 1903, Schroeder 1986, Planat [P09], Vaidyanathan & Tenneti [V14a, V14b]) treat the divisors of $N_0$ as a flat list of arithmetic frequencies. The FVLT exploits the *lattice structure* — the partial order, covering relations, $\Omega$-stratification, CRT fiber decomposition, and primary–dual pairing — as an integral part of the analysis. The Hasse diagram is not a visualization of the transform; it is the transform's coordinate system.

<!-- id: RMK-1.2a-UNIVERSAL-LATTICE | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 1.2 -->
**Remark 1.2a (Ambient lcm lattice, v3.2.0 scoped).** The 37-node $720/1001$ instrument $\mathrm{Div}(720) \cup \mathrm{Div}(1001)$ (Part V / V.5) is a sub-topology of the ambient lcm construction: for any pair of positive integers $a, b$, the minimal divisor lattice containing both is $\mathrm{Div}(\mathrm{lcm}(a, b))$. The worked bridge instance at $N_0 = 720720$ is a constrained sub-topology of $\mathrm{Div}(720720)$, with the constraint being the bridge-through-$1$ routing between basis and complement sectors (Part V / V.7). When a signal contains structure at a prime $p \geq 17$ (outside both $B$ and $B'$), the atlas extends to $\mathrm{Div}(\mathrm{lcm}(720720, n))$: the lattice metric (Part I / I.7), covering edges, and walk engine (Part IV) all generalize without modification. The 37-node topology is a justified local choice, not a boundary or center of the formalism.

**Classical fan reading (Rmk 9.3d, residue-fan half, preserved).** The classical residue fan $y = Nx + b$ is the modular-arithmetic substrate that the Hasse readout discretizes: the primitive arms ($\gcd(b, N) = 1$) are the reduced residue classes modulo $N$, i.e., the arithmetic progressions appearing in Dirichlet's theorem on primes in APs. The residue-ray scaffolding is classical, not FVLT-originated. The 720-architectural reading of the fan — the selection rule under which it reads as basis-versus-complement architecture — lives in Part V / V.10.

---

## II.2 Harmonic / Ramanujan / supercharacter readout

**Source:** §5.5 preamble (v2.7.14); Rmk 5.7a (v2.7).  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.2.  
**Status:** unconditional / interpretive (per-block).

The harmonic readout pairs each divisor with its supercharacter / Ramanujan channel. Where the Hasse readout addresses *position*, the harmonic readout addresses *measurement* (Part II / II.3, load-bearing).

**Frame (§5.5 preamble, v3.2.0 notation corrected).** The supercharacter $c_q$ on $\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}$ is the sum of primitive $q$-th roots of unity that participate in the divisor $q \mid N$. The scalar coherent statistic $E_q^{\mathrm{line}}$ measures energy on the Ramanujan line spanned by $c_q$; the full exact-conductor energy $E_q^{\mathrm{full}}$ measures energy in the whole conductor subspace $V_q$. The supercharacter machinery is the harmonic instrument; the Ramanujan sums are its native vocabulary.

<!-- id: RMK-5.7a-COPRIME-INDICATOR-DECOMPOSITION | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: adapted | depends: 5.5, 8.19 -->
**Remark 5.7a (Coprime Indicator Decomposition, v2.7, preserved).** For squarefree $q$ and any $n$ with $\gcd(q, n) = q$ (so $q \mid n$),
$$
E_q^{\mathrm{full}}(\chi_n) \;=\; \frac{\mu(q)^2 \, \varphi(n)^2}{n^2 \, \varphi(q)}
$$
at $L = N = n$. Structural consequences: squarefree-skeleton support, radical invariance, closed-form concentration ratio. Calibration role: $\chi_n$ serves as a known-spectrum reference for $\mathrm{ce}_z$. Empirically verified to two decimals on three independent radicals ($30$, $210$, $1001$) via Ratioque calibration chamber v0.1.1 — the first complete kernel-prediction → instrument-measurement loop.

**Classicality of the closed form (v3.1.6, annotation).** The Rmk 5.7a closed form is a re-derivation of classical content, with no FVLT-original input. It reduces in three steps to: the value of a Ramanujan sum on units, $c_q(s) = \mu(q)$ for $\gcd(s, q) = 1$ (Ramanujan 1918); the count of reduced residues, $\#\{s : \gcd(s, n) = 1\} = \varphi(n)$ (Euler); and the Ramanujan-sum orthogonality $\sum_{s \bmod n} |c_q(s)|^2 = n\,\varphi(q)$ over a reduced residue system (Carmichael 1932). The energy functional here is the Ramanujan full subspace projection energy $E_q^{\mathrm{full}}$ of Vaidyanathan's published framework. Rmk 5.7a *records and uses* this identity as a calibration anchor — $\chi_n$ as a known-spectrum reference; the identity is classical, the calibration role is the FVLT contribution. The remark's *status* label (*unconditional*) is unchanged; per Chris audit the HTML-metadata `provenance` field is set to `adapted` — classical content adapted into the FVLT register, the calibration-anchor framing being the FVLT contribution. Determination: `fvlt_rmk_5_7a_classicality_resolution.md` (May 18, 2026); that note's downstream proposal — reclassifying the `ratioque_chamber` R1 rung from internal cross-check to external validation — is chamber-package scope (a `ratioque_chamber` v1.0.1 patch), separately audited, and is not a kernel change.

The harmonic-register closed form $E_q^{\mathrm{full}}(\chi_n) = \mu(q)^2 \varphi(n)^2 / (n^2 \varphi(q))$ is the supercharacter-side expression of an algebraic structure also readable on the phasor side. The compact $N$-form used below is a reduced-curve notation, not the primitive viewer formula. In the viewer's full six-parameter formula the phasor is $z = n_6 (n_1! - n_7)^\alpha e^{i k s \pi / n_3}$: the operating value is the factorial-strip value $N = n_1! - n_7 = k! + 1 - \varphi_3$ with $n_7 = \varphi_3 - 1$ the walk position below the factorial shell, $n_1! - n_7$ is the radial-amplitude and gearbox base, $n_3 = n_1! - n_7$ is retained as the angular-denominator container even where numerically equal to it, and the gearbox teeth are $k_1 = (n_1! - n_7) t_1 - t_2$ and $k_2 = (n_1! - n_7) t_1 + t_2$. ALG-2 is stated on the reference reduced curve $t_1 = t_2 = 1$, where $k_1 = N - 1$ and the single phasor becomes $z_1(N, s) = N^\alpha e^{i(N-1)s\pi/N}$. At integer spin $s$, this reference single phasor stratifies the operating-shell space by $d = \gcd(s, N)$ into cyclotomic-order classes indexed by divisors of $s$, with stratum sizes $\varphi(s/d)$ in any $|s|$-length window. Three cyclotomic orders are distinguished — Euler-residual $m_0$, plotted-phasor $m_{\mathrm{plot}}$, contact-normalized $m_1$ — each labeling a different reading convention; the contact stratum $m_1 = 1$ recovers the ALG-1 divisor predicate. The cyclotomic stratification by $d = \gcd(s, N)$ is thus a reduced-curve readout; the factorial strip and gearbox parameters $(n_7, t_1, t_2)$ are upstream structure, not additional cyclotomic strata. See Algorithm Note ALG-2.

The harmonic readout is compatible with coprime factorization via Theorem 8.19 (harmonic register): $c_{AB}(s) = c_A(s) \cdot c_B(s)$ for $\gcd(A, B) = 1$. Same factorization principle in the harmonic register that Part I / I.8 Rmk 2.2e provides in the coordinate register.

## II.3 Hasse–harmonic pairing

**Source:** Rmk 1.5c (v2.7.11).  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.3.  
**Status:** interpretive.

<!-- id: RMK-1.5c-SAME-DIVISOR-TWO-READOUTS | register: interpretive | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: RMK-1.5b-SUBSTRATE-DUALITY, 5.5, 8.19 -->
**Remark 1.5c (Same Divisor, Two Readouts, v2.7.11, preserved).** For each ambient $N$, the same divisor $q \mid N$ participates in two readout regimes.

In the *Hasse regime*, $q$ is a coordinate node of $\mathrm{Div}(N)$. Its role is navigational: it has a position in the prime-exponent lattice, Hasse-cover adjacencies, rank $\Omega(q)$, and lens-relative relations to the unit $1$ and apex $N$.

In the *harmonic regime*, $q$ is a supercharacter / Ramanujan channel. Its role is measurement: $q$ indexes the orbit-summed cyclic readout $c_q$ over $\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}$ or over the relevant quotient / lens specified in Part II / II.2.

Thus the divisor $q$ is not merely a containment object and not merely a frequency object. It is a shared channel: its Hasse position says where the channel lives, while its supercharacter readout says what the channel measures.

The two readouts are compatible with coprime factorization. In the fused lens $N_{\mathrm{FUSED}} = 720 \cdot 1001$, the fibered coordinate presentation $\mathrm{Div}(720720) \cong \mathrm{Div}(720) \times \mathrm{Div}(1001)$ (Part I / I.8 Rmk 2.2e) and the CRT factorization of Ramanujan channels $c_{720720}(s) = c_{720}(s) \cdot c_{1001}(s)$ (Theorem 8.19) express the same basis–complement separation in the coordinate and harmonic registers respectively.

This remark introduces no new arithmetic claim; it records the architectural pairing between the coordinate material of Part II / II.1 and the harmonic material of Part II / II.2.

**Load-bearing pair (preserved):** *Hasse diagram = where the divisor channel lives; supercharacter = what that divisor channel measures.*

Under the integer-spin specialization, the pairing is one-directionally generative: the single-phasor imaginary part of the harmonic readout detects which divisors of the spin index land at zero, recovering the Hasse vertex set constructively. See Algorithm Note ALG-1.

<!-- id: RMK-II.3a-SIGNATURE-REGISTER-FACTORIZATION | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 1.5c, II.2, II.3, 5.5, 8.19 -->
**Remark II.3a (Signature Register Factorization of the Harmonic Readout, v3.1.11, new, unconditional).**
Let $N = \prod_i p_i^{a_i}$ and let $x : \{1, \ldots, L\} \to \mathbb{C}$ be a signal. The $q$-channel Ramanujan-Fourier inner product
$$
S_q \;=\; \sum_{n=1}^{L} x(n)\, c_q(n) \qquad (q \mid N)
$$
factors through the **capped $N$-valuation signature space**
$$
\Sigma_N \;:=\; \prod_i \{0, 1, \ldots, a_i\}, \qquad
\sigma_N(n) \;=\; \bigl(\min(v_{p_i}(n),\, a_i)\bigr)_i.
$$
Define the **signature mass** of $x$ at signature $\sigma \in \Sigma_N$ by
$$
X_\sigma \;=\; \sum_{n :\, \sigma_N(n) = \sigma} x(n),
$$
and the **signature-indexed Ramanujan kernel** $C_q : \Sigma_N \to \mathbb{Z}$ by
$$
C_q(\sigma) \;=\; c_q(n) \quad \text{for any } n \text{ with } \sigma_N(n) = \sigma.
$$
Then
$$
\boxed{\, S_q \;=\; \sum_{\sigma \in \Sigma_N} X_\sigma \, C_q(\sigma). \,}
$$

*Proof.* The function $c_q(n)$ depends on $n$ only through $\gcd(q, n)$. For $q \mid N$ with $q = \prod_i p_i^{e_i}$, $e_i \leq a_i$, we have
$$
\gcd(q, n) \;=\; \prod_i p_i^{\min(e_i,\, v_{p_i}(n))} \;=\; \prod_i p_i^{\min(e_i,\, \sigma_N(n)_i)},
$$
the last equality because $e_i \leq a_i$ implies $\min(e_i, v_{p_i}(n)) = \min(e_i, \min(v_{p_i}(n), a_i))$. Hence $\gcd(q, n)$, and so $c_q(n)$, are functions of $\sigma_N(n)$ alone. $C_q$ is therefore well-defined and the factorization follows by partitioning the sum over $n$ by signature. $\square$

**Cardinal identity.** $|\Sigma_N| = \prod_i (a_i + 1) = \tau(N)$. The signature space and the divisor space are the same exponent cube read in two directions:
$$
q \mid N \;\leftrightarrow\; (e_i) \in \prod_i \{0, \ldots, a_i\}, \qquad
\sigma_N(n) \;\leftrightarrow\; (\min(v_{p_i}(n), a_i)) \in \prod_i \{0, \ldots, a_i\}.
$$
The kernel $C(q, \sigma) = C_q(\sigma)$ is a $\tau(N) \times \tau(N)$ integer matrix on this cube. Multiplicativity of $c_q$ over coprime prime support (Thm 8.19) shows that $C$ tensor-decomposes across primes: $C_q(\sigma) = \prod_i C_{p_i^{e_i}}(\sigma_i)$, each factor a small $(a_i+1) \times (a_i+1)$ integer block.

**Scope.** This is a routing identity, not a new Ramanujan-sum identity. The Hasse–harmonic pairing (Rmk 1.5c) names two readouts of the same divisor $q \mid N$ — Hasse coordinate node and supercharacter / Ramanujan channel. The signature register names the *intermediate stage* through which signal mass reaches the harmonic channel: the index register $\{1, \ldots, L\}$ collects into $\Sigma_N$, then $\Sigma_N$ enters $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ through $C_q$. The supercharacter is what the divisor channel measures; the signature is where the signal mass is gathered before the measurement.

**Empirical paragraph (implementation, benchmark-dependent).** Direct evaluation of $S_q$ across $q \mid N$ via the period-precomputation form costs $O(L \cdot \tau(N))$ multiply-adds and requires a length-$q$ table for each divisor, which becomes infeasible for $q$ near $N$ in browser environments (e.g. $q = N = 901{,}800{,}900$ implies about 7.2 GB as `Float64Array`, or about 3.6 GB as `Float32Array`). Signature-register evaluation costs $O(L) + O(\tau(N)^2)$: one streaming pass to populate $X_\sigma$, followed by a $\tau(N) \times \tau(N)$ matrix-vector product. For $N = 30030^2$, $\tau(N) = 729$ and the compact transform is $\sim 5 \times 10^5$ ops; the aggregation pass dominates and for the prime-indicator signal it reduces further via the Lehmer prime-counting algorithm to sub-linear in $L$. The asymptotic speedup ratio approaches $\tau(N)$ for general signals at large $L$; runtime constants are device- and runtime-dependent and require independent benchmarking before commit.

<!-- id: RMK-II.3b-PRIME-POWER-TENSOR-DECOMPOSITION | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: II.3a, II.3, 8.19, K.2 -->
**Remark II.3b (Prime-Power Tensor Decomposition of the Signature Kernel, v3.1.12, new, unconditional).** This remark elevates the tensor-decomposition consequence already noted at the end of Rmk II.3a's Cardinal identity into its own anchored statement, and records its computational consequence (Stage 2 of the harmonic-readout cost ladder of §II.3).

Under the exponent-vector ordering of both $\Sigma_N$ and $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ — each as the cube $\prod_i \{0, \ldots, a_i\}$ — the signature-indexed Ramanujan kernel $C_N(q, \sigma) = c_q(\sigma)$ factors as a Kronecker product across prime-power axes:
$$
\boxed{\, C_N \;\cong\; \bigotimes_{p^a \,\Vert\, N}\, C_{p^a}. \,}
$$
Equivalently, in exponent-vector coordinates with $q = \prod_i p_i^{e_i}$ ($e_i \leq a_i$) and $\sigma = (s_i)_i$,
$$
C_N(q, \sigma) \;=\; \prod_i C_{p_i^{a_i}}(e_i,\, s_i),
$$
each factor $C_{p^a}$ a small $(a+1) \times (a+1)$ integer block.

*Proof.* From Rmk II.3a, $C_N(q, \sigma) = c_q(n)$ for any $n$ with $\sigma_N(n) = \sigma$. By the multiplicativity of $c_q$ over coprime prime support (Thm 8.19), $c_q(n) = \prod_i c_{p_i^{e_i}}(n)$. Each factor $c_{p_i^{e_i}}(n)$ depends on $n$ only through $\min(e_i, v_{p_i}(n)) = \min(e_i, s_i)$ (with $e_i \leq a_i$), hence only through $s_i$. Setting $C_{p_i^{a_i}}(e_i, s_i) := c_{p_i^{e_i}}(n)$ for any $n$ with $\min(v_{p_i}(n), a_i) = s_i$ gives a well-defined $(a_i+1) \times (a_i+1)$ integer matrix, and $C_N(q, \sigma) = \prod_i C_{p_i^{a_i}}(e_i, s_i)$, the Kronecker product structure. $\square$

**Computational consequence (Stage 2).** Direct evaluation of the signature-to-harmonic step of Rmk II.3a as a dense matrix-vector product costs $O(\tau(N)^2)$ multiply-adds. Under the tensor structure, the same product evaluates as successive one-axis contractions: with $X \in \mathbb{C}^{\Sigma_N}$ laid out on the cube and indexed by signature, applying $C_{p_i^{a_i}}$ along axis $i$ in turn reduces the cost to
$$
O\!\Bigl(\tau(N) \cdot \textstyle\sum_i (a_i + 1)\Bigr).
$$
The harmonic-readout cost ladder of §II.3 then carries three stages:

| Stage | Operation | Cost |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | flat sample × channel readout | $O(L \cdot \tau(N))$ |
| 1 | signature aggregation (Rmk II.3a) | $O(L) + O(\tau(N)^2)$ |
| 2 | tensor-axis contraction (Rmk II.3b) | $O(L) + O(\tau(N) \cdot \sum_i (a_i + 1))$ |

The asymptotic Stage 1 → Stage 2 gain factor is $\tau(N) / \sum_i (a_i + 1)$, growing as $2^{\omega(N)-1} / \omega(N)$ for squarefree $N$ and proportionally for moderate exponents — the regime where the canonical instrument, the squarefree primorials, and the atlas-level profile transport (§10.8) live.

**Scope.** The multiplicativity is classical; the FVLT contribution is the alignment of the Hasse product, signature register, and harmonic tensor table as the same exponent cube in three registers:
$$
\underbrace{P_{a_1+1} \,\square\, \cdots \,\square\, P_{a_r+1}}_{\text{Hasse product (Thm K.2)}}
\;\leftrightarrow\;
\underbrace{\textstyle\prod_i \{0, \ldots, a_i\}}_{\text{signature cube (Rmk II.3a)}}
\;\leftrightarrow\;
\underbrace{\textstyle\bigotimes_{p^a \,\Vert\, N} C_{p^a}}_{\text{kernel tensor table (Rmk II.3b)}}.
$$
This is the structural content of the elevation: not new arithmetic, but a sightline through three registers of one combinatorial substrate. The atlas-level profile transport (§10.8), the signature aggregation (Rmk II.3a), and the kernel tensor structure all read from this same cube.

**Positioning.** Cooley–Tukey-like in mechanism, not the FFT itself. The classical FFT exploits Kronecker structure of the DFT matrix on $\mathbb{Z}/N$ under coprime factorization $N = N_1 N_2$, enabling the usual FFT reduction from dense quadratic DFT application toward radix-factored near-linearithmic evaluation. The Stage 2 contraction exploits the same kind of Kronecker structure, but on the divisor / signature cube rather than the cyclic sample group: *DFT on cyclic index group* vs. *Ramanujan-supercharacter readout on divisor/signature cube*. The mathematics is analogous; the data structure differs. No identity is claimed.

**Empirical paragraph (implementation, benchmark-dependent).** Stage 2 operation counts at representative $N$: $N = 720$ ($\tau = 30$), $\tau^2 = 900$ vs $\tau \cdot \sum(a_i+1) = 300$ (3.0×); $N = 30030$ ($\tau = 64$), $4{,}096$ vs $768$ (5.3×); $N = 720720$ ($\tau = 240$), $57{,}600$ vs $3{,}840$ (15×); $N = 30030^2$ ($\tau = 729$), $531{,}441$ vs $13{,}122$ (40.5×); $N = (2\cdot 3\cdot 5\cdot 7\cdot 11\cdot 13)^3$ ($\tau = 4{,}096$), $16{,}777{,}216$ vs $98{,}304$ (170.7×). For Rmk II.3a's $L$-bound benchmarks the Stage 1 aggregation pass dominates and Stage 2 is invisible against it; Stage 2 matters when the same kernel is applied repeatedly at fixed $N$, where the per-application cost of the kernel step is what gets multiplied (e.g. supercharacter / atlas viewer redraws, multi-signal scans, calibration sweeps). Runtime constants are device- and runtime-dependent and require independent benchmarking before being treated as empirical performance claims. Verification companion: structural identity $C_N = \bigotimes_{p^a \,\Vert\, N} C_{p^a}$ as numpy array equality through $N = 30030$; computational identity dense vs. tensor application result-equal across test cases.

**Three-stack lineage.** Chris/ChatGPT named the structural alignment "same exponent cube in three registers" and proposed the kernel-ready Rmk II.3b formulation; Robin Miller directed the elevation framing (II.3a content stands; II.3b extracts the tensor consequence into its own anchor), audited the Cooley–Tukey analogy as map-not-territory, and ruled on the option (a) elevation; Claude verified the structural identity ($C_N = \bigotimes C_{p^a}$ as numpy array equality) and the computational identity (dense vs. tensor application result-equal) numerically through $N = 30030$ in `verify_tensor_speedup_v2.py`.

<!-- id: THM-II.3c-SUPERCLASS-HASSE-HARMONIC-BRIDGE | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: adapted | depends: 1.5c, II.3a, II.3b, II.8, II.9, 5.2, 5.3 | locked: Robin 2026-07-14 (item); v3.2.5 release LOCKED by Robin 2026-07-14 -->
**Theorem II.3c (Superclass Hasse–Harmonic Bridge, v3.2.5).** The Hasse register and the harmonic register meet canonically on the gcd-invariant superclass algebra, but the bridge is algebraic, weighted, and non-flat — **not** a free unitary identification between $\ell^2(\mathrm{Div}(N))$ and $L^2(\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z})$. This promotes Rmk 1.5c ("same divisor, two readouts") from description to theorem.

**Statement.** For $N \ge 1$, let $A_N = \{ f : \mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{C} \mid f(x) \text{ depends only on } \gcd(x,N)\}$ be the gcd-invariant superclass algebra. Then $\dim A_N = \tau(N)$, and $A_N$ carries two divisor-indexed bases:

- **class/Hasse basis** $1_d(x) = \mathbf{1}_{\gcd(x,N)=d}$, $d \mid N$;
- **harmonic/Ramanujan basis** $c_q(x)$, $q \mid N$, the Ramanujan sum of conductor $q$ pulled back to $\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}$.

The transition table from class values to Ramanujan values is the integer $\tau(N)\times\tau(N)$ matrix, invertible over $\mathbb{Q}$,
$$T_{q,d} = c_q(d) = \mu\!\left(\tfrac{q}{\gcd(q,d)}\right)\frac{\varphi(q)}{\varphi\!\left(q/\gcd(q,d)\right)}.$$
Writing $f = \sum_{q\mid N} a_q c_q$ with $f_d$ the value of $f$ on the superclass $\gcd(x,N)=d$, the Ramanujan coefficients are recovered by weighted Möbius inversion on divisor classes,
$$a_q = \frac{1}{N\varphi(q)} \sum_{d\mid N} \varphi(N/d)\, f_d\, c_q(d).$$

**Metric warning (non-flatness).** Superclass sizes are not flat: $|\{x \bmod N : \gcd(x,N)=d\}| = \varphi(N/d)$, so the inherited $L^2$ inner product on $A_N$ is $\langle f,g\rangle_N = \sum_{d\mid N} \varphi(N/d)\, f_d \overline{g_d}$, under which $\langle c_q, c_r\rangle_N = 0$ for $q \ne r$ and $\langle c_q, c_q\rangle_N = N\varphi(q)$. Thus $c_q/\sqrt{N\varphi(q)}$ are orthonormal for the inherited metric, but under the naive flat divisor metric $\sum_{d\mid N} f_d\overline{g_d}$ the same table is not unitary in general — the precise sense in which the bridge is canonical but not free.

**Phase-loss identity.** The full harmonic space decomposes by exact additive conductor, $L^2(\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}) = \bigoplus_{q\mid N} H_q$, $\dim H_q = \varphi(q)$ (II.9). The superclass algebra keeps exactly one invariant vector per conductor block, $A_N = \bigoplus_{q\mid N} \mathbb{C}\, c_q$, so the Hasse/gcd-invariant shadow loses exactly
$$\sum_{q\mid N}(\varphi(q)-1) = N - \tau(N)$$
phase dimensions — the exact statement of what the gcd-invariant Hasse shadow cannot see.

**Local prime comparison.** For $N=p$ prime the local table (columns: unit class, zero class) is $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1 & 1\\ -1 & p-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)$; at $p=2$ this is $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1 & 1\\ -1 & 1\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, Walsh up to sign/order convention. For odd $p$ it is not Walsh: the $p-1$ primitive additive characters bundle into the single Ramanujan supercharacter $c_p$. Walsh is the Boolean local case of the Hasse side; Ramanujan is the conductor-bundled harmonic side.

**Provenance.** `provenance: adapted`. Classical supercharacter theory of $\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}$ — Ramanujan sums and Hölder's closed form (Ramanujan 1918, Hölder 1936), Ramanujan-sum orthogonality (Carmichael 1932), the superclass/supercharacter pairing (Diaconis–Isaacs 2008 [DI08]). FVLT-original and the only content asserted new: (1) seating $A_N$ as the canonical non-unitary Hasse↔harmonic bridge; (2) the $N-\tau(N)$ phase-loss accounting; (3) the $p=2$ Walsh identification. Mirrors the Rmk 5.7a provenance discipline (classical content; the seating/accounting is the contribution).

**Non-claims.** No canonical unitary between flat $\ell^2(\mathrm{Div}(N))$ and $L^2(\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z})$; no dynamics; no `L1c`; no analytic-number-theory derivation; no broader collapse of the Hasse and harmonic registers. Static bridge theorem only.

**Cross-references.** Proves Rmk 1.5c (the two readouts are the two divisor-indexed bases of $A_N$, joined by $T$, inverted by Möbius inversion on $\mathrm{Div}(N)$); II.8 (Def 5.2, weighted Ramanujan sums $c_q^w$; the $w=1$ case is this classical basis); II.9 (exact-conductor block structure, to which this adds the invariant-vector restriction and phase-loss identity).

**Verification.** Companion `verify_superclass_bridge.py` (SHA-256 `4e94508f622f9fd47dffdeb8f52dd46733443cc2e6de1b3b5b95549f2bbd5e18`) recomputes $N=1..720$ — class counts, superclass sizes, transition entries, $\mathbb{Q}$-invertibility, Ramanujan orthogonality, coefficient reconstruction of every class basis vector, exact phase-loss identity — and compares to the pinned witness `RESULTS_SUPERCLASS_BRIDGE_2026-07-08.json` (SHA-256 `da8d62bd1ea0992c142f8ce8946d9f6002c088b5949e33214a116e0c15079a4c`); last run `VERIFY OK … range=1..720 maxTau=30 failures=0`. Item locked Robin 2026-07-14; full record `fableous/locked/lock_record_2026-07-14_superclass-bridge.md` and `fableous/locked/synthesis_theorem/`.

## II.4 Trilattice and Möbius partition

**Source:** §3 preamble, §3.4, §3.5 (v2.7.14); §9.4 SL(2,ℤ) + Hyperbolic-plane items.  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.4.  
**Status:** unconditional / interpretive (per-block).

The trilattice instrument reads three consecutive shells $(N_0 - 1, N_0, N_0 + 1)$ as a single hyperbolic-geometric object. At the 720 factorial-shell operating instance, the trilattice is $(719, 720, 721)$.

<!-- id: DEF-3.4-ZETA-ANGLE | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: none -->
**Definition 3.4 (Zeta-Angle, preserved).** The hyperbolic step size of the trilattice $T(N_0)$:
$$
\zeta(N_0) \;=\; \mathrm{arctanh}\!\left(\frac{1}{N_0}\right) \;=\; \frac{1}{2}\ln\!\left(\frac{N_0 + 1}{N_0 - 1}\right).
$$

<!-- id: DEF-3.5-VERNIER-RATIO | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: none -->
**Definition 3.5 (Vernier Ratio, preserved).** The multiplicative scale factor of the Farey triple:
$$
V(N_0) \;=\; \frac{N_0 + 1}{N_0 - 1} \;=\; e^{2\zeta(N_0)}.
$$

For $S_6$: $\zeta(720) = \mathrm{arctanh}(1/720) \approx 0.001389$ and $V(720) = 721/719 \approx 1.00278$.

<!-- id: RMK-3.6-GEOMETRIC-CONTENT | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: none -->
**Remark 3.6 (Geometric Content, preserved).** The zeta-angle $\zeta(N_0)$ is the hyperbolic distance between adjacent Farey neighbors at scale $N_0$ — the natural metric of the trilattice. The identity $V(N_0) = e^{2\zeta(N_0)}$ means the Vernier ratio is not an additive offset of $2$ but an exponential in the hyperbolic metric. The relationship $\tanh(\zeta(N_0)) = 1/N_0$ connects the hyperbolic step to the shell index: the Farey triple's width in hyperbolic geometry is determined by the reciprocal of the factorial.

The ratio $\zeta(N_0)/(1/N_0) \to 1$ as $k \to \infty$. For $k \geq 4$, $\zeta(N_0) \approx 1/N_0$ to six significant digits. The hyperbolic metric and the Euclidean reciprocal become indistinguishable at the operating shell.

**Trilattice residue symmetry (§3.5, preserved).** Theorem 3.7: for every $d \mid N_0$ with $d > 1$, $719 \equiv -1 \pmod{d}$ and $721 \equiv +1 \pmod{d}$. The trilattice's residue structure is rigidly antisymmetric across the base shell; this uniformity supplies the architectural ground for the residue-fan reading of Part II / II.1.

**§9.4 classical connections — SL(2,$\mathbb{Z}$) and the hyperbolic plane (preserved):**

| Classical Object | Role in FVLT |
|---|---|
| **SL(2,$\mathbb{Z}$)** | Farey adjacency = modular group action. Trilattice fibers are local SL(2,$\mathbb{Z}$) orbits. |
| **Hyperbolic plane** | Farey tessellation tiles the upper half-plane [For38]. The $\alpha = 0$ collapse is a hyperbolic structure collapsing to its boundary. |
