## II.5 Vernier interference system

**Source:** §3.6, §3.7, §3.8, §4 preamble, §4.1, §4.2, §4.3 (v2.7.14); §9.4 Ford-circles item.  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.5.  
**Status:** unconditional / definitional / interpretive (per-block).

The Vernier instrument extends the trilattice to a full interference system: dual phasors over the basis primes, envelope–carrier decomposition, and the temporal gearbox that drives the phase relationship.

**Temporal gearbox (§3.6, preserved).** The Vernier ratio $V(N_0) = (N_0 + 1)/(N_0 - 1)$ governs the relative phase advance of the lower (Farey) and upper (factorial) tracks of the trilattice. At each shell step, the lower track advances by factor $(N_0 + 1)$ relative to the upper track's $(N_0 - 1)$; the gearbox is the operational form of this relative advance.

The trilattice gearbox $(N-1, N+1)$ is the canonical $t_1 = t_2 = 1$ reduction of a 2-parameter Vernier family $(N t_1 - t_2,\, N t_1 + t_2)$ exposed by FVLT Fourier instrumentation. Antipodal placement of $(N-1, N+1)$ around $N$ produces the integer-spin symmetry $\Im(z_1 + z_2) = 0$; divisibility information lives in the single-phasor imaginary part. See Algorithm Note ALG-1. The integer-spin symmetry above is the imaginary-part case of the full **Vernier Conjugate-Pair Lemma** (Lemma II.23.2, Part II / II.23): the gearbox pair $z_1, z_2$ are exact complex conjugates at every integer signal index whenever $t_1$ is integer.

At integer spin, the Vernier sum factorizes as $z_1 + z_2 = 2 N^\alpha (-1)^s \cos(p\pi/q)$ where $d = \gcd(s, N)$, $p = s/d$, $q = N/d$. This value lies in the real subfield of the cyclotomic field $\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{m_0})$, with $m_0 = 2q/\gcd(p, 2q)$ the Euler-residual order. The cyclotomic field is therefore the algebraic home of the integer-spin Vernier readout, and the gcd stratification of operating shells inherits a cyclotomic-tower structure. See Algorithm Note ALG-2.

The Vernier-sum identity above is a *fixed-$N$* identity at integer spin. Its continuous-$N$ counterpart is the angular winding of the single phasor along a continuous path $N_1 \to N_2$ between two integer divisor nodes $N_1 \mid N_2 \mid s$: $\Delta\theta = \pi\ell(r-1)$ with $r = N_2/N_1$ and $\ell = s/N_2$, recovering the divisor ratio $r$ as $(\Delta\theta/\pi)/\ell + 1$. The integer-$N$ slice gives identities; the continuous-$N$ integration gives ratios. Hasse covers follow from combining geometric ratio recovery with the combinatorial atomicity test on $r$ in $\mathrm{Div}(s)$. See Algorithm Note ALG-3.

**Convergence to calculus (§3.7, preserved).** In the limit $N_0 \to \infty$, the trilattice's discrete hyperbolic structure converges to standard differential-geometric machinery on the Poincaré upper half-plane. Discrete derivatives of Vernier phasors approach continuous derivatives; pinch-point structure approaches stationary-phase structure.

**Vernier degeneration (§3.8, preserved).** When the operating shell $N_0$ is replaced by a non-factorial integer, the Vernier system degenerates in specific ways — locality of degeneration: degeneration is shell-local, not framework-wide. The instrument remains usable; its calibrations require re-derivation.

<!-- id: DEF-4.1-DUAL-PHASORS | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: 3.5 -->
**Definition 4.1 (Dual Phasors, preserved).** For each basis prime $p_i \in B$, the dual phasor at signal index $s$ is
$$
\phi_{p_i}(s) \;=\; e^{2\pi i s / p_i}.
$$
The product $\Phi_B(s) = \prod_{p_i \in B} \phi_{p_i}(s)$ is the basis-product phasor; its conjugate at the complement primes gives the complete Vernier phasor pair.

**Envelope–carrier decomposition (§4.2, preserved).** The Vernier phasor pair decomposes any signal into an *envelope* (slow Vernier modulation indexed by $1/(N_0 - 1)$ and $1/(N_0 + 1)$) and a *carrier* (fast oscillation indexed by basis-prime frequencies). The decomposition is exact, not approximate — Vernier interference, not Fourier transformation.

**Pinch points (§4.3, preserved).** Pinch points are signal indices $s$ at which $\Phi_B(s) = 1$ — that is, every basis-prime phasor returns simultaneously to unity. Pinch points are the natural sample times of the Vernier instrument; they tile the integer line at intervals of $\mathrm{lcm}(B) = N_0$.

**§9.4 classical connection — Ford circles (preserved):**

| Classical Object | Role in FVLT |
|---|---|
| **Ford circles** | Circle of radius $1/(2q^2)$ at $p/q$; tangent iff Farey neighbors [For38]. Vernier phasors trace paths between tangent Ford circles. |

<!-- id: RMK-II.5a-ROSETTE-FACTORIAL-ATLAS-MAP | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: I.2, I.5.3, I.6, II.5, II.23.2, V.1, V.2 -->
**Remark II.5a (FVLT rosette as factorial atlas map, v3.1.22, new, interpretive).** The two-parameter gearbox family $(N t_1 - t_2,\, N t_1 + t_2)$ above, with its canonical $t_1 = t_2 = 1$ reduction $(N-1, N+1)$, generates a finite factorial-window instrument when the phasor pair is stacked over integer spin and factorial trail position. Call this stack the **FVLT rosette**.

Fix factorial setting $n_1$ and write $F = n_1!$. The rosette domain is the integer grid
$$
0 \le s \le F+1, \qquad 0 \le \varphi_3 \le F, \qquad s, \varphi_3 \in \mathbb{Z},
$$
with
$$
n_3 = F + 1 - \varphi_3, \qquad\text{so}\qquad n_3 \in \{F+1, F, \ldots, 1\}.
$$
The terminal value $n_3 = 0$ is not in the domain; it is the degenerate guarded case of the viewer implementation (no phasor point). For gearbox parameters $t_1, t_2, n_6, \alpha$,
$$
k_1 = n_3 t_1 - t_2, \qquad k_2 = n_3 t_1 + t_2,
$$
$$
R_\alpha(n_3) = \begin{cases} 0, & n_6 = 0,\\ n_6 n_3, & \alpha = 1,\\ n_6, & \alpha = 0,\\ n_6 / n_3, & \alpha = -1, \end{cases}
$$
$$
z_1(s, \varphi_3) = R_\alpha(n_3)\, \exp\!\left(i \frac{k_1 s \pi}{n_3}\right), \qquad z_2(s, \varphi_3) = R_\alpha(n_3)\, \exp\!\left(i \frac{k_2 s \pi}{n_3}\right).
$$
At integer $s$ with integer $t_1$, $z_2 = \overline{z_1}$ by the Vernier Conjugate-Pair Lemma (Lemma II.23.2; extended to real $n_3$ in Rmk 7.1). The rosette at $n_1$ is the $s \times \varphi_3$ stack
$$
\mathcal{R}_{n_1}(t_1, t_2, n_6, \alpha) = \{ z_1(s, \varphi_3),\, z_2(s, \varphi_3) : 0 \le s \le F+1,\ 0 \le \varphi_3 \le F \},
$$
in which $n_3 = n_1! + 1 - \varphi_3$ serves simultaneously as plotted radius, angular denominator, and divisor-contact candidate.

**Contact identity.** Assume $R_\alpha(n_3) \ne 0$ (the $n_6 = 0$ collapse to the origin is excluded). At fixed integer $s$, the $z_1$ phasor lies on the real axis precisely when $\sin(k_1 s \pi / n_3) = 0$, i.e.
$$
\operatorname{Im} z_1(s, \varphi_3) = 0 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; \frac{k_1 s}{n_3} \in \mathbb{Z}.
$$
This includes the negative real ray (the case $k_1 s / n_3$ an odd integer, angle $\pi$); "on the real axis" means $\pm$real, origin excluded. Since $k_1 = n_3 t_1 - t_2$,
$$
n_3 \mid k_1 s \;\Longleftrightarrow\; n_3 \mid t_2 s.
$$
So the general gearbox contact readout is not automatically the divisor set of $s$: it is the set of denominators $n_3$ dividing $t_2 s$ within the factorial window.

In the reference contact specialization $t_1 = t_2 = 1,\ n_6 = 1,\ \alpha = 1$, the condition specializes to $n_3 \mid s$, so the real-axis contact set in column $s$ is
$$
\mathcal{C}_{n_1}(s) = \{ n_3 \in \{1, \ldots, n_1! + 1\} : n_3 \mid s \},
$$
and for $1 \le s \le n_1! + 1$,
$$
\mathcal{C}_{n_1}(s) = \mathrm{Div}(s).
$$
Hence the column $s = N$ realizes the atlas atom $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ by contact in this restricted reference case, and the reference rosette realizes the finite atlas window
$$
\mathcal{A}_{\le n_1! + 1} = \{ \mathrm{Div}(s) : 1 \le s \le n_1! + 1 \}.
$$
The rosette is therefore not a visual overlay only. It is the Fourier / Vernier realization of a finite factorial-window atlas map: each column freezes one divisor atom, while the whole stack sweeps the divisor-contact readout across the window.

**Rim discipline.** The outer rim of the rosette at factorial setting $n_1$ is $n_1! + 1$, not $n_1!$. At $\varphi_3 = 0$, $n_3 = n_1! + 1$; at $\varphi_3 = n_1!$, $n_3 = 1$. Thus at $n_1 = 6$ the rosette is bounded by $721$. The worked $720$ column is the interior column $s = 720$, not the outer rim. This preserves the atlas-first distinction of Part I / I.2: $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ is a 720 factorial-shell instance, while the rosette at $n_1 = 6$ displays the factorial atlas window through $721$.

**Relation to the positive atlas.** The rosette supplies a viewer-side finite map into the positive atlas $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}} = \{\mathrm{Div}(n) : n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}\}$ (Part I / I.2). It does not redefine the substrate and does not replace the divisor lattice; it names an instrument already present in the Vernier viewer — the integer phasor stack whose column contacts recover divisor atoms. In this register: lattice atom $=$ frozen divisor object $\mathrm{Div}(N)$; rosette column $=$ instrument readout realizing $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ by contact; rosette stack $=$ finite factorial-window sweep $\mathcal{A}_{\le n_1! + 1}$; $720$ worked column $=$ distinguished interior column at $n_1 = 6$, not the rosette boundary.

**$S_n$-register firewall.** The rosette is a full factorial-shell atlas map in the indexing sense
$$
n_1 \longmapsto n_1! \longmapsto \{1, \ldots, n_1! + 1\} \longmapsto \{\mathrm{Div}(s) : 1 \le s \le n_1! + 1\}.
$$
This is a shell-indexed, instrument-theoretic use of $S_n$-language, not an automorphism-group claim. Group-action $S_n$ remains restricted to the existing primorial / squarefree register: Lemma I.5.3 gives $\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{Div}(N)) \cong S_m$ for squarefree $N = p_1 \cdots p_m$ acting by prime permutation, and already separates the $S_6$ realization on $\mathrm{Div}(30030)$ from the numerical coincidence $|S_6| = 720 = |\mathrm{Div}(720)|$ (integer-cardinality register, not poset-automorphism register). The rosette's factorial-shell indexing is a third register and must not import squarefree automorphism symmetry onto non-squarefree factorial atoms.

> The rosette is shell-indexed and instrument-theoretic; group-action $S_n$ claims remain restricted to the existing primorial / squarefree automorphism register.

**Scope.** This item adds no new divisor theorem; it names and scopes an existing viewer-side instrument. The only algebraic identity added is the elementary contact condition $n_3 \mid t_2 s$, with divisor recovery restricted to the reference $t_2 = 1$ specialization. No factoring advantage is claimed: the contact readout is a divisibility scan (evaluating $n_3 \mid s$ per column), not an algorithmic speedup over divisibility testing.

**Cross-reference (two collapse registers).** The contact identity's canonical specialization to $\mathrm{Div}(s)$ is one of the two collapse registers distinguished at Rmk II.23.2c; the other, Q-residual collapse, has exact locus $t_1 s \in \mathbb{Z}$ (Cor II.23.2.2).

<!-- id: RMK-II.5b-ROSETTE-FAREY-FORD | register: remark | status: unconditional (det/mediant/tangency); interpretive (cone-placement reading) | provenance: original | depends: II.5a, 9.4(Ford), 9.2(Stern-Brocot), 3.5, 3.6, 9.4b -->


**Remark II.5b (Farey/Ford Law of the FVLT Rosette, v3.1.23, new).**
For $N \geq 2$, the FVLT rosette of Rmk II.5a — the canonical $(N-1,\, N,\, N+1)$ triple — read in the reciprocal /
unit-fraction layer is a canonical Farey/Ford configuration. With $1/(N-1) > 1/N > 1/(N+1)$:

- **Mediant (unconditional).** $1/N$ is the reduced mediant of the flanks:
  $\tfrac{1+1}{(N-1)+(N+1)} = \tfrac{2}{2N} = \tfrac1N$ (Stern–Brocot, §9.2).
- **Adjacency (unconditional).** The center is Farey-adjacent to each immediate flank:
  $\left|\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\ N-1&N\end{smallmatrix}\right| = 1$,
  $\left|\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\ N&N+1\end{smallmatrix}\right| = 1$.
- **Ford tangency (unconditional).** $C_{1/N}$ (radius $1/2N^2$) is tangent to $C_{1/(N-1)}$ and
  $C_{1/(N+1)}$, since tangency $\iff$ determinant $1$ (§9.4 Ford circles, [For38]).

**Guard 1 (Chris — boundary).** The two flanks are *not* mutually adjacent:
$\left|\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\ N-1&N+1\end{smallmatrix}\right| = 2$, and the $\pm 2$ neighbors are
also determinant $2$ relative to the center. So the statement is "center adjacent to each immediate
flank," **not** "all three mutually adjacent." The break at $\pm 2$ is the boundary of
unimodularity, not a failure of the law.

**Guard 2 (Chris — definition).** Farey adjacency is the determinant condition $|ps - qr| = 1$, which
is **strictly stronger** than coprimality (e.g. $1/3, 1/5$ are reduced with coprime denominators yet
$\det = 2$). It collapses to *consecutive denominators* on the unit-fraction successor line, so
$\gcd(N, N\pm 1) = 1$ appears as the same local fact.

**Two-register seam.** This is the additive / reciprocal-register law of the rosette. It is
co-resident with but **not the same action** as the multiplicative cyclic-orbit family (Rmk 9.7y):
exponent-seat rotation is multiplicative/relabeling; Farey successor adjacency is additive/reciprocal.
(The SUCC/TIMES co-residence seam — co-residence only, no unification claimed.)

**Scope (guard).** This governs the rosette's *reciprocal-layer* structure, **not** the integer
factorization profiles of $N \pm 1$, which carry no law beyond $\gcd(N, N\pm 1) = 1$ / disjoint prime
support; the apparent $\pm 1$ profile pattern breaks at $\pm 2$.

**Checkable proposal (interpretive, not claimed).** If the §9.7 / cone radius law reduces to $1/q^2$
on the cursor fractions, "where the ghost $\pm 1$ triplet sits in the cone" is exactly this Ford
tangency. Kill set: tangent at $\pm 1$, broken at $\pm 2$; non-unit-fraction placements break it. Ties
to the Farey-Walker-1 / reciprocal-cone gate.

<!-- id: RMK-II.5c-QPLUS-EXPONENT-COMPLETION | register: remark | status: unconditional (group iso, reciprocal=negation, extended Ω-grade, balanced throat, 2/3–3/2 det/mediant, unit-fraction spine det=k); interpretive (unit-as-origin reading, extends 9.7x/9.3g); candidate (cell/tiling/space-group — NOT entered, OP pointer only, full text at bibliography OP 31) | provenance: original | depends: II.5b, 9.2(Stern-Brocot), 9.4(Ford/SL2Z), II.13(Farey-spine), 9.7x(unit-as-origin), 9.3g(unit-floor), I.3(signed-completion-DISAMBIG), 1.2a(universal-lattice), 9.7y(seat-rotation-seam-DISAMBIG) -->


**Remark II.5c (ℚ⁺ Exponent Completion and the Balanced Throat, v3.2.2, new).**
The reciprocal/unit-fraction layer of Rmk II.5b extends to the full positive-rational multiplicative
group. Write $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Q}^+} = \mathbb{Q}^+ \cong \bigoplus_p \mathbb{Z}$, the free abelian
group on the primes, with each $x = \prod_p p^{e_p}$ ($e_p \in \mathbb{Z}$, finite support).
$\mathrm{Div}(N)$ is the bounded nonnegative box $0 \le e_p \le v_p(N)$; the **exponent completion**
lifts the box to the integer-exponent lattice. This is a **category change** — bounded divisor poset
$\to$ integer-exponent free abelian group — not a new instrument on $\mathrm{Div}(N)$.

- **Group / involution (unconditional).** The reciprocal involution $x \mapsto x^{-1}$ is exactly
  exponent-vector negation $(e_p)_p \mapsto (-e_p)_p$.
- **Extended $\Omega$-grade (unconditional).** $\Omega$ extends to the grading homomorphism
  $\Omega(p/q) = \Omega(p) - \Omega(q) = \sum_p e_p \in \mathbb{Z}$ (so $\Omega(x^{-1}) = -\Omega(x)$).
- **Balanced throat (unconditional).** The grade-zero subgroup $B = \ker\Omega = \{\prod_p p^{e_p} :
  \sum_p e_p = 0\}$. Negation sends the slice $\Omega = c$ to $\Omega = -c$, so $\Omega = 0$ is the
  **unique grade-slice fixed setwise** by the involution — the slice on which it acts internally.
  Inside $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ the $\Omega = 0$ layer is only the unit; in $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Q}^+}$ it
  opens into a full balanced lattice.

**Minimal witness (unconditional).** The first non-axis, non-unit balanced points are $2/3 = 2^1
3^{-1}$ and $3/2 = 2^{-1} 3^1$, grades $\Omega(2/3) = \Omega(3/2) = 0$, exponent coordinates
$(1,-1) \leftrightarrow (-1,1)$ swapped by negation. They are the smallest place where numerator and
denominator both carry prime structure, so reciprocal becomes a genuine numerator–denominator
exchange rather than an axis flip. On the Farey face, $2/3 = \tfrac{1+1}{2+1}$ is the mediant of
$1/2, 1/1$ and $3/2 = \tfrac{1+2}{1+1}$ the mediant of $1/1, 2/1$. All four adjacency checks are
det-1: for $1/2 \leftrightarrow 2/3 \leftrightarrow 1/1$, $\left|1\cdot 3 - 2\cdot 2\right| = 1$ and
$\left|2\cdot 1 - 3\cdot 1\right| = 1$; for $1/1 \leftrightarrow 3/2 \leftrightarrow 2/1$,
$\left|1\cdot 2 - 1\cdot 3\right| = 1$ and $\left|3\cdot 1 - 2\cdot 2\right| = 1$. First interior
Stern–Brocot witnesses flanking unity.

**Unit-fraction spine law (unconditional; generalizes II.5b Guard 1).** For $1/(N-k)$ and $1/N$ with
$N > k \ge 1$, the Farey determinant is $|1\cdot N - (N-k)\cdot 1| = k$, so they are Farey-adjacent
**iff $k = 1$**; for $k > 1$ they are separated along the unit-fraction spine by the det-1 chain
$1/N \leftrightarrow 1/(N-1) \leftrightarrow \cdots \leftrightarrow 1/(N-k)$. Thus **$k$ is Farey
determinant separation along the unit-fraction spine, not Stern–Brocot generation depth.** The
endpoint mediant is $\operatorname{med}(1/(N-k), 1/N) = \tfrac{2}{2N-k}$: a unit fraction
$\tfrac{1}{N-k/2}$ iff $k$ is even (the $k=2$ case $\tfrac{2}{2N-2} = \tfrac{1}{N-1}$ is II.5b's
$\pm 2$ coincidence), not a unit fraction for odd $k$. The $\pm 2$ Vernier triple $(N-2, N, N+2)$
with $N > 2$ therefore reads in the reciprocal layer as det-2 (not adjacent), factoring through the
$\pm 1$ neighbors — a confirming test of II.5b's "break at $\pm 2$ is the boundary of unimodularity,"
not a new law.

**Bridge (unconditional, global).** Reciprocal inversion on $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Q}^+}$ and the
projective Farey reflection $x \mapsto 1/x$ are the *same* involution on all of $\mathbb{Q}^+$. The
balanced throat $B$ is the grade-slice where that global involution acts internally.

**Guard (G-seam — does not collapse II.5b).** The involution unified here is reciprocal $=$
exponent-negation $=$ projective reflection $x \mapsto 1/x$ (group inversion). It is **not** the
SUCC/TIMES pairing of II.5b — multiplicative seat-rotation (Rmk 9.7y) with additive Farey-successor
adjacency — which remains co-resident-only, no unification claimed. Reflection $\neq$ successor; group
inversion $\neq$ seat rotation. The II.5b two-register seam is untouched.

**Guard (G-signed — disambiguation from I.3).** Where this remark says "signed" (exponent sign
$e_p \in \mathbb{Z}$), it means the multiplicative group completion $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Q}^+}$. This
is **not** the I.3 *signed completion* $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times} = \{\mathrm{Div}^{\pm}(n)\}$,
which signs the *orientation/chirality* $\mathbb{Z}/2$ ($\theta \mapsto -\theta$), a lattice-congruent
two-sheeting of the **same** point set. The two completions of $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$ sign
orthogonal axes (exponent range vs. orientation) and must not be conflated.

**Guard (G-register — disambiguation from 9.3g).** Going below $1$ here is the **multiplicative**
reciprocal exit (negative prime exponents, the reciprocal cone / Farey-Walker-1 gate of II.5b). It is
**not** the Rmk 9.3g "slope register" exit, which is the **additive / difference-vs-slope** boundary
(factorial denominators, Rmk 2.2a.1). Both are "fractions below the unit" but in orthogonal registers.

**Interpretive (unit as origin; extends 9.7x, 9.3g).** Note 9.7x reads the unit as the $\bot$ of the
*bounded* graded lattice (absence-given-a-position); Rmk 9.3g names it the *floor* of multiplicative
descent. Inside the exponent completion, the unit is no longer functioning merely as $\bot$ or floor;
it becomes the **fixed origin** of the balanced lattice — the descent does not terminate at $1$, it
passes through $1$ into the negative-exponent half. The unit is not destroyed; it is reinterpreted
from boundary to center. (Democritean register: the occupied origin remains occupied; it ceases to be
the lowest inhabited site.)

**Open-problem pointer.** The exponent-completion object suggests a further cell/tiling/space-group
extension, but that extension is not part of this remark. It is referred to the bibliography for
Open-Problem registration (OP 31), conditional on specifying (1) the translation lattice, (2) the
embedding in which rotations/reflections act, and (3) the compatible point-group action.

**Scope (guard).** This governs the reciprocal/unit-fraction layer extended to
$\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Q}^+}$ and the throat involution. It makes no claim about the integer
factorization profiles of $N \pm k$ (II.5b scope guard stands), and asserts no coupling of the
exponent completion to the I.3 orientation completion or to $\chi/w$ (post-v3.0 scope, cf. II.6 /
Rmk 9.7z).
---

<!-- id: RMK-II.5d-FAREY-FACTORIAL-RESOLUTION | register: remark | status: unconditional (denominator containment, lcm host, shell bound — i-iii) + interpretive (register readout, cousinhood pointer — iv-v) | provenance: original | depends: 2.2a, 2.2d, 9.1a, II.5b, 1.2a, 9.6, II.23.5, II.5c, 2.2b.1 -->
**Remark II.5d (Farey Order and the Factorial Tower, v3.2.4, new).**

**(i) Denominator containment (unconditional).** For every integer $k \geq 1$, the denominator set of the Farey sequence $F_k$ is exactly $\{1, 2, \ldots, k\}$. Conversely, every such denominator occurs: for each $1 \leq q \leq k$, the reduced fraction $1/q$ lies in $F_k$. Every member divides $k!$:

$$q \leq k \implies q \mid k!$$

since $q$ occurs as a factor in $k! = 1 \cdot 2 \cdots k$. Hence the full denominator support of $F_k$ is resident in $\mathrm{Div}(k!)$, the $k$-th rung of the factorial tower. The nested classical filtration $F_1 \subset F_2 \subset \cdots$ is thereby majorized, rung for rung, by the factorial filtration $N_k = k!$ of Rmk 2.2a.

**(ii) Minimality guard (unconditional).** $\mathrm{Div}(k!)$ is a host, not the minimal host. The minimal containing lattice for the $F_k$ denominator set is $\mathrm{Div}(\mathrm{lcm}(1, \ldots, k))$ (cf. Rmk 1.2a), which for $k \geq 4$ is already a proper divisor-sublattice of $\mathrm{Div}(k!)$ ($k \leq 3$ are equality cases). Witness: $k = 5$ gives $\mathrm{lcm}(1,\ldots,5) = 60$ against $5! = 120$. The factorial shell is the canonical tower rung; the lcm lattice is the tight fit.

**(iii) Shell bound (unconditional).** For every $q \geq 1$, $\mathrm{shell}(q) \leq q$, since $q \mid q!$. Equality holds for every prime $p$ (a prime first appears as a factor at $p!$), though not only for primes — this is not an iff: $\mathrm{shell}(4) = 4$. Strict inequality occurs once the needed prime factors are supplied earlier by the factorial product; witnesses include $\mathrm{shell}(6) = 3$, $\mathrm{shell}(8) = 4$, and $\mathrm{shell}(9) = 6$. The shell function of Def 2.2d is thus the kernel-native answer to the resolution question: it assigns each denominator its minimal factorial rung.

**(iv) Register readout (interpretive).** The classical Farey order $k$ is a genuine resolution parameter: the levels are totally ordered, nested, and exhaust $\mathbb{Q} \cap [0,1]$ in the limit. Clause (i) shows this resolution register is carried inside the kernel by the factorial tower, stepped at rungs $k!$ rather than unit increments. This names the *legitimate* resolution knob so that the illegitimate one stays fenced: the helix index $n$ of §9.1a remains the order of the cyclic group being unfolded — partially ordered by divisibility, non-nested (Lemma 9.3a: disjoint cylinders), with no kernel-native resolution limit object — and is **not** a resolution parameter. Same integers, two registers; the firewall of §9.1a is the boundary between them.

**(v) Cousinhood pointer (interpretive, cf. §9.6).** This same Farey-resolution register is the discrete counterpart of the Mandelbrot/Arnold-tongue cousinhood discussed in §9.6 and II.23.5; the present remark records only the factorial-tower seating of the denominator filtration.

---

**Guards.**

**G-support (containment is of denominators, not fractions).** The embedding in (i) maps denominator supports into a divisor lattice: $q \mapsto q \in \mathrm{Div}(k!)$. It does not transport Farey structure onto Hasse structure. Farey adjacency is det-1 unimodularity (Rmk II.5b); Hasse adjacency is a single-prime cover. These are distinct relations on overlapping index sets, and no claim is made that Farey neighbors land Hasse-adjacent. (They generally do not: $1/2$ and $1/3$ are Farey-adjacent in $F_3$, while $2$ and $3$ sit at lattice distance $2$, Cor 2.2b.1.)

**G-register (distinct from the reciprocal layer).** The ℚ⁺ exponent completion (Rmk II.5c) hosts the *fractions themselves* as integer-exponent vectors, with the unit-fraction spine and Farey-separation law. Clause (i) is the coarse shadow of that layer: it tracks only denominator support inside a bounded positive lattice. The two must not be conflated — one is a completion of the substrate, the other a containment statement about a tower rung.

**G-knob (what is and is not claimed).** No new arithmetic is claimed; (i)–(iii) are one-line classical facts. The content of the remark is register placement: which kernel object legitimately carries the resolution reading, and which is fenced against it.

---

<!-- id: RMK-II.5e-TWO-GRADINGS-TWO-THROATS | register: remark | status: unconditional/adapted (D1-D3) + interpretive (D4) | provenance: adapted (classical grade maps, product formula) over original (two-throat seating, compress/stretch reading) | depends: II.5c, 7.1, II.23.7 -->
**Remark II.5e (Two gradings, two throats, and the product formula, v3.2.4, new).**

**(D1) Two gradings (unconditional).** On $\mathbb{Q}^+$ two natural grading homomorphisms into $(\mathbb{R}, +)$ share one valuation vector and differ only in weights:

$$\Omega(x) = \sum_p v_p(x) \qquad \text{(arithmetic grade; every prime weighs 1; integer-valued)}$$
$$L(x) = \ln x = \sum_p v_p(x)\ln p \qquad \text{(Archimedean grade; prime } p \text{ weighs } \ln p\text{; dense image)}$$

$\Omega$ is the extended grade of the v3.2.2 block — **the arithmetic grade; it becomes a rank only after a bounded orientation/register is declared** (e.g. within a fixed $\mathrm{Div}(N)$, where Lemma 9.3h's down-sets carry it; per audit). $L$ is the display height of the log-mirror rendering.

**(D2) Two throats (unconditional; guard).** $\ker \Omega = B$, the balanced throat of Rmk II.5c — **the balanced exponent subgroup** (additive $\mathbb{Z}$-lattice of exponent vectors summing to zero) containing the minimal witnesses $2/3, 3/2$. **Guard (per audit): $\ker\Omega$ is a subgroup, not a meet/join sublattice of the residence order** — exponent-wise min of $(1,-1)$ and $(-1,1)$ is $(-1,-1) \leftrightarrow 1/6$ with $\Omega(1/6) = -2$, outside the throat; the throat is not closed under the residence meets and joins. $\ker L = \{1\}$ — trivial: the geometric waist of the log-mirror hourglass is a single point. **Witness forcing the distinction:** $2/3$ sits *at* the $\Omega$-throat ($\Omega = 1 - 1 = 0$) but *below* the $L$-waist (log-height $1 + \ln(2/3) \approx 0.5945$). An instrument with height $= \Omega$ (the "where it sits in the cone" convention) places $2/3$ on the waist plane; the log-mirror places it beneath the tip. Both are correct; they are different throats. **G-throat:** the balanced throat ($\Omega = 0$, arithmetic) and the geometric waist ($L = 0$ about the unit, Archimedean) must not be conflated; every "throat" claim must declare its grading.

**(D3) Product formula as the bridge (unconditional; `provenance: adapted`).** For $x \in \mathbb{Q}^+$, with $|x|_p = p^{-v_p(x)}$ and $|x|_\infty = x$:

$$|x|_\infty \cdot \prod_p |x|_p = 1 \iff \ln x = \sum_p v_p(x) \ln p,$$

i.e. the Archimedean grade is exactly the log-$p$-weighted sum of the arithmetic valuations — the infinite place determined by the finite places. This is the compress/stretch cancellation observed in the log-mirror: the map's local magnification is $1/x$, so mirror pairs $x, 1/x$ carry reciprocal magnifications with product $1$, and the throat is the locus of unit magnification. Classical content: the product formula on $\mathbb{Q}$ (Artin–Whaples lineage); the FVLT contribution is the seating — $\Omega$ as arithmetic grade, $L$ as log-mirror height, the product formula as the relation between them, the two-throat guard as the firewall. **Instrument evidence (deployed):** the "where it sits in the cone" viewer's readout line $|d|_\infty \cdot \prod |d|_p = 1$ displays this identity instrument-side; this remark supplies the kernel seat.

**(D4) Interpretive cross-references.** (a) The log-mirror height is the Weyl-1918 scale potential $G = \log\lambda$ of Rmk 7.1: the viewer is a $G$-coordinate display on $\mathbb{Q}^+$, and the log-compressed adaptive shell of Rmk II.23.7 uses the same compression for the same reason (multiplicative range, additive display budget). (b) The log-mirror renders the multiplicative Haar measure $dx/x$ uniformly — the unique measure (up to scale) invariant under both scaling and the reciprocal involution; equal ratios read as equal distances, so the display shows the signal in the geometry where multiplication is homogeneous. (c) The pair $(\Omega, L)$ are the arithmetic and Archimedean logarithms of one object; their disagreement pattern (D2 witness) is not an artifact but the two-places structure of $\mathbb{Q}$ made visible.

**Guards.** *G-throat* (D2, above). *G-classical:* D3 is classical; `adapted`, with the seating named as the contribution. *G-adelic-scope:* no adele-ring machinery is imported — the statement is confined to $\mathbb{Q}^+$ and its two grade maps; the full places-of-ℚ formalism remains instrument-side until separately seated.

<!-- id: RMK-II.5f-STAIRCASE-FORD-CHAINS | register: remark | status: (i)-(iv) unconditional / (v) unconditional + interpretive | provenance: original (seating; classical Ford geometry) | depends: II.5b, II.5c, II.5a, 9.7f | locked: Robin 2026-07-14 v1.0 (item); v3.2.5 release LOCKED by Robin 2026-07-14 -->
**Remark II.5f (Staircase Profiles as Connected Ford Chains, v3.2.5).**

**Setup.** Let $N = \prod_{i=1}^{r} p_i^{a_i}$ with exponent **value set** $V = \{a_1, \dots, a_r\} \subset \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 1}$ (repeated exponent values map to the same spine point and contribute one circle). Place the profile on the unit-fraction spine as classical Ford circles: for each $a \in V$, the circle $F(1/a)$ tangent to the real axis at $1/a$ with radius $1/(2a^2)$. Ford tangency (classical; the denominator specialization of II.5b's determinant law): $F(x/y)$ and $F(x'/y')$ are tangent iff $|xy' - x'y| = 1$, disjoint otherwise. On unit fractions this is the II.5c spine law $\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1 & 1\\ a & b\end{smallmatrix}\right) = |b - a| = k$.

**(i) [unconditional] Chain criterion.** The tangency graph of $\{F(1/a) : a \in V\}$ is the path graph on $V$ in value order, with an edge exactly between consecutive integers both present in $V$. The Ford system is connected **iff $V$ is an interval of consecutive integers**, and additionally anchored at the unit circle $F(1/1)$ iff $\min V = 1$. For gap-free staircase profiles $V = \{1, 2, \dots, n\}$ (any assignment to primes), the profile's Ford spine is a single connected tangency chain.

**(ii) [unconditional] Label-blindness.** The tangency graph depends only on the exponent value set $V$, never on which prime carries which exponent — a value-set/coordinate fact, not a label fact, expressed under the Rmk II.5a register firewall and the Note C3e-II.1 / Rmk 9.7f profile-versus-prime-label guard. No group action on the profile is invoked.

**(iii) [unconditional] Gap width and mediant repair.** If $a, a+k \in V$ with no intermediate values present, the II.5c law gives Farey determinant $k$ between the flanking circles: $\boxed{\text{exponent-gap width } k = \text{Ford determinant separation } k.}$ Complete tangency repair inserts the intermediate unit fractions $\tfrac{1}{a+1}, \ldots, \tfrac{1}{a+k-1}$. In the minimal break $k = 2$, the endpoint mediant is exactly the unique missing exponent, $\operatorname{med}(\tfrac{1}{a}, \tfrac{1}{a+2}) = \tfrac{2}{2a+2} = \tfrac{1}{a+1}$, i.e. $\boxed{\text{missing exponent} = \text{missing mediant denominator}.}$ For $k > 2$ the endpoint mediant $\tfrac{2}{2a+k}$ is a unit fraction iff $k$ is even, and even then identifies only the midpoint — not the full missing set. The mediant identity is exact only at $k = 2$.

**(iv) [worked fixture: $N_0 = 720$].** Profile $(4,2,1)$, $V = \{1,2,4\}$. Chain $F(1/1)$–$F(1/2)$ tangent ($k=1$); break $F(1/2)$–$F(1/4)$, $\det = 2$, disjoint — the minimal break, so the mediant identity applies exactly. Repair $\operatorname{med}(\tfrac12, \tfrac14) = \tfrac26 = \tfrac13$ — the missing exponent $3$; restored chain $\tfrac12 \leftrightarrow \tfrac13 \leftrightarrow \tfrac14$ is a pair of determinant-one tangencies. Profile repair $(4,2,1) \to (4,3,2,1)$ requires one new prime slot: canonical first-prime realization $2^4 3^3 5^2 7 = 75600$. Companion figure: `fixture_720_ford_chain.svg`.

**(v) [unconditional + interpretive] Strict-staircase subclass and canonical representative.** [Unconditional:] The Ford spine is connected and unit-anchored iff $V = \{1, \ldots, n\}$, irrespective of repetitions or assignment to prime labels (e.g. profile $(3,2,1,1)$ has the same connected chain as $(3,2,1)$). The **strict staircase** condition additionally requires each value $1, \ldots, n$ to occur exactly once; for a fixed $n$-prime support $q_1 < \cdots < q_n$ its canonical decreasing assignment is $N_{\mathrm{stair}}(q_1, \ldots, q_n) = \prod_{i=1}^{n} q_i^{\,n-i+1} = \prod_{j=1}^{n} (q_1 \cdots q_j)$; at the first $n$ primes this is $\prod_{j=1}^{n} p_j\# = 2, 12, 360, 75600, \ldots$ Every permutation of the exponent assignment has the same label-blind Ford chain but is generally a different integer, and is not asserted to arise from a divisor-lattice automorphism. [Interpretive fixture:] the minimal strict-staircase repair of the $720$ profile $(4,2,1)$ is $2^4 3^3 5^2 7 = 75600$; the observation $720 = 2 \cdot 360$ remains a rhyme only (cf. the doubled-$(4,2,1)$ example of Rmk 9.7y — no structural claim).

**Guards / non-claims.** *G1:* scope is the classical Ford spine (one register); the generalized-packing phenomenon where determinant-one pairs can fail tangency without register alternation (arXiv:2510.21702v2 octahedral/triangular two-orbit parametrizations) is out of scope, *candidate pending independent verification*, recorded only as the boundary not crossed. *G2:* the coincidence of octahedral inner-product coefficients $(4,2,1)$ with 720's multiplicity profile is a label coincidence (they arise as $2^2,2^1,2^0$ from register curvature ratios), explicitly not structure. *G3:* no claim about Ford circles at non-unit fractions, nor any neighbor-profile law beyond II.5b. *G4:* connectivity requires an interval, not specifically $\{1,\dots,n\}$ (e.g. $V=\{2,3,4\}$ chains but is not unit-anchored); "staircase" is the anchored case $\min V = 1$, "strict staircase" additionally requires each value once. *G5:* connected-chain class $\supsetneq$ staircase class $\supsetneq$ strict-staircase class; the primorial-product formula names the canonical representative of the strict-staircase class for a declared support, not a characterization of the connected-chain class. **Companions:** `fixture_720_ford_chain.svg` (SHA-256 `b08fefc65850ce2210733e9ac118fce0bd1231e8f42bbc09d216dfeb6066dd3b`), `verify_rmk_II5f.py` (SHA-256 `04fdde45baa14559814d07ebb061c0ff2265682b85f05f0aaf9f55cfc9b69cb8`, last run ALL PASS: 3,081 exact unit-fraction pairs, 2,000 randomized profiles, 720 fixture, strict-staircase canonical identity). Item locked v1.0 (Robin, 2026-07-14); cold-passed body SHA-256 `3e034a6e4b96d24e428aa9977fbec355763990d41d863387eb0747bdbdbb7360`. Full lock/verification record: `fableous/locked/rmk_II5f_locked_v1_0.md`.

## II.6 Phasor collapse, clinamen, and unitarity

**Source:** §4.4, §4.5, §4.6, §4.7a (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.6.  
**Status:** unconditional / interpretive (per-block).

The Vernier system carries three load-bearing structures: parity / chirality, the clinamen, and unitarity.

**Parity and chirality (§4.4, preserved).** The basis-prime phasor $\phi_p(s)$ has an associated parity $\epsilon_p \in \{+, -\}$ governing whether the phasor advances clockwise or counterclockwise in the complex plane. The choice of parity is the *chirality* of the instrument. Chirality and signed completion of the atlas (Part I / I.3) are distinct $\mathbb{Z}/2$ operations declared independent in v3.0; their coupling is post-v3.0 scope.

<!-- id: DEF-4.5-CLINAMEN | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: 4.1 -->
**Definition 4.5 (The Clinamen — Discrete Derivative, preserved).** The clinamen at index $s$ is the discrete derivative of the Vernier phasor pair:
$$
\mathrm{Cl}(s) \;=\; \Phi_B(s+1) \cdot \overline{\Phi_B(s)} - 1.
$$
$\mathrm{Cl}(s)$ measures the discrete phase deviation accumulated in one signal step. At pinch points, $\mathrm{Cl}(s) = 0$; between pinch points, $\mathrm{Cl}(s)$ traces the local geometry of the phasor curve.

The clinamen reading is the framework's load-bearing connection to Lucretian *clinamen* — the minimal deviation that introduces structure into otherwise determined motion. In FVLT, the clinamen is not metaphor; it is the discrete derivative through which the system accumulates trajectory away from periodic uniformity.

<!-- id: THM-4.6-VERNIER-UNITARITY | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 4.1, 4.5, DEF-ENERGY-FULL -->
**Theorem 4.6 (Vernier Unitarity — Pythagorean Identity, v3.2.0 scoped).** The active Pythagorean statement is the full exact-conductor Plancherel identity: for $x \in H_{\mathrm{harm}}(N_0)$,
$$
\sum_{s=0}^{N_0 - 1} |x(s)|^2 \;=\; \sum_{q \mid N_0} E_q^{\mathrm{full}}(x).
$$
This is a statement about the orthogonal conductor projectors $P_q$, not about the scalar line statistic $E_q^{\mathrm{line}}$ for a general signal.

*Proof sketch (v3.2.0 scoped).* The Fourier characters split $H_{\mathrm{harm}}(N_0)$ into mutually orthogonal exact-conductor subspaces $V_q$. Orthogonality and completeness of the projectors $P_q$ give the Pythagorean identity. The scalar lines spanned by $c_q$ do not span the full primitive eigenspaces in general and therefore do not satisfy this identity for arbitrary signals. $\square$

**Selective phasor collapse (§4.7a, preserved).** When the instrument operates under a *lens contraction* (Part IV / IV.1) — replacing $N_t = N_0$ with a smaller $N_t^* \mid N_0$ — the phasor space collapses selectively: pinch points spaced at $\mathrm{lcm}(B \cap \pi(N_t^*))$ replace those at $N_0$; Vernier interference is reorganized at the operative scale. The collapse is selective in the sense that prime factors absent from $N_t^*$ are not measured but are not removed — they remain available under lens re-expansion.

---

## II.7 FVLT transform proper

**Source:** §5 preamble, §5.1, Def 5.1 (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.7.  
**Status:** definitional / unconditional.

The FVLT transform is the core instrument: a forward map from signal space to a graded divisor-indexed energy space that partitions, reconstructs, and prioritizes structure.

<!-- id: DEF-5.1-AMPLITUDE-EXPONENT | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: 1.2, 5.2 -->
**Definition 5.1 (Amplitude Exponent and Energy Split, v3.2.0 corrected).** For a signal $x: \mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{C}$ and a tilt parameter $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$, the scalar line statistic and full conductor energy are

<!-- id: DEF-ENERGY-LINE | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: 5.1 -->
<!-- id: DEF-ENERGY-FULL | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: adapted | depends: 5.1, H_harm(N) -->
$$
E_q^{\mathrm{line}}(x) = \frac{|\langle x,c_q\rangle|^2}{\|c_q\|^2},
\qquad
E_q^{\mathrm{full}}(x) = \|P_q x\|^2,
$$
where $P_q$ is the orthogonal projector onto the exact-conductor subspace $V_q \subset H_{\mathrm{harm}}(N)$. Tilted forms are
$$
E_q^{\mathrm{line},\alpha}(x) = \varphi(q)^{-\alpha} E_q^{\mathrm{line}}(x),
\qquad
E_q^{\mathrm{full},\alpha}(x) = \varphi(q)^{-\alpha} E_q^{\mathrm{full}}(x).
$$

The tilt parameter $\alpha$ controls the relative emphasis on small-$q$ versus large-$q$ channels:
- $\alpha = 0$: uniform weighting; Plancherel-faithful for $E_q^{\mathrm{full}}$.
- $\alpha > 0$: emphasizes small-$q$ (coarse, low-multiplicity divisors); the walk concentrates near the unit.
- $\alpha < 0$: emphasizes large-$q$ (fine, high-multiplicity divisors); the walk concentrates near the apex.

The amplitude exponent is the parameter through which the instrument's bias is tuned. At $\alpha = 0$, the full conductor transform is energy-faithful; at $\alpha \neq 0$, the transform is *steered* toward a chosen multiplicity scale.

The forward FVLT transform may produce either divisor-indexed vector $(E_q^{\mathrm{line},\alpha}(x))_{q \mid N}$ or $(E_q^{\mathrm{full},\alpha}(x))_{q \mid N}$, depending on the declared measurement object. Partition and Parseval claims use the full conductor vector; coherent scalar diagnostics use the line vector. Reconstruction requires phase-retaining coefficients, not energy-only output (Part II / II.9).

---

## II.8 Weighted Ramanujan sums and supercharacters

**Source:** §5.2, §5.5 body (v2.7.14); §8.3 Rmk 8.5c–e scanner-mechanics half.  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.8.  
**Status:** unconditional / interpretive (per-block).

The harmonic instrument has structured weighting beyond unweighted Ramanujan sums.

<!-- id: DEF-5.2-WEIGHTED-RAMANUJAN-SUMS | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: 5.1 -->
**Definition 5.2 (Weighted Ramanujan Sums, preserved).** The weighted Ramanujan sum at divisor $q$ with weight $w: \mathrm{Div}(q) \to \mathbb{C}$ is
$$
c_q^w(s) \;=\; \sum_{d \mid q} w(d) \, \mu(q/d) \, e^{2\pi i s d / q},
$$
the FGK / supercharacter-family generalization of the classical Ramanujan sum $c_q(s) = c_q^{\mathbf{1}}(s)$.

Weighted Ramanujan sums form a complete basis for $\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z}$ under any non-degenerate weight $w$; the unweighted case ($w \equiv 1$) is the classical Ramanujan sum.

**§5.5 Ramanujan Resonance body (preserved).** The Ramanujan resonance result — that for squarefree $q$, energy in $c_q$ admits the closed form of Rmk 5.7a (Part II / II.2) — extends to weighted cases under canonical weight choices. The weighted case introduces additional radical-invariance properties; the closed-form concentration ratio remains derivable.

**Sliding energy scanner (§8.3 Rmk 8.5c-e, v3.2.0 notation corrected).** The sliding scanner instrument moves a fixed declared measurement, either $E_q^{\mathrm{line}}$ or $E_q^{\mathrm{full}}$, across a sequence of signals or signal-window translations. A windowed measurement must declare its support $W \subset \mathbb{Z}$, whether indexing is absolute or reanchored to the window origin, its normalization, and its taper policy. The scanner mechanics depend on the DFT resolution constraint and the shell selection rule, both of which live at the atlas regime level (Part III / III.12, III.13).

---

## II.9 Decomposition, reconstruction, and diagnostic priority

**Source:** §5.3, §5.4 (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.9.  
**Status:** definitional / interpretive (per-block).

The FVLT transform supports three operations on the divisor-indexed energy vector: decomposition, reconstruction, and diagnostic priority. These operations are the instrument's *discipline* — what it does with what it measures.

**Decomposition (§5.3, v3.2.0 scoped).** The full energy vector $(E_q^{\mathrm{full},\alpha}(x))_{q \mid N}$ decomposes the signal's energy across exact-conductor subspaces indexed by divisors. The decomposition is exact for $E_q^{\mathrm{full}}$ at $\alpha = 0$ (Plancherel-type); under tilt $\alpha \neq 0$, it is weighted. The line vector $(E_q^{\mathrm{line},\alpha}(x))_{q \mid N}$ is a coherent scalar statistic and is not a general Parseval decomposition.

**Reconstruction (§5.3, v3.2.0 scoped).** Reconstruction is permitted only when phase-retaining coefficient data are retained. Energy-only readouts, including both $E_q^{\mathrm{line}}$ and $E_q^{\mathrm{full}}$, lose phase and do not reconstruct a general signal. The FVLT instrument distinguishes complex-valued channel coefficients and primitive-mode Fourier coefficients from their magnitude-squared energies; reconstruction operates on the former, channel-energy diagnostics on the latter.

**Declared-shell restriction (§5.3, v3.2.0 scoped).** Signals of length or modulus $N$ are decomposed against divisor labels $q \mid N$. In the local $N_0 = 720$ worked shell, $H_{\mathrm{harm}}(720)$ has $720$ dimensions and its conductor labels are the $30$ divisors of $720$; the Hasse atom $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ has $30$ nodes. Operations that exceed the declared shell (e.g., reading channels indexed by primes not in $\pi(N)$) require lens expansion (Part IV / IV.1).

**Diagnostic priority (§5.3, preserved).** The instrument's diagnostic priority orders channel-energy readouts by structural significance, not by magnitude. Specifically: complement-energy z-score $\mathrm{ce}_z$ (Part V / V.8), bridge depth $\beta(x)$ (Part V / V.8), and null-model calibrated p-value $p_{\mathrm{null}}$ (Part V / V.17) carry diagnostic priority over raw channel magnitudes. The instrument refuses claims based on raw magnitudes alone; structured-diagnostic-priority is the discipline that makes the FVLT a calibrated instrument (Part VI / VI.1).

<!-- id: SECTION-5.4-GEOMETRY-VS-MEASUREMENT | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 5.1, 5.3 -->
**§5.4 Geometry vs. Measurement (preserved).** Two distinct registers operate inside the FVLT transform. The *geometry register* — the helix-readout and the multi-prime polygonal embedding (Part II / II.13, II.14) — describes where divisors sit in space. The *measurement register* — the supercharacter / Ramanujan channel decomposition — describes how much energy each divisor channel carries. The two registers share a common indexing set ($\mathrm{Div}(N)$, Part II / II.3) but answer different questions: geometry asks "where," measurement asks "how much."

Conflation produces register-confusion errors: a geometric fact (such as which divisors are bridges in Part V / V.7) is not a measurement claim, and a measurement fact (such as which channels carry energy) is not a geometric arrangement. The transform discipline keeps the two registers separate while allowing them to cross-reference each other at the divisor-as-shared-channel level (Rmk 1.5c, Part II / II.3). The measurement register is operationalized in Part II / II.23 (Measurement Instrument), which names the readouts (spine adherence γ, $H_T$, Harmonic Contrast, time arrow, μ-class assignment), the gearbox formalism (Lemma II.23.2), the bracket taxonomy (II.23.3) with its Chebyshev-cell reading (Rmk II.23.4), and the L/D signature protocol (II.23.6).

---

## II.10 Persistent lattice energy state

**Source:** Def 5.1a (v2.7.14); Rmk 9.4a (v2.7.13); Note 9.4b (v2.7.13).  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.10.  
**Status:** definitional / empirical / interpretive (per-block).

The instrument carries persistent state on $\mathrm{Div}(N)$: a lattice-indexed energy distribution that evolves under repeated measurement.

<!-- id: DEF-5.1a-PERSISTENT-STATE | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: 1.2, 5.1 -->
**Definition 5.1a (Persistent State on the Divisor Lattice, preserved).** The state $\pi_t \in \mathbb{R}^{|\mathrm{Div}(N)|}_{\geq 0}$ at step $t$ is a probability distribution over divisors. State update under signal observation:
$$
\pi_t \;=\; (1 - \eta) \, \pi_{t-1} \;+\; \eta \cdot e(x_t),
$$
where $e(x_t)$ is the declared channel-energy vector of the observed signal $x_t$ (usually $E^{\mathrm{full}}$ for measurement-state updates unless a section explicitly declares $E^{\mathrm{line}}$) and $\eta \in (0, 1]$ is the learning rate. With divisor-graded $H = \mathrm{Diag}(\eta_q)$, the update becomes
$$
\pi_t \;=\; (I - H) \, \pi_{t-1} \;+\; H \cdot e(x_t),
$$
with per-channel learning rates $\eta_q$ controlling channel-specific time constants.

**Ergodic and EMA reading (§5.1a, preserved).** Cesàro and EMA averages of $\pi_t$ agree at stationarity only; in transient regimes they diverge with characteristic time constants controlled by $H$. The Fiedler relaxation rate of $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ at $N = 720$ — $\lambda_1 = 1/\varphi^2$ (Part V / V.14, Thm K.3) — sets the lower bound on the slowest channel's time constant.

**Orbit coarsening (§5.1a, preserved).** Under group action (e.g., the automorphism group of $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ acting on divisors by permuting primes), $\pi_t$ coarsens to orbit-summed quantities. The orbit decomposition (Part II / II.13 helix structure) supplies the orbit-coarsened state vector $\bar{\pi}_t$ used for harmonic-readout summaries.

The decomposition $\delta(e, \pi)$ — divergence between the instantaneous channel-energy and the persistent state — admits a per-orbit decomposition; under $H$, it acquires per-orbit temporal structure.

**§5.1a discipline:** $\pi_t \in \mathbb{R}^{|\mathrm{Div}(N_t)|}$, not $\in \mathrm{Div}(N_t)$. The lattice *indexes* the state space; it is not the state space.

<!-- id: RMK-9.4a-WALK-REGIME-TAXONOMY | register: remark | status: empirical | provenance: original | depends: 5.1b, 9.4 -->
**Remark 9.4a (Walk Regime Taxonomy, preserved, empirical).** The cumulative walk (Definition 5.1b) exhibits three qualitative geometric regimes:

| Walk regime | Dominant $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}}$ range | Walk geometry |
|---|---|---|
| Helix | $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}} < 0$ | Contracting; early harmonics dominate |
| Circle | $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}} \approx 0$ | Closed or nearly closed rosette |
| Spiral | $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}} > 0$ | Expanding; later harmonics dominate |

*Status: Empirical.* The three-regime classification is observed computationally. It describes the dominant tendency of the walk shape as a function of $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}}$.

**Critical nuance.** The regime is NOT controlled by $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}}$ alone. It emerges from the interaction of spin, mode ($k_1$, $k_2$), and $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}}$ jointly. At spin $= 0$, every phase argument vanishes, every step points in the same direction, and the walk collapses to a line segment regardless of $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}}$. Closed forms can occur at slightly positive or negative $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}}$ when spin and mode conspire to produce cancellation. The classification above is the dominant tendency, not a strict partition.

The three walk regimes are structurally suggestive of the three isometry types of the Poincaré half-plane (Hyperbolic, Elliptic, Parabolic) — the Farey tessellation tiles the upper half-plane (Part II / II.4), and there are exactly three isometry classes. Whether this correspondence can be made precise is open (see Part VII / VII.7, Open Problem 25, and Poincaré-metric discussion in the bibliography).

*α_Tilt is the amplitude exponent α (Def 5.1) in the walk/dynamics register; the subscript marks that the walk-derived weighting may diverge from the single-evaluation α (cf. testable prediction 3).*

<!-- id: NOTE-9.4b-FAREY-BOUNDARY | register: interpretive | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 5.1b, 5.4b, 9.4, 9.4a -->
**Interpretive Note 9.4b (Farey Boundary Hypothesis, preserved, interpretive).** The kernel identifies the FVLT carrier axis with the Farey spine (Part II / II.13), connects Vernier phasors to Ford circles (Part II / II.5), and places the Farey tessellation in the upper half-plane (Part II / II.4). The Dirichlet-type weighting identification (Remark 5.4b) shows that negative $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}}$ moves the walk's weighting law into the convergent half-plane of $\zeta(s)$, where arithmetic structure dominates.

**Hypothesis.** Negative $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}}$ performs *rational descent* — motion toward the Farey boundary of the instrument, not merely visual contraction. Specifically:
$$
\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}} < 0 \implies \text{walk privileges low-denominator rational closure scaffold}.
$$

Under this reading: positive $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}}$ — harmonic proliferation, richer interior texture; $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}} = 0$ — boundary between expanding-sum and convergent-sum regimes; negative $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}}$ — rational descent toward boundary-organized rational closure.

**Testable predictions.** If the hypothesis is correct, negative $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}}$ should produce: (1) greater concentration of walk-derived $E_q$ in low-denominator divisor channels; (2) stronger or cleaner throat localization on rational grids in spin/$N$ space; (3) larger divergence between single-evaluation $E_q$ and walk-derived $E_q$; (4) enhanced complement diagnostics ($\mathrm{ce}_z$, $\beta$) in regimes the plan flags as exterior structure.

**Kill condition.** The hypothesis is weakened if negative $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}}$ produces only visual contraction with no corresponding change in the channel structure — i.e., if walk-derived $E_q$ distributions are statistically indistinguishable across $\alpha_{\mathrm{Tilt}}$ values after normalization.

This note does not claim a theorem. It proposes a geometric interpretation and states how it should be tested.

## II.11 Variational completion

**Source:** §5.6 (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.11.  
**Status:** proposed (preserved verbatim).

<!-- id: SEC-5.6-VARIATIONAL-COMPLETION | register: definitional | status: proposed | provenance: original | depends: 5.1 -->
**§5.6 Variational Completion of the Walk Instrument (preserved).** The walk instrument (Part II / II.10) admits a variational completion: a Lagrangian formalism on the divisor lattice from which the persistent-state update law is recoverable as the Euler-Lagrange equation. The proposed action
$$
S_N[\pi] \;=\; \sum_t \mathcal{L}(\pi_t, \pi_{t+1}, x_t)
$$
where $\mathcal{L}$ couples the state vector to the channel-energy vector through a divisor-graded potential. Under the natural choice of potential, unconstrained variational extremals carry conserved divisor-graded momentum.

*Status: proposed.* The formal derivation of the Euler–Lagrange equation from a stated Lagrangian, and the verification that the persistent-state update law is its extremal, is not complete in v2.7.14 and remains the sole *proposed* item in the kernel. See Part VII / VII.7 for the explicit open scope.

## II.12 Gauge structure

**Source:** §7 (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.12.  
**Status:** per §7 block-level (interpretive / structural).

<!-- id: SEC-7-GAUGE-STRUCTURE | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 5.1, 5.3 -->
**§7 Gauge Structure (preserved, v3.2.0 scoped).** The FVLT transform's degrees of freedom decompose into gauge-invariant and gauge-dependent parts. The gauge group is the prime-permutation automorphism of $\mathrm{Div}(N)$, available only when prime-axis lengths permit permutation symmetry. In the $720$ worked instance, $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ carries distinguished, non-interchangeable basis axes of lengths $(5, 3, 2)$ — the asymmetric signature $(4, 2, 1)$ blocks the free $S_3$ on basis primes, so the $720$ instance's automorphism group is trivial on basis primes (Part V / V.16 for the broader $S_6$ automorphism context). Under gauge transformation, individual channel coefficients transform; orbit-summed quantities are gauge-invariant.

**Gauge structure as instrument architecture.** The gauge structure is an architectural feature of the instrument, not an application. It controls which readouts are intrinsic to the signal (gauge-invariant) versus which are instrument-frame-dependent (gauge-covariant). Diagnostic-priority readouts ($\mathrm{ce}_z$, $\beta$, $p_{\mathrm{null}}$, Part V / V.7–V.17) are gauge-invariant by construction; raw individual-channel readouts are not.

<!-- id: RMK-7.1-WEYL-SCALE-GAUGE-CANDIDATE | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 7, IV.1, II.23.2 -->
**Remark 7.1 (Weyl-1918 Scale Gauge as a Candidate Second Gauge Structure, v3.1.10, new, interpretive).** §7 names the kernel's *discrete* gauge structure: the prime-permutation automorphism group of $\mathrm{Div}(N)$, which is trivial on basis primes of $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ and is $S_6$ on $\mathrm{Div}(30030)$ (Lemma I.5.3, Rmk 8.20h.5). This remark records a candidate *continuous* gauge structure on the breathing operating shell, complementary to the permutation gauge.

In Weyl's 1918 gauge theory — originally a proposed unification of electromagnetism with general relativity through local scale-change, later reinterpreted by Weyl 1929 via complex phase — a local scale factor $\lambda(x)$ is treated as a gauge degree of freedom, and the corresponding *gauge potential* is the log derivative
$$
A \;=\; d \log \lambda.
$$
A signal-driven scaling of the Vernier operating shell $n_3(t) = N_{\mathrm{base}} \cdot \lambda(t)$ (with $\lambda(t)$ instrument-declared) carries a Weyl-1918 *scalar scale potential* / log-scale coordinate
$$
G(t) \;=\; \log \lambda(t),
$$
with associated Weyl-1918 *connection one-form*
$$
A \;=\; dG \;=\; d\log \lambda.
$$
The Vernier Conjugate-Pair Lemma (II.23.2) is stated for integer $n_3$ in v3.1.7. The same algebraic proof extends to positive real $n_3(t)$, since the conjugacy step only requires $e^{i s t_1 \pi} = (-1)^{s t_1}$ for integer $s, t_1$, not integrality of $n_3$. In adaptive-shell deployments, $n_3(t)$ is therefore an instrument denominator / operating shell, not necessarily the index of a literal divisor atom. The conjugate-pair structure $z_2 = \bar z_1$ at integer $s$ is preserved under arbitrary gauge choice $\lambda(t)$.

**Coexistence with the permutation gauge.** The permutation gauge of §7 acts on the prime-axis *labels* of $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ at a fixed lens; it is discrete (subgroup of $S_m$). The Weyl scale gauge acts on the *magnitude* of the operating shell across time; it is continuous ($\lambda \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$). The two gauges are orthogonal: the permutation gauge holds $n_3$ fixed and shuffles axis labels, while the Weyl gauge holds the axis labels fixed and scales $n_3$. Their joint structure (e.g., whether a combined gauge group $S_m \times \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ acts coherently, and what the joint invariants would be) is not worked out in v3.1.x and is reserved for future scope.

**Distinction from the kernel's other $\mathbb{Z}/2$ involutions.** The kernel already names three $\mathbb{Z}/2$ involutions: chirality $\chi$ (signed completion at the geometric realization, Part I / I.3), Weyl reflection $w$ (representation-readout level, §G companion-spine), and signed completion (atlas level, Part I / I.3); and one $\mathbb{Z}/2$-like §9.7 operation: winding-only reversal $W$ (Def 9.7o, v3.1.8). All four act on *discrete orientation or sign*. The Weyl-1918 scale gauge of this remark acts on *continuous magnitude*; it is a distinct structural object, not a fifth involution. The historical naming clash (Weyl appears in both "Weyl reflection $w$" and "Weyl-1918 scale gauge") is unavoidable; the two are different constructions from different periods of Weyl's work (the reflection is from finite reflection groups / Lie theory, the scale gauge is from the 1918 unification attempt).

**Status caveats.** This remark records a *modeling choice*, not a theorem. (i) The existence of a Weyl-1918 gauge structure on the breathing operating shell is asserted by the modeling choice $\lambda \mapsto \log \lambda$; it is not derived from prior kernel content. (ii) The specific form of $\lambda(t)$ — including its dependence on signal variance, time, or other external parameters — is instrument-declared and lives at the empirical-instrument register (cf. II.23.7 for the canonical schedule). (iii) Compatibility with the Vernier Conjugate-Pair Lemma is the load-bearing structural anchor for this remark; without II.23.2's algebraic extensibility to real $n_3$, the Weyl-1918 framework would not admit a real-time-varying $n_3(t)$ without breaking the gearbox.

**Provenance.** Origin: a Robin Miller + Gemini exploration of Weyl gauge theory, fall 2025, that produced the Ratioque v2.6 corporate integrity monitor (November 30, 2025) with "Axiom A2 Weyl-Miller Gauge Potential." The v2.6 framing tied the gauge to "depth into outer automorphism space" — that framing is *superseded* by v3.1.9's explicit Out($S_6$) closure (Rmk 8.20h.5, order-2 discrete with three same-size class-pair swaps). The Weyl-1918 structural reading of the formula survives the supersession; this remark records the survival.

## II.13 Helix readout

**Source:** §9 preamble, §9.1, §9.1a, §9.2, §9.3 preamble, Rmk 9.3b (v2.6.2), Rmk 9.3c (v2.7.4), §9.3a + Note 9.3a.1 (v2.7.13), §8.7 general-representation half, §9.4 Stern–Brocot item.  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.13.  
**Status:** unconditional / definitional / interpretive (per-block).

The helix readout realizes $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ as a geometric figure in $\mathbb{R}^3$. The reading was authored before the kernel — Robin's 2011 helix drawing, formalized in GeoGebra; the kernel articulates what the construction already contained.

**§9 preamble (preserved).** The Farey spine is the rational skeleton; the helix family is its exponential lift. This is the single sentence to carry forward.

The construction has **three distinct objects** (§9.3a, do not conflate):

1. **Farey spine** $S = \{(0, 0, z) : z \in \mathbb{R}\}$ — rational ordering axis carrying integer markers, Farey ticks, factorial-height markers. Projection target, not a member of the helix family.
2. **Helix family** $\{\Gamma_n\}_{n \geq 2}$ where $H_n(u) = ((1/n)\cos 2\pi u,\, (1/n)\sin 2\pi u,\, u)$. Each $\Gamma_n$ lies on cylinder $C_n$ of radius $1/n$.
3. **Projection relation**: rational $j/n$ ↔ bead $H_n(j/n) = ((1/n)\cos 2\pi j/n,\, (1/n)\sin 2\pi j/n,\, j/n)$, doubly coding $j/n \leftrightarrow e^{2\pi i j/n}$.

**§9.1 The Identification (preserved).** The single-cylinder identification of rational $j/n$ with helix bead $H_n(j/n)$ is the basic geometric move: a rational lives twice — once as a position on the spine ($z = j/n$), once as a phase on a circle (cylinder cross-section). The phase encoding is automatic from the spine position when the cyclic order $n$ is named.

**§9.1a Rational skeleton and exponential lift (preserved, load-bearing).** Define the cylinder $C_n$ of radius $1/n$ co-axial with the spine. The helix $\Gamma_n$ traces $C_n$ as $H_n(u)$ varies over $u \in [0, 1)$, returning to base after one period. The bead $H_n(j/n)$ sits at angular position $2\pi j/n$ on $C_n$; under projection to the spine, it lands at $z = j/n$. The doubly-coded reading $j/n \leftrightarrow e^{2\pi i j/n}$ is intrinsic to $\Gamma_n$.

**§9.2 Induced structures (preserved).** From the helix family inherit: Farey ordering (along the spine), Stern–Brocot tree structure (mediants under the helix), cylindrical-cross-section harmonic structure ($n$-th roots of unity at $\Gamma_n$'s base), and pinch-point structure (apparent crossings as projection artifacts).

<!-- id: LEM-9.2a-FINITE-IDEAL-FRONTIER-LAW | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: adapted (classical binary-tree combinatorics; FVLT contribution is seating + instrumentation only) | depends: 9.2, 9.1a | routing: kernel-body LOCKED by Robin 2026-07-14 (ratified amendment to the 2026-07-12 WO-SB/M lock, correcting its kernel-non-contact clause); statement/proofs drafted by Fable, COLD-PASSED by Codex 2026-07-14 (PASS-WITH-PATCHES, both patches applied — see COLDPASS_REQUEST_Lemma_9_2a_frontier_law_2026-07-14.md); LEMMA LOCKED by Robin 2026-07-14 ("lock it"); v3.2.5 release LOCKED by Robin 2026-07-14 -->
**Lemma 9.2a (Finite-Ideal Frontier Law for the Stern–Brocot Tree, v3.2.5).**

Let $T$ be the (infinite) Stern–Brocot tree: the binary tree whose vertices are the interior mediants of the root interval $(0/1,\,1/1)$, root $1/2 = \operatorname{med}(0/1,1/1)$, each vertex the mediant of its two Farey-adjacent flanks and carrying exactly two children (left and right mediants). The endpoints $0/1,\,1/1$ are the bounds of the root interval and are **not** vertices of $T$. A **finite order ideal** $I \subseteq V(T)$ is a finite subset such that, whenever a non-root vertex $v \in I$, $\operatorname{parent}(v) \in I$ (the root $1/2$, whose tree parent is undefined, is exempt from the condition). Write $\partial I = \{\,\text{children of } I \text{ not in } I\,\}$ for its **frontier** (the minimal excluded vertices — an antichain), and $\operatorname{open}(I)$ for the number of **open adjacency edges**: consecutive pairs in the spine order of $I \cup \{0/1,1/1\}$ whose mediant is a frontier vertex, *excluding the two extreme pairs that touch $0/1$ and $1/1$*.

**(i) Frontier law (nonempty $I$).** $\;|\partial I| = |I| + 1.$

**(ii) Open-edge law (nonempty $I$).** $\;\operatorname{open}(I) = |I| - 1,\;$ hence $\;|\partial I| = \operatorname{open}(I) + 2.$

**(iii) Empty-ideal boundary.** For $I = \varnothing$: $\partial\varnothing = \{1/2\}$ (the root, whose parent is the root interval, not a vertex), so (i) holds as $1 = 0 + 1$; $\operatorname{open}(\varnothing) = 0$ and (ii) fails ($0 \neq -1$). Only the frontier law survives the empty boundary.

*Proof of (i) — child-slot count.* Root $T$ at $1/2$; every vertex has exactly two children. Let $I$ be nonempty finite, $|I| = k$. As a down-set containing the root, $I$ is a subtree with $k$ vertices and $k-1$ edges. The child-slots emanating from $I$ number $2k$; of these, $k-1$ land in $I$ (each of the $k-1$ non-root vertices of $I$ is the unique child-image of a vertex of $I$). The remaining $2k - (k-1) = k+1$ land outside $I$; since each vertex has a unique parent these are $k+1$ distinct vertices, precisely $\partial I$. Hence $|\partial I| = k+1$. For $I=\varnothing$, the root $1/2$ is the unique vertex whose parent is not a vertex, so $\partial\varnothing = \{1/2\}$. $\square$

*Proof of (ii) — induction on frontier admission.* Admit frontier vertices one at a time (each admission preserves the down-set / tessellation-convexity). Base $I = \{1/2\}$: the spine list is $0/1,\,1/2,\,1/1$; both edges touch an endpoint and are excluded, so $\operatorname{open} = 0 = |I|-1$. Step: admitting a frontier vertex $v = \operatorname{med}(u,w)$ replaces the consecutive pair $(u,w)$ by $(u,v),(v,w)$. If both flanks are interior, $(u,w)$ was open and is replaced by two open edges: net $+1$. If one flank is an endpoint, that new edge is excluded and only the interior one is open: still net $+1$ (the previously-uncounted endpoint edge is replaced by one uncounted endpoint edge and one open edge). Either way $\operatorname{open}$ rises by $1$ as $|I|$ rises by $1$, so $\operatorname{open}(I) - (|I|-1)$ is invariant at its base value $0$. The "$+2$" is exactly the two extreme frontier vertices, namely the mediants of the leftmost and rightmost endpoint-touching consecutive pairs in the spine order. Those two adjacency edges are excluded from $\operatorname{open}(I)$, while the two frontier vertices remain counted in $\partial I$. $\square$

**Provenance and scope.** `provenance: adapted`. The content is classical finite-binary-tree combinatorics (child count $2k$, subtree edge count $k-1$); the FVLT contribution is the *seating* of the law on the §9.2 Stern–Brocot structure induced by the helix family, and its *instrumentation* by the ideal-windows viewer. **Out of scope here** (bibliography / viewer tier, per the WO-SB/M lock record and its amendment): the ideal-windows and Markov viewers and their on-load batteries; the "second additive-side atlas" interpretation (candidate/interpretive per §9.6 cousinhood discipline); and the implementation-specific `GROW_MAX_DEN` / "children fully represented" rendering guard — in the abstract tree $T$ both children exist automatically, so that guard only certifies that a *finite viewer* has rendered them and is not a hypothesis of this lemma.

**§9.3 Farey Helix preamble (preserved).** The Farey helix is the union of beads of $\Gamma_n$ over $n \leq N_0$ at canonical Farey-tick positions, equipped with the spine as projection target.

<!-- id: RMK-9.3b-PARITY-CYCLIC-LIFT | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 9.1a -->
**Remark 9.3b (Parity of Cyclic Lift, v2.6.2, preserved).** The cyclic lift $u \mapsto (cos 2\pi u, \sin 2\pi u, u)$ has period $1$ in $u$ but distinguishes positions modulo $1/n$ on $\Gamma_n$. The lift's parity — whether $H_n(j/n)$ and $H_n((j + n/2)/n)$ are antipodal on $C_n$ — depends on the parity of $n$.

For even $n$: $H_n(j/n)$ and $H_n((j + n/2)/n)$ are antipodal in the $(x, y)$ plane (rotated by $\pi$). The pentagon at $n = 5$ has no antipodal pairs; the hexagon at $n = 6$ has three antipodal pairs $\{1/6, 4/6\}$, $\{2/6, 5/6\}$, $\{3/6, 6/6\}$.

This parity distinguishes $n$-even from $n$-odd helix structure at the bead level.

<!-- id: RMK-9.3c-SUBSTRATE-LEVEL-PHI | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 9.1a, 9.3 -->
**Remark 9.3c (Substrate-Level $\varphi$ on $\Gamma_5$, v2.7.4, preserved).** On $\Gamma_5$, the second-neighbor / first-neighbor chord ratio is
$$
\frac{|H_5(0) - H_5(2/5)|}{|H_5(0) - H_5(1/5)|} \;=\; \frac{\sin(2\pi/5)}{\sin(\pi/5)} \;=\; 2\cos(\pi/5) \;=\; \varphi.
$$
This is the regular pentagon's classical diagonal-to-side ratio (Euclid Book IV) expressed in helix-bead language.

**Substrate-level location.** Fivefold cyclic bead geometry therefore carries $\varphi$ at the substrate level, before any particular readout mechanism is selected. This locates a geometric ancestor common to every downstream appearance of $\varphi$ that arises from 5-fold structure, but it does not identify those mechanisms with one another: the divisor-lattice route (Part V / V.14, Thm K.3 and Rmk K.3d) reads 5-fold structure through path-graph spectra; modular and Rogers–Ramanujan routes read it through level structure, modular equivariance, and complex multiplication; icosahedral routes read it through $A_5$ representation theory. The substrate is shared; the orbit machines that act on it are not.

### §9.3a Geometric progenitor — three objects, one lemma (with Note 9.3a.1, preserved together)

<!-- id: LEM-9.3a-NO-ACCIDENTAL-INTERSECTIONS | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 9.1a, 9.3 -->
<!-- id: LEMMA-9.3h-RESIDENCE-ORDER | register: lemma | status: unconditional (residence order, meet, join, atlas realization — i-iv) + interpretive (register readings — v) | provenance: adapted (classical order fact) over original (residence on the §9.1a progenitor, kernel cross-wiring) | depends: 9.1a, 9.3a, 2.2b.2, 1.2a, II.5d -->
**Lemma 9.3h (Residence Order: the Atlas on the Geometric Progenitor, v3.2.4, new).**

**Definition (residence set).** For $n \geq 1$, the **residence set** of $\Gamma_n$ is the collapse of its bead set to the spine:

$$R(\Gamma_n) \;=\; \mathrm{collapse}\{H_n(j/n) : j \in \mathbb{Z}\} \;=\; \tfrac{1}{n}\mathbb{Z} \;\subset\; \mathbb{R}.$$

Residence is a *collapsed* datum: the curves themselves remain pairwise disjoint in $\mathbb{R}^3$ (Lemma 9.3a); all order structure below is read through shared spine coordinates, exactly as the 9.3a consequence rule requires.

---

**Statement.**

**(i) Inclusion is divisibility (unconditional).**
$$R(\Gamma_m) \subseteq R(\Gamma_n) \iff m \mid n.$$
*Proof.* If $R(\Gamma_m) \subseteq R(\Gamma_n)$ then $1/m = i/n$ for some integer $i$, so $n = im$ and $m \mid n$. Conversely if $n = m m'$ then $j/m = j m'/n$. $\square$

**(ii) Meet is gcd (unconditional).**
$$R(\Gamma_m) \cap R(\Gamma_n) = R(\Gamma_{\gcd(m,n)}).$$
*Proof.* If $xm \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $xn \in \mathbb{Z}$, then $x(am + bn) \in \mathbb{Z}$ for all integers $a, b$, hence $x\gcd(m,n) \in \mathbb{Z}$ by Bézout. Conversely $\gcd(m,n) \mid m, n$ gives containment both ways. $\square$

Read geometrically: **the heights at which $\Gamma_m$ and $\Gamma_n$ both carry beads are exactly the rationals with denominator dividing $\gcd(m,n)$** — a Vernier coincidence law for gcd on the progenitor, and the geometric parent of Prop 2.2b.2 ($\mathrm{Div}(A) \cap \mathrm{Div}(B) = \mathrm{Div}(\gcd(A,B))$).

**(iii) Join is lcm (unconditional).** The minimal residence set containing $R(\Gamma_m) \cup R(\Gamma_n)$ is $R(\Gamma_{\mathrm{lcm}(m,n)})$; equivalently, the subgroup of $(\mathbb{Q}, +)$ generated by $1/m$ and $1/n$ is $\tfrac{1}{\mathrm{lcm}(m,n)}\mathbb{Z}$.
*Proof.* $(1/N)\mathbb{Z} \supseteq$ both residence sets iff $m \mid N$ and $n \mid N$ iff $\mathrm{lcm}(m,n) \mid N$; minimal such $N$ is the lcm. For the subgroup form: $\mathrm{lcm}/m$ and $\mathrm{lcm}/n$ are coprime, so integer combinations of $1/m, 1/n$ reach $1/\mathrm{lcm}$. $\square$

This is Rmk 1.2a's minimal-containing-lattice principle in the height register.

**(iv) Atlas realization (unconditional).** Define the **residence order** on the family: $\Gamma_m \preccurlyeq_R \Gamma_n \iff R(\Gamma_m) \subseteq R(\Gamma_n)$. The map $n \mapsto R(\Gamma_n)$ is an order isomorphism from $(\mathbb{N}_{>0}, \mid)$ onto the residence sets ordered by inclusion, carrying gcd to intersection and lcm to join. Then, for every $N$,

$$\mathrm{Div}(N) \;\cong\; \{\, \Gamma_n : n \mid N \,\}$$

as the principal down-set below $\Gamma_N$ in the residence order $\preccurlyeq_R$; equivalently, $\mathrm{Div}(N) \cong \{\, R(\Gamma_n) : R(\Gamma_n) \subseteq R(\Gamma_N) \,\}$. The order is on residence-read curves, never spatial containment of curves. **The concentric helix family carries the entire atlas $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$ as an order structure**, read through residence. The v3.2.0 contract made $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$ the substrate; this clause seats the substrate on the geometric progenitor itself.

**(v) Register readings (interpretive).**
- *Farey-tick corollary:* the tick set of $F_k$ resides inside $R(\Gamma_{\mathrm{lcm}(1,\ldots,k)})$, canonically hosted at the factorial rung $R(\Gamma_{k!})$ — the residence-order face of Rmk II.5d clause (i).
- *Phase rigidity:* since every $H_n(u)$ shares the parameter $u$, the family renders the single universal character $x \mapsto e^{2\pi i x}$ at radii $1/n$; orders differ only in bead residence. The double coding $j/n \leftrightarrow e^{2\pi i j/n}$ is thus family-wide: the phase at height $x$ is $e^{2\pi i x}$ on every cylinder, and residence selects which heights are *marked*.
- *Two realizations, two registers:* the §9.7 multi-prime embedding realizes one atom $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ at a time as *position* (translated cylinders, chords, strain); the present lemma realizes the whole atlas order at once as *residence*. Position and residence are distinct registers over the same substrate and must not be conflated.

---

**Classicality note (cf. Rmk 5.7a precedent).**

The abstract content of (i)–(iv) is classical: the sets $\tfrac{1}{n}\mathbb{Z}$ are the preimages of the finite cyclic subgroups of $\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}$, and the lattice of finite cyclic subgroups of $\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}$ is textbook-isomorphic to $(\mathbb{N}_{>0}, \mid)$ with meet/join = gcd/lcm. `provenance: adapted`. The FVLT contribution is threefold: the *residence* seating on the §9.1a construction (bead sets of the physical helix family, compatible with Lemma 9.3a's disjointness), the identification of the atlas $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$ as already-resident on the progenitor, and the cross-wiring (Prop 2.2b.2 as meet clause, Rmk 1.2a as join clause, Rmk II.5d as Farey corollary).

---

**Guards.**

**G-disjoint.** The order lives on collapsed residence sets, never on curve intersection. $\Gamma_m \cap \Gamma_n = \emptyset$ stands (Lemma 9.3a); no spatial-containment claim is made or implied.

**G-lift.** The family-of-lifts doctrine is unchanged: no canonical lift is selected. The isomorphism orders the *totality*; it does not privilege a member.

**G-position.** No claim that residence order reproduces §9.7 position data (embedding coordinates, apex defect, strain). Residence is coarser by design: it forgets radius, phase anchor, and multiplicity geometry, retaining only the divisibility skeleton.

---

<!-- id: RMK-9.3h-A-TWIST-STRATUM | register: remark | status: definitional (viewer-implemented construction; gearbox correspondence open) | provenance: original | depends: 9.1a, 9.3h -->
**Remark 9.3h·A (Twist parameter: the $\Gamma_{n,k}$ stratum, v3.2.4, new).**

The viewer `farey_helix_family_super_.html` implements a **twist count** $k$: bead $j$ at height $j/(kn)$ with phase $2\pi j/n$. Equivalently, the bead at rational $x = j/(kn)$ carries phase $e^{2\pi i k x}$ — the $k$-th character of the circle in place of the fundamental one — and the bead-to-phase map realizes the quotient homomorphism $\mathbb{Z}/kn \twoheadrightarrow \mathbb{Z}/n$ ($kn$ beads per unit height, $n$ angular positions). The §9.1a family as written is the $k = 1$ stratum. The twist axis extends the construction from fundamental characters to the character group, giving inter-order quotient maps geometric residence.

**Open correspondence (do not assert):** whether the twist parameter is the geometric face of the Vernier gearbox parameters (II.23.2, $k_j = n_3 t_1 \pm t_2$) requires verification before any claim. Staged as a question, not a result.

**Notation guard (required, per audit 2026-07-05):** the twisted stratum carries its own index — $\Gamma_{n,k}$ (equivalently $H_{n,k}$), never bare $\Gamma_n$. Its residence set is $R(\Gamma_{n,k}) = \tfrac{1}{kn}\mathbb{Z}$: twist changes residence density, and Lemma 9.3h is the $k = 1$ residence order. Reusing $\Gamma_n$ unindexed would silently mutate the lemma. Seat: definitional/viewer-construction note near §9.1a or immediately after Lemma 9.3h — not inside it.
