<!-- id: RMK-9.3i-RECIPROCAL-RESIDENCE | register: remark | status: unconditional (C1-C3, C4 reduced-bead transpose, C6) + interpretive (C4 display/throat readings, C5 star-polygon) | provenance: adapted (cyclic-subgroup lattice of ℚ) over original (reciprocal-tower residence on the progenitor) | depends: 9.3h, II.5c, 9.3c -->
**Remark 9.3i (Reciprocal residence: the exponent completion on the progenitor, v3.2.4, new).**

**Origin:** Robin's observation on the passed lemma — *"this is just 1/n … there are also n/1."* The lemma's residence sets $(1/n)\mathbb{Z}$ are the subgroups of $\mathbb{Q}$ *containing* $\mathbb{Z}$ (upper tower). The sets $n\mathbb{Z}$ — sparse sub-spines marking every $n$-th integer — are residence sets *inside* $\mathbb{Z}$ (lower tower), with inclusion reversed: $m\mathbb{Z} \subseteq n\mathbb{Z} \iff n \mid m$.

**(C1) General residence (unconditional).** For $q \in \mathbb{Q}^+$, set $R(q) = q\mathbb{Z} \subset \mathbb{Q}$. Every infinite cyclic subgroup of $\mathbb{Q}$ is $q\mathbb{Z}$ for a unique $q \in \mathbb{Q}^+$. The lemma's $R(\Gamma_n)$ is $R(1/n)$; the lower tower is $R(n)$.

**(C2) Inclusion is reverse ℚ⁺-divisibility (unconditional).** $q\mathbb{Z} \subseteq r\mathbb{Z} \iff q/r \in \mathbb{Z} \iff e_p(q) \geq e_p(r)$ for all $p$. The map $q \mapsto q\mathbb{Z}$ is an order anti-isomorphism from the exponent completion $\mathbb{Q}^+ \cong \bigoplus_p \mathbb{Z}$ (Rmk II.5c) onto the cyclic subgroups of $\mathbb{Q}$. Meets and joins: $q\mathbb{Z} \cap r\mathbb{Z} = \mathrm{lcm}_{\mathbb{Q}^+}(q,r)\mathbb{Z}$ (exponent-wise max); $q\mathbb{Z} + r\mathbb{Z} = \gcd_{\mathbb{Q}^+}(q,r)\mathbb{Z}$ (exponent-wise min, via Bézout). Clauses (i)–(iii) of the lemma are the restriction to the upper tower.

**(C3) Reciprocal involution (unconditional).** $q \mapsto 1/q$ is exponent negation (Rmk II.5c) and acts on residence as the order-reversing involution of the cyclic-subgroup lattice fixing the origin $\mathbb{Z} = R(1)$, exchanging the two towers. The v3.2.2 minimal witnesses $2/3, 3/2$ acquire residence sets $(2/3)\mathbb{Z} \leftrightarrow (3/2)\mathbb{Z}$ — the first off-tower pair. Geometric form of the v3.2.2 keeper: *the unit was not destroyed; it was revealed as the fixed origin* — here literally the spine's integer lattice, the fixed point of the involution around which the towers reflect.

**(C4) Transpose involution and the throat's geometric location. The reduced-bead transpose is **unconditional**; the display/throat-location reading is **interpretive**. Robin origin — "the helix here is just one side; it can wind from the opposite side starting at 1/1 or −1/1 and going to infinity; this is where the throat is located".** **On reduced beads**, the reciprocal map $x \mapsto 1/x$ acts as the **index transpose**: bead $j$ of $\Gamma_n \mapsto$ bead $n$ of $\Gamma_j$ (height $j/n \mapsto n/j$, cylinder $1/n \mapsto 1/j$, phase $\zeta_n^{\,j} \mapsto \zeta_j^{\,n}$; reducedness is preserved since $\gcd(j,n) = 1$ is symmetric). **Reduced/unreduced guard (per audit):** for non-coprime pairs the winding entries $n/j$ (e.g. $4/2 = 2/1$) are an *unreduced bead-family display path*; the canonical residence denominator is obtained only after reducing $n/j$. The transpose rule is stated on reduced beads only; the viewer's full-$j$ threads are display, not canonical residence. The fixed rational is $1/1$, with $-1/1$ as its mirror on the I.3 signed sheet (G-signed: orientation register, not touched here). **Throat register declaration (per audit — never say "the throat" bare):** *in the family register, the throat is the unit-height plane* $z = 1$, threaded by every helix at phase $0$; *in the log-mirror register, the throat is the unit point* $1/1$, the tip/waist fixed by $x \leftrightarrow 1/x$. Consequences: the construction below the unit and the construction above the unit are one bead family, transpose-paired; the reciprocal winding of order $n$ starts at the throat and threads *down the cylinder family* — $n/(n{-}1)$ on $\Gamma_{n-1}$, $n/(n{-}2)$ on $\Gamma_{n-2}$, … — while rising, landing on the spine at $n/1$ (the multiples ray of C1–C3); the v3.2.2 minimal witnesses appear as $2/3$ on $\Gamma_3 \leftrightarrow 3/2$ on $\Gamma_2$, the first interior transpose pair straddling the throat. Viewer realization: `farey_helix_family_super_.html` "Reciprocal winding (throat at 1/1)" toggle (2026-07-05). **Display registers (two, keep separate):** the *family register* places every rational on its denominator cylinder (radius $1/\mathrm{den}$; above the unit everything stays within radius $1/2$, collapsing toward the spine); the *value register* places the bead of value $x$ at radius $x$ — the reciprocal side then lies on the cone $r = z$ carrying the universal character angle $2\pi x$, integers land on the $+X$ axis at $(n, 0, n)$, and the unit fractions $1/n$ already lie on the same cone below the throat (radius $1/n$ = height). One cone carries the whole exchange: unit fractions descend into the apex at $0$, reciprocals flare toward infinity, and the waist is the circle $r = 1, z = 1$ where the cone crosses the unit cylinder $C_1$ — the §9.1a $n = 1$ helix, previously "labeling convention only," acquiring a structural job as the throat cylinder (Robin's correction, 2026-07-05: "it moves to the center but not outward" — the family-register rendering was replaced by the value-register rendering for the reciprocal side). **Final display (Robin's selection, after comparison with the "where it sits in the cone" instrument — "I don't think the cone works that way"): the log-mirror register.** Value $x$ at height $1 + \ln x$, radius $|\ln x|$: the reciprocal map is an *exact reflection* through the unit tip $(0,0,1)$; pairs $x, 1/x$ share radius and mirror in height; the exchange is a tip-to-tip double cone whose waist is the single point $1/1$, matching the cone instrument's convention (unit at tip; reciprocal cone through the zero — the lower sheet passes through height $0$ toward value $0$ at $-\infty$). The value-register cone $r = z$ was rejected because it places the throat as a circle at height 1 rather than as the unit point, and cannot render reciprocal as reflection. Three display registers now on record: family (denominator cylinder), value (cone $r=z$), log-mirror (double cone, reciprocal = reflection); the log-mirror is the exponent-completion-native one (reciprocal $=$ negation $=$ reflection).

**(C5) Star-polygon reading (interpretive).** For reduced $a/b$, the residence $R(a/b) = (a/b)\mathbb{Z}$ read against $\Gamma_b$ is the stride-$a$ subsample of its beads: consecutive residence heights differ by $a/b$, consecutive phases by $\zeta_b^{\,a} = \zeta_b^{\,a \bmod b}$ — **the stride is read modulo $b$** (per audit; this matters for fractions above $1$, where $a > b$). Since $\gcd(a,b) = 1$ the phase orbit is all of $\mu_b$. The chord traversal is the star polygon $\{b/a\}$ with $a$ read modulo $b$, traced helically. Rmk 9.3c's substrate-level $\varphi$ — the second-neighbor/first-neighbor chord ratio on $\Gamma_5$ — is the $\{5/2\}$ pentagram instance of this general stride construction.

**Guards.** *G-signed (per Rmk II.5c):* this signs the residence axis (exponent range), not orientation/chirality; no contact with the kernel I.3 signed completion $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}$. *G-cyclic:* scope is cyclic subgroups only; non-cyclic subgroups of $\mathbb{Q}$ (e.g. $\mathbb{Z}[1/2]$, the Steinitz/supernatural range) are out of scope — flag only, no OP opened. *G-lemma:* the passed Lemma 9.3h is not modified; this companion extends it through the origin and is auditable independently.

**(C6) Contact-locus identity chain (definitional; origin — Robin: "it does remind me a bit of the supercharacter viewer").** For each $n$, three registers name the same set. The residence set $n\mathbb{Z}$ (C1/C3; the viewer's multiples marks) restricted to positive integers equals the integer locus of ALG-1's divisor gate at $N = n$ ($\operatorname{Im}(z_1) = 0$ at integer spin $s \iff n \mid s$) and equals the signed-maximum set of the Ramanujan/supercharacter channel $c_n$ ($c_n(s) = \varphi(n) \iff n \mid s$; all other values are strictly smaller as signed quantities). Divisor contact rendered three ways: **residence** (geometric marks on the spine), **gate** (phasor readout), **peak** (harmonic channel). This is the residence-register face of Rmk 1.5c's same-divisor-two-readouts pairing and a third instance of II.23.8's four-readouts-of-one-winding discipline: position says where the channel lives, the supercharacter says what it measures, residence says when it fires.

<!-- id: RMK-9.3j-LOGMIRROR-PHASE-RIGIDITY | register: remark | status: interpretive (display-register reading; unconditional content underneath is 9.3i C1-C3) | provenance: original (dual of 9.3h(v) on the log-mirror register) | depends: 9.1a, 9.3h, 9.3i, II.5e | locked: Robin 2026-07-06 (item); v3.2.5 release LOCKED by Robin 2026-07-14 -->
**Remark 9.3j (Log-mirror phase rigidity — the dual of 9.3h(v), v3.2.5).**

Lemma 9.3h(v) reads phase rigidity in the *family register*: the family renders the single universal character $x \mapsto e^{2\pi i x}$ at radii $1/n$, and residence selects which heights are marked. The *log-mirror register* (Rmk 9.3i C4; II.5e D1, $L(x)=\ln x$) renders the *same* universal character $x \mapsto e^{2\pi i x}$ at radius $|L(x)|$ and height $1 + L(x)$: one curve, residence selecting the marks.

The two are the two faces of the §9.1a lift/collapse duality:

- The **family lift** unfolds *cyclic identity* — "which $\mathbb{Z}/n$ is this rational a phase of?" — a question with no canonical answer, hence a **family** of curves at radii $1/n$ (the "no canonical lift" doctrine of §9.1a).
- The **log-mirror** is a **lift of the collapse**: it takes each bead, collapses it to its spine value $x$, and re-lifts by the Archimedean grade — "how large is this rational?" — a question with exactly **one** answer, hence **one** curve. Bead position factors through the spine value alone: $\;\mathbf{F}(x) = \big(|L(x)|\cos 2\pi x,\; |L(x)|\sin 2\pi x,\; 1 + L(x)\big)$, a function of $x$, not of $n$.

**Consequence.** Every bead of every reciprocal-winding thread ($x \in \mathbb{Q}^+$; the map is defined only for positive values, $0$ and the signed sheet excluded), all orders, lies on the single curve $\mathbf{F}$. Any positive value realized on more than one (order, thread) has all its realizations coincide at the one point $\mathbf{F}(x)$; the realization count is window-dependent, the collapse point is not. The two radius laws are orthogonal — family radius $1/n$ (cyclic order) vs log-mirror radius $|L(x)|$ (Archimedean magnitude) — carried on the *same* phase $2\pi x$; arithmetic relations in both registers are read through the shared spine collapse, per the §9.1a consequence rule. *The family lift asks which cyclic group; the log-mirror lift asks how large. Same beads, same phase, two orthogonal radius laws — the two sides of one collapse.*

**Domain / reflection guard.** The log-mirror register is defined on positive values $x \in \mathbb{Q}^+$; $0$ and the signed sheet are outside this map. The reciprocal symmetry is the **log-profile** symmetry: $x \leftrightarrow 1/x$ preserves radius $|\ln x|$ and reflects height $1+\ln x$ across the unit level $z=1$. It is **not** in general a full point-reflection through the unit tip $(0,0,1)$ in $\mathbb{R}^3$, because the retained phase runs from $2\pi x$ to $2\pi/x$. Full 3D point-reflection holds at the fixed throat $x=1$ trivially ($\mathbf{F}(1)=(0,0,1)$ is the tip itself, zero radius); for $x\neq1$ it holds **iff** $x - 1/x \equiv \tfrac12 \pmod 1$. The invariant asserted here is **value-collapse**, not 3D reflection.

**Bridge to Rmk 9.3i(C4).** This clarifies the Rmk 9.3i(C4) "exact reflection through the unit tip" wording: the exact reflection is the log-profile symmetry — shared radius and mirrored height — not generally full 3D point-reflection once the retained phase $2\pi x$ is included, except at the fixed throat $x=1$ and the non-throat solutions $x - 1/x \equiv \tfrac12 \pmod 1$.

**Verification.** Companion scripts, per-file SHA-256: `verify_logmirror_collapse.py` `7ad96353086417c46405758b565c5c285d7727d68d04e6bf714e357d43bb13a2` (value-collapse, max deviation $0.0$ over 420 beads; orthogonal radius laws; Farey-tick corollary); `verify_reflection_guard.py` `2c4adc9282caf089973c8be1e226b96d48498a7118f3b2df0a45e45ff8747819` (log-profile reflection all $x>0$; throat-aware exact rational predicate); `check_1_6_witness.py` `134b50ee7fe20a9aae225a9a049290696a684a723ed075a62f47c1bc60bbd8b5` (window-dependence of the $1/6$ realization count). Viewer of record `farey_helix_family_super_.html` `30036119f14301335fe9e0aa1e020375aa6792f6dc35a9b5204831662c01cf0d`. Item locked Robin 2026-07-06; full provenance `fableous/locked/rmk_9_3j_LOCKED_kernel_insert.md`.

**Lemma 9.3a (No Accidental Spatial Intersections, preserved, unconditional).** For distinct $m, n \geq 2$, $\Gamma_m \cap \Gamma_n = \emptyset$; for all $n \geq 2$, $\Gamma_n \cap S = \emptyset$. Cylinders are disjoint; helices share no points in $\mathbb{R}^3$.

**Consequence.** Apparent crossings in 2D views are projection artifacts. Spine projections are intentional readout. Arithmetic relations are read through shared spine coordinates, Farey ordering, and phase identification — never through curve intersection.

<!-- id: NOTE-9.3a.1-CANONICAL-VS-TRANSLATED | register: note | status: structural | provenance: original | depends: 9.3a, 9.7 -->
**Note 9.3a.1 (Canonical concentric form vs multi-prime translated form, v2.7.13, preserved).** Lemma 9.3a applies to the canonical concentric-cylinder form of §9.1a. The multi-prime divisor-lattice embedding of Part II / II.14 translates each cylinder so that the unit lands on the spine; cylinders in that form share the spine-contact locus by construction and are not pairwise disjoint. The two forms are structurally complementary, not in conflict. *(Per Robin's verdict, the lemma and note remain together: the note is the scope boundary on the lemma's geometric regime.)*

**§8.7 Spinor–Tensor Decomposition of the Divisor Lattice (general representation half, preserved).** The Möbius function $\mu$ and the additive function $\Omega$ together decompose the divisor lattice into sectors with distinct algebraic character. The squarefree sublattice carries the full Möbius weight; the non-squarefree sublattice is Möbius-silent. This decomposition has the structure of a spinor–tensor grading.

<!-- id: DEF-8.35-SPINOR-AND-TENSOR-SECTORS | register: definitional | status: unconditional | provenance: adapted | depends: 8.17 -->
**Definition 8.35 (Spinor and Tensor Sectors, preserved).** The divisor lattice $\mathrm{Div}(N_0)$ decomposes as:

- *Spinor sector*: $\mathcal{S}(N_0) = \{q \in \mathrm{Div}(N_0) : \mu(q) \neq 0\}$ — squarefree divisors, carrying nonzero Möbius weight.
- *Tensor sector*: $\mathcal{T}(N_0) = \{q \in \mathrm{Div}(N_0) : \mu(q) = 0\}$ — non-squarefree divisors, containing at least one repeated prime factor.

**Proposition 8.36 (Möbius Chirality, preserved, unconditional).** The Möbius function grades the spinor sector into two chiralities: $\mu(q) = +1$ (even $\Omega$, even chirality) and $\mu(q) = -1$ (odd $\Omega$, odd chirality).

**Proposition 8.37 (Tensor Rank, preserved, unconditional).** $\Omega(q)$ stratifies $\mathrm{Div}(N_0)$ by tensor rank: scalar ($\Omega = 0$), vector ($\Omega = 1$), rank-$\Omega$ tensor for higher values, volume form at $\Omega = \Omega_{\max}$. Covering edges $q \to pq$ correspond to index-raising; descent corresponds to contraction.

The 720-specific instantiation of these structures — Propositions 8.38 (Bridge as Spinor Isomorphism), 8.39 (Double Cover via $\omega^{N_0/2} = -1$), and the spinor–tensor architecture at $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ — lives at Part V / V.13.

**§9.4 classical connection — Stern–Brocot tree (preserved):**

| Classical Object | Role in FVLT |
|---|---|
| **Stern–Brocot tree** | Binary tree of mediants [Ste58, Bro61]. Factorial shells sit at specific depths. |

## II.14 Multi-prime polygonal embedding

**Source:** Def 9.7, Lem 9.7a, Rmk 9.7e (v2.7.13).  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.14.  
**Status:** definitional / unconditional / interpretive (per-block).

The multi-prime embedding extends the single-cylinder helix of Part II / II.13 to realize $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ as a geometric lattice for $N$ with multiple prime factors, while keeping the unit anchored on the spine.

<!-- id: DEF-9.7-MULTI-PRIME-EMBEDDING | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: 9.3, 9.3a -->
**Definition 9.7 (Multi-Prime Divisor-Lattice Embedding, v2.7.13, preserved).** For
$$
N \;=\; \prod_{i=0}^{m-1} p_i^{a_i}
$$
under canonical prime ordering $p_0 < p_1 < \cdots < p_{m-1}$, let
$$
n_i = a_i + 1, \qquad r_i = \frac{1}{n_i}, \qquad \varphi_i = \frac{2\pi i}{m}.
$$
The multi-prime embedding places each divisor $d \mid N$, with exponent vector $(e_0, e_1, \ldots, e_{m-1})$, at
$$
\mathbf{P}(d) \;=\; \sum_{i=0}^{m-1} r_i \!\left( \cos\!\left(\tfrac{2\pi e_i}{n_i} + \varphi_i\right) - \cos\varphi_i,\; \sin\!\left(\tfrac{2\pi e_i}{n_i} + \varphi_i\right) - \sin\varphi_i,\; e_i \right) \;\in\; \mathbb{R}^3.
$$
The $z$-coordinate is $\Omega(d) = \sum_i e_i$. The unit $1$ sits at the origin on the spine.

**Properties.** (i) Each prime $i$'s shifted helix passes through the spine at $z = 0$; the cylinder is centered at $(-r_i \cos\varphi_i, -r_i \sin\varphi_i)$ and has radius $r_i$, placing the open $\Omega$-spine on its boundary as a tangent generator. (ii) Distinct prime cylinders share the spine-contact locus by construction; no pairwise-disjointness claim is made in the translated multi-prime form (cf. Part II / II.13 Note 9.3a.1). (iii) The mono-prime chain $\{1, p_i, p_i^2, \ldots, p_i^{a_i}\}$ traces prime $i$'s shifted helix. (iv) $\Omega$-grading is the open spine direction; cyclic structure is the $(x, y)$ direction; the helix family is the bundle relating them.

<!-- id: LEM-9.7a-HASSE-HELIX-CORRESPONDENCE | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 9.7 -->
**Lemma 9.7a (Hasse–Helix Correspondence, v2.7.13, preserved, unconditional).** Under the embedding of Definition 9.7, each Hasse cover edge $d \to d \cdot p_i$ in $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ is realized geometrically as the *translated one-step chord* of prime $i$'s shifted helix, with all other prime contributions held fixed. The cover advances $\Omega$ by exactly $1$ and rotates the $i$-th contribution around its offset cylinder by $2\pi / n_i$. The mono-prime chain $1 \to p_i \to p_i^2 \to \cdots \to p_i^{a_i}$ is the untranslated chord polygon on prime $i$'s shifted helix, identical up to translation to the chord polygon of $\Gamma_{n_i}$ in the canonical concentric form.

*Proof.* Fix $d = \prod_j p_j^{e_j}$ and consider the cover $d \to d \cdot p_i$. The exponent vector advances $e_i \to e_i + 1$ with all other $e_j$ unchanged. By Definition 9.7, $\mathbf{P}(d \cdot p_i) - \mathbf{P}(d)$ has $j$-component zero for $j \neq i$ and prime-$i$ component a one-step chord on prime $i$'s shifted helix, with $\Omega$ advanced by $1$. The mono-prime chain claim follows by iteration with $e_j = 0$ for $j \neq i$. $\square$

> *The Hasse diagram is not overlaid on the helix family; it is the helix family read as a graph.*

The multi-prime embedding provides $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ as a geometric object; the cyclotomic-order stratification of the single phasor at integer spin $s$ provides a *spectral-side* readout of the same lattice, where each gcd class $\{M \in \mathbb{N}_{>0} : \gcd(s, M) = d\}$ for $d \mid s$ corresponds to a divisor row of the embedded lattice, with stratum size $\varphi(s/d)$ matching the cyclotomic-order count at that row. The embedding and ALG-2 read the divisor lattice through two different instruments — geometric and spectral — and the divisor index $d$ is the common labeling. See Algorithm Note ALG-2 (Cyclotomic Stratification of the Single-Phasor Curve at Integer Spin).

The edges of the embedded lattice — divisibility relations $N_1 \mid N_2$ — carry not only existence but ratio. For $N_1 \mid N_2 \mid s$, the angular winding of the continuous-$N$ phasor along $N_1 \to N_2$ at fixed spin $s$ is $\Delta\theta = \pi\ell(r-1)$ with $r = N_2/N_1$ and $\ell = s/N_2$, recovering $r$ as $(\Delta\theta/\pi)/\ell + 1$. Combined with the combinatorial atomicity test on $r$ in $\mathrm{Div}(s)$, this identifies the Hasse cover edges of the embedded lattice. The three readouts now act on the same lattice in parallel: ALG-1 supplies vertices, ALG-2 supplies cyclotomic strata, ALG-3 supplies edge ratios. See Algorithm Note ALG-3 (Continuous-Phasor Winding Recovery of Divisor Lattice Ratios).

<!-- id: RMK-9.7e-ARITHMETIC-CRYSTAL | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 9.7, 9.7b, 9.7c, 9.7d -->
**Remark 9.7e (Arithmetic-Crystal Vocabulary, v2.7.13, preserved, interpretive).** The §9.7 embedding renders $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ as a *graded arithmetic crystal*: a finite poset realized in $\mathbb{R}^3$ with $\Omega$ as the stacking axis, $m$-fold rotational structure under canonical phasing, and apex defect $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(N)$ as a strain-like quantity whose squared-displacement form is energy-like. $\|A(N)\|^2$ has the form of a *dimensionless squared strain / energy-like defect*. The mechanical vocabulary is structurally faithful, not a claim of physical elasticity: there are no physical units, no constitutive law, and no stiffness tensor in the kernel object.

| profile | crystal vocabulary | structural meaning |
|---|---|---|
| constant multiplicity, closure | closed crystal | $\widehat{f}(-1) = 0$ via character orthogonality; chord skeleton on the $\Omega$-spine |
| asymmetric multiplicity, $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} > 0$ | strained crystal | $\widehat{f}(-1) \neq 0$; chord skeleton preserved but apex pulled off-axis |
| $T^3$ projection of strained crystal | crystal shadow | open-$\Omega$ direction collapsed; residual = apex defect under wrong basis |
| open-$\Omega$ embedding (Def 9.7) | crystal with rank restored | full strain visible as geometric quantity |

> *The multi-prime embedding renders $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ as a graded arithmetic crystal: constant multiplicity closes the crystal on the $\Omega$-spine, while apex defect measures multiplicity strain.*

**Note on crystallographic restriction (preserved).** For constant multiplicity profiles, the *unlabeled phase skeleton* has $m$-fold rotational symmetry around the spine. (Labeled divisor crystals carry distinct prime tags and are not literally rotation-invariant.) For $m \in \{1, 2, 3, 4, 6\}$ this matches the classical 2D crystallographic symmetries; for $m = 5, 7, \ldots$ the unlabeled skeleton sits in the *quasicrystalline* range. Whether this is coincidental or structural is open and worth flagging as a candidate connection between FVLT divisor lattices and quasicrystal phase structure. *Not a kernel claim; a candidate connection flagged for future work.*

<!-- id: RMK-9.7y-ROTATION-MULTIPLICATIVE-FAMILY | register: remark | status: unconditional (orbit/realization); interpretive (chiral-pair reading) | provenance: original | depends: 9.7, 9.7a, 9.7e, 9.7c.1, I.3(chi), gauge(§7/§II.12) -->


**Remark 9.7y (Rotation Orbit as a Multiplicative Integer Family, v3.1.23, new, unconditional).**
The cyclic group $C_m$ acts on the ordered exponent vector $(a_0, \ldots, a_{m-1})$ by seat
rotation. Re-evaluated against the **fixed** prime seats $p_0 < \cdots < p_{m-1}$, each distinct
orbit member is realized as an integer $\prod_i p_i^{a_{\rho(i)}}$, so the orbit is realized as a
family of $|C_m\text{-orbit}|$ integers sharing one abstract profile (multiset) — and the orbit size
may be a proper divisor of $m$ for a periodic vector, as the example below shows. Under §9.7 a cyclic relabel is
a rigid rotation of the embedded crystal about the $\Omega$-spine (Lemma 9.7a / Def 9.7 $m$-fold
structure); orbit members are the same crystal at $m$ rotational seats, and every rotation-invariant
readout — $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}$, the magnitude spectrum $\{|\widehat{f}(k)|\}$, the unlabeled
abstract Hasse graph — is constant on the orbit (the prime-labeled axes move).

This is the $C_m \leq S_m$ sub-action of the permutation gauge (§7 / §II.12 acts on prime-axis labels
at a fixed lens), made arithmetic: the gauge orbit is a concrete set of integers, not only a
relabeling. When the reverse is not cyclically equivalent (the necklace is chiral), reversal gives
the chiral partner (handedness flip $\chi$, Part I / I.3); the $C_m$-orbit and its reverse then form
a **chiral pair** of orbits sharing the magnitude spectrum with
$k \leftrightarrow -k$ exchanged (the dihedral-orientation action of the companion lock
`lock_record_2026-06-07_apex-defect-winding-orientation`). An achiral necklace (reverse a rotation of
itself) is its own partner.

**Example.** Doubled-$(4,2,1)$ atom, primes $\{2,3,5,7,11,13\}$, period $3 \Rightarrow$ orbit size $3$:

| exponent vector | $N$ | class |
|---|---|---|
| $(4,2,1,4,2,1)$ | $2{,}719{,}276{,}560$ | A |
| $(1,4,2,1,4,2)$ | $70{,}147{,}227{,}150$ | A |
| $(2,1,4,2,1,4)$ | $115{,}457{,}842{,}500$ | A |
| $(1,2,4,1,2,4)$ | $272{,}150{,}628{,}750$ | B (reverse) |
| $(2,4,1,2,4,1)$ | $15{,}108{,}633{,}540$ | B |
| $(4,1,2,4,1,2)$ | $5{,}356{,}150{,}800$ | B |

All six share profile $(4,4,2,2,1,1)$ and $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} = 0$ (Cor 9.7c.1).

**Scope (guard).** The orbit is the **cyclic-shift class** only. The full profile multiset
$(4,4,2,2,1,1)$ has $6!/2^3 = 90$ arrangements; the rotation is one orbit among them, not the profile
space.
<!-- id: DEF-9.7o-WINDING-ONLY-REVERSAL | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: 9.7 -->
**Definition 9.7o (Winding-Only Reversal, v3.1.8, new).** The *winding-only reversal* $W$ of the §9.7 embedding sends each divisor $d \mid N$ to
$$
\mathbf{P}_W(d) \;=\; \sum_{i=0}^{m-1} r_i \!\left( \cos\!\left(-\tfrac{2\pi e_i}{n_i} + \varphi_i\right) - \cos\varphi_i,\; \sin\!\left(-\tfrac{2\pi e_i}{n_i} + \varphi_i\right) - \sin\varphi_i,\; e_i \right) \;\in\; \mathbb{R}^3.
$$
That is, per-prime helical advancement direction flips ($2\pi e_i / n_i \mapsto -2\pi e_i / n_i$) while the azimuthal anchor $\varphi_i = 2\pi i/m$ is held fixed. The $\Omega$-coordinate is unchanged.

**Distinction from the kernel's three involutions** (Part I / I.3 closing paragraph). $W$ is *not* chirality $\chi$ at the geometric realization level: chirality flips the entire angular argument of the per-prime phasor (Part II / II.6 Def 4.4 parity), which under the embedding sends both winding and anchor through complex conjugation — geometrically a reflection $(x, y, z) \mapsto (x, -y, z)$, the realization of signed completion (Part I / I.3) on the embedding coordinates. $W$ flips only the winding part. It is also not Weyl $w$ (a representation-readout-level operation per §G companion-spine) or signed completion itself (an atlas-level operation per I.3). $W$ is an alternative embedding map, not a $\mathbb{Z}/2$ involution on $\mathcal{A}_\mathbb{Z}^\times$, and it is named here as a probe of §9.7 geometry rather than as a fourth involution alongside the three.

<!-- id: LEM-9.7o-TENSOR-SECTOR-SUPPORT | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 9.7, DEF-9.7o-WINDING-ONLY-REVERSAL -->
**Lemma 9.7o (Tensor-Sector Support of Winding-Only Reversal, v3.1.8, new, unconditional).** Let $N = \prod_{i=0}^{m-1} p_i^{a_i}$ with $m \geq 1$. The winding-only reversal $W$ acts as the identity on the §9.7 embedding of $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ if and only if $N$ is squarefree (all $a_i = 1$).

*Proof.* The per-prime helix of prime $p_i$ has $n_i = a_i + 1$ beads at angles $2\pi k / n_i$ for $k \in \{0, 1, \ldots, a_i\}$. Under $W$, the bead at exponent $e_i$ has angle $-2\pi e_i / n_i$ (anchor $\varphi_i$ unchanged across the comparison).

**($\Leftarrow$)** Suppose $N$ is squarefree, so $a_i = 1$ and $n_i = 2$ for every prime. Each per-prime helix has only two beads: $e_i = 0$ at angle $0$ (unchanged under $W$, since $-0 \equiv 0$) and $e_i = 1$ at angle $\pi$ (sent by $W$ to $-\pi \equiv \pi \pmod{2\pi}$). Both beads are bead-wise fixed; summing per-prime contributions, $\mathbf{P}_W(d) = \mathbf{P}(d)$ for every $d \mid N$.

**($\Rightarrow$)** Suppose $N$ is not squarefree, so $a_i \geq 2$ and $n_i \geq 3$ for at least one index $i_0$. On the $i_0$-th helix the bead at $e_{i_0} = 1$ has angle $2\pi / n_{i_0} \in (0, 2\pi/3]$, sent by $W$ to $-2\pi / n_{i_0}$. Since $0 < 4\pi / n_{i_0} \leq 4\pi/3 < 2\pi$, the angles $2\pi / n_{i_0}$ and $-2\pi / n_{i_0}$ are distinct modulo $2\pi$, so the two beads occupy distinct points on the $i_0$-th cylinder. The divisor $d_0 = p_{i_0}$ (exponent vector $e_i = \delta_{i, i_0}$) therefore has $\mathbf{P}_W(d_0) \neq \mathbf{P}(d_0)$. $\square$

**Three equivalent readings of the iff.** Cf. Part V / V.13 §8.7 spinor–tensor decomposition (Prop 8.36): $N$ is squarefree iff $\mu(N) \neq 0$ iff $N$ lies in the spinor sector. The Lemma states that $W$'s identity-support coincides exactly with the spinor sector; its action-support coincides exactly with the tensor sector.

> *Winding-only reversal is silent on the spinor sector and active on the tensor sector. Where chirality $\chi$ (signed completion at the geometric realization, Part I / I.3) acts on every atom — flipping helices and reflecting cylinder positions together — $W$ acts only where there is internal multiplicity structure to flip.*

**Mechanical reading.** $W$ on the divisor $(e_0, \ldots, e_{m-1})$ positions it where the canonical embedding would place a different exponent vector: for $e_i \geq 1$, the per-prime bead at $e_i$ goes to the bead at $n_i - e_i$; for $e_i = 0$, the bead stays. At squarefree axes ($n_i = 2$), $n_i - e_i = 1 = e_i$ when $e_i = 1$, so the per-prime permutation is the identity; at non-squarefree axes ($n_i \geq 3$), the per-prime permutation is non-trivial. The Lemma is the lattice-level statement of this per-prime bead-fixing fact.

---

## II.15 Apex defect and cancellation

**Source:** Def 9.7b, Lem 9.7c, Rmk 9.7d (v2.7.13).  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.15.  
**Status:** definitional / unconditional / interpretive (per-block).

<!-- id: DEF-9.7b-APEX-DEFECT | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: 9.7 -->
**Definition 9.7b (Apex Defect, v2.7.13, preserved).** Let $N$ have exponent-multiplicity profile $f: \mathbb{Z}/m \to \mathbb{C}$ defined by
$$
f(i) = \frac{e^{-2\pi i/n_i} - 1}{n_i}.
$$
Under the canonical prime phasing $\varphi_i = 2\pi i/m$ of Definition 9.7, the apex's complex displacement from the spine in the $(x, y)$ plane is
$$
A(N) \;=\; \sum_{i=0}^{m-1} f(i) \cdot e^{2\pi i \cdot i/m} \;=\; m \cdot \widehat{f}(-1).
$$
The **apex defect** of $N$ is
$$
\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(N) \;:=\; |A(N)|^2.
$$
Equivalently, $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(N)$ is the squared $L^2$-norm of the first phase-weighted Fourier coefficient of the exponent-multiplicity profile, and equivalently the squared distance of $\mathbf{P}(\mathrm{apex}(N))$ from the spine.

> *Apex defect is the Plancherel residual of multiplicity asymmetry under canonical prime phasing.*

> *Register note (see "How to read this document"): phase readouts here are roots-of-unity statements first. A displayed $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} = 0.000000$ is the decimal shadow of an exact cancellation ($\widehat{f}(-1) = 0$, Lem 9.7c); a near-integer such as $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(\mathrm{Sp}_6) = 1.9961\ldots$ is a cyclotomic value, not a decimal identity (Lem 9.7q).*

<!-- id: LEM-9.7c-CANCELLATION-LAW | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 9.7b -->
**Lemma 9.7c (Cancellation Law for Apex Defect, v2.7.13, preserved, unconditional).** The apex defect $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(N)$ vanishes exactly when $\widehat{f}(-1) = 0$. In particular, $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(N) = 0$ for every $N$ whose multiplicity profile is constant ($a_i \equiv k$ for some $k \geq 1$ and all $i \in \{0, \ldots, m-1\}$).

*Proof.* The first claim is immediate from $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(N) = |A(N)|^2 = m^2 |\widehat{f}(-1)|^2$. For the second, if $a_i \equiv k$, then $n_i \equiv k+1$ and $f(i) \equiv (e^{-2\pi i/(k+1)} - 1)/(k+1)$ is constant in $i$, call it $c$. Then $A(N) = c \sum_{i=0}^{m-1} e^{2\pi i \cdot i/m} = 0$ by orthogonality of the trivial character with the first nontrivial character on $\mathbb{Z}/m$ for $m \geq 2$. $\square$

**Verified examples (numerical, via the helix-lattice viewer instrument, preserved):**

| $N$ | profile | $m$ | $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}$ | reading |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| $30 = 2 \cdot 3 \cdot 5$ | $(1,1,1)$ | $3$ | $0.000000$ | constant / squarefree closure |
| $900 = 2^2 \cdot 3^2 \cdot 5^2$ | $(2,2,2)$ | $3$ | $0.000000$ | constant / non-squarefree closure |
| $1001 = 7 \cdot 11 \cdot 13$ | $(1,1,1)$ | $3$ | $0.000000$ | complement-cube closure |
| $30030 = 2 \cdot 3 \cdot 5 \cdot 7 \cdot 11 \cdot 13$ | $(1,1,1,1,1,1)$ | $6$ | $0.000000$ | squarefree six-prime closure |
| $720 = 2^4 \cdot 3^2 \cdot 5$ | $(4,2,1)$ | $3$ | $0.892582$ | asymmetric residual |
| $2520 = 2^3 \cdot 3^2 \cdot 5 \cdot 7$ | $(3,2,1,1)$ | $4$ | $1.141346$ | asymmetric residual |
| $720720 = 2^4 \cdot 3^2 \cdot 5 \cdot 7 \cdot 11 \cdot 13$ | $(4,2,1,1,1,1)$ | $6$ | $1.864204$ | full asymmetric residual |

**Discussion (preserved).** Squarefree $N$ is the canonical $k = 1$ case of constant-multiplicity closure. Other nonconstant profiles may vanish by Fourier cancellation accidents — specific arithmetic combinations of the $n_i$ values producing $\widehat{f}(-1) = 0$ even though $f$ is non-constant — but these are phase-profile coincidences rather than the structural closure family. The constant-profile family is the canonical structural source of closure; non-constant cancellation, when it occurs, requires separate verification.

<!-- id: NOTE-9.7x-UNIT-AS-SHARED-ORIGIN | register: note | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 9.7, 9.7h, 9.7a, 9.7b, 9.7c, II.2, II.8 -->
**Note 9.7x (The Unit as Shared Origin — Absence Given a Position, v3.1.21, new, interpretive).**

The `+1` in $n_i = a_i + 1$ is the identity state made visible. A prime-power fiber $p^a$ does not carry exponent set $\{1, \ldots, a\}$; it carries $\{0, 1, \ldots, a\}$ — $a{+}1$ states — because the prime can be *absent*, and absence is not nothing: it is the unit contribution $p^0 = 1$. The prime-2 fiber of $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ is thus a pentagon as the complete exponent cycle of $2^4$, whose closing vertex $e = 0$ is $2^0 = 1$. The product $\tau(N) = \prod_i (a_i + 1)$ then reads as the number of ways each prime can be present, *including not present*.

One object, two readings: the **Hasse reading** is the exponent chain ordered from absence to full multiplicity ($1 \to p \to \cdots \to p^a$); the **helix/phase reading** is the closed root-of-unity fiber $\zeta_{n_i}^{0}, \ldots, \zeta_{n_i}^{a_i}$, where $\zeta_{n_i}^{0} = 1$ is the vertex that closes the polygon.

$\mathrm{Div}(N)$ is a bounded graded lattice suspended between two extremal corners. The **bottom** $\bot$, $e = (0, \ldots, 0)$, $\prod_i p_i^0 = 1$: all fibers absent, the shared origin of every fiber, spine contact guaranteed (Def 9.7 properties). The **apex** $\top$, $e = (a_1, \ldots, a_m)$, $\prod_i p_i^{a_i} = N$: all fibers saturated, where spine contact is *not* guaranteed — return to the spine for a closed crystal, displacement (apex defect $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}$, Def 9.7b / Lemma 9.7c) for a strained one.

*The atlas is built by giving absence a position; the apex records what full presence does with that anchoring — closure or strain.*

The unit is therefore load-bearing in four registers at once: **arithmetic** — the multiplicative identity $1$; **order** — the bottom element $\bot$ of $\mathrm{Div}(N)$; **geometry** — the shared spine anchor where every zero-exponent fiber state coincides; **harmonic** — the trivial supercharacter channel $c_1 \equiv 1$ (Part II / II.2, II.8), the constant/DC baseline the rest of the spectrum is read against. The unit is the one object that is the same thing in every register — which is what an anchor is.

Consequence for the framework: dropping $1$ does not remove a trivial element; it removes the order-bottom, the origin of the exponent cube, the common seat of every fiber, and the spine contact that makes closure definable (closure just *is* the apex returning to where the unit sits). The proper/improper-divisor convention, legitimate elsewhere, is ill-fitting for this construction: FVLT begins where every prime is absent and the product is still present as identity.

<!-- id: RMK-9.7d-COMPRESSED-SHADOW | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 9.7, 9.7b -->
**Remark 9.7d (Compressed-Shadow Framing, v2.7.13, preserved, interpretive).** A pure-$T^3$ readout of $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ collapses the open $\Omega$-spine into cyclic closure: graded ascent compresses into the same toroidal coordinate as cyclic phase. Under that compression, multiplicity asymmetry remains present in the geometry but loses its hierarchical coordinate. What remains is a residual that cannot be absorbed into cyclic phase.

This residual was visible empirically in the February 2026 market-data viewer as a $12.5\%$ Hasse-residual term in chord-distance MDS over $22$ tickers structured by $\mathrm{Div}(N_0)$. Apex defect is the candidate right-basis diagnostic for this residual — open $\Omega$-spine with shift-and-rotate embedding, Definition 9.7 — where the residual has a name, a sign, and a vanishing condition. The identification with the February 2026 residual remains *diagnostic rather than theorem-level* until the normalization between chord-distance residual and $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}$ is fixed.

> *$T^3$ residual was not error. It was the compressed shadow of open-$\Omega$ multiplicity imbalance.*

The §9.7 readout decomposes cleanly into three first-class structural components: $T^3$ / cyclic (closure, seam, phase, cross-section); open $\Omega$ spine (hierarchy, rank, Hasse ascent, residual); helix chord skeleton (cover relation as geometry). These three are not independent visualizations of one object; they are the three structural components, simultaneously legible only in the multi-prime embedding of Definition 9.7.

<!-- id: COR-9.7c.1-PERIODIC-CANCELLATION | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 9.7b, 9.7c, 9.7e, 9.7p, 9.3a(register) -->


**Corollary 9.7c.1 (Periodic-Profile Cancellation, v3.1.23, new, unconditional).**
Let $N$ have multiplicity profile $a: \mathbb{Z}/m \to \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}$ and suppose $a$ is
periodic with a **proper** period $d \mid m$, $d < m$ (i.e. $a_{i+d} = a_i$ for all $i$). Then
$\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(N) = 0$.

*Proof.* $f(i) = (e^{-2\pi i/n_i}-1)/n_i$ inherits the period $d$, so $\widehat{f}(k) = 0$
unless $(m/d) \mid k$. Since $m \equiv 0$ and hence $m-1 \equiv -1 \pmod{m/d}$ with $m/d \geq 2$,
$(m/d) \nmid (m-1)$, so $\widehat{f}(-1) = 0$; apply Lemma 9.7c. $\square$

The constant-profile case of Lemma 9.7c is the $d = 1$ instance (every nonzero harmonic absent).
This carves the periodic profiles out of Lemma 9.7c's "other nonconstant profiles may vanish by
Fourier cancellation": for a **proper period** the vanishing is **structural**, not a phase-profile
accident — it is the same character-orthogonality on $\mathbb{Z}/m$ that closes the constant crystal.
In the roots-of-unity register (v3.1.18): the $m=6$, $d=3$ case factors as
$A(N) = \big(\sum_{i=0}^{2} f(b_i)\,\omega_6^{\,i}\big)(1 + \omega_6^{3}) = 0$ since $\omega_6^{3} = -1$
— the register note's own example $1 + \omega_6^3 = 0$.

> *A proper period in the multiplicity profile closes the crystal: the apex defect vanishes
> structurally, the constant profile being the period-1 case.*

**Coarseness complement (interpretive).** $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}$ reads only $\widehat{f}(\pm 1)$
(Def 9.7b); a proper-period-$d$ profile carries its content on harmonics that are multiples of
$m/d$ (for $m=6, d=3$: $k = 2, 4$). The apex scalar is therefore structurally **blind** to a
period-$d$ crystal — a period-doubling blind spot, distinct from the tensor-sector coarseness of
Rmk 9.7p but of a piece with it: the scalar samples one harmonic, and a periodic crystal displaces
its content away from it. The embedded figure (Def 9.7) is not closed in the colloquial sense — it
rotates — but the first-harmonic readout cannot register the turn.

**Canonical example (numerical, helix-lattice viewer).** $N = 2{,}719{,}276{,}560 = 2^4 \cdot 3^2
\cdot 5 \cdot 7^4 \cdot 11^2 \cdot 13$, exponent vector $(4,2,1,4,2,1)$, profile $(4,4,2,2,1,1)$,
$m = 6$, period $d = 3$: $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} = 0$ (positive/winding/global all $0$), with live
spectrum at $k = 2$ ($1.889531$) and $k = 4$ ($1.200390$).
<!-- id: RMK-9.7p-APEX-DEFECT-COARSENESS | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 9.7b, 9.7c, 9.7e, DEF-9.7o-WINDING-ONLY-REVERSAL, 8.7 -->
**Remark 9.7p (Apex-Defect Coarseness Relative to Tensor-Sector Support, v3.1.8, new, interpretive).** The §9.7 closure/strain partition (Rmk 9.7e, Lem 9.7c) is a *geometric* readout of multiplicity structure via $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}$; the §V.13 §8.7 spinor/tensor decomposition is an *algebraic* readout of the same multiplicity structure via Möbius silence. Together with Lemma 9.7o (Part II / II.14), they form a three-cell structure on $\mathcal{A}_\mathbb{N}$ at $m \geq 2$:

| | Closed ($\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} = 0$) | Strained ($\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} > 0$) |
|---|---|---|
| **Spinor** (squarefree, $\mu \neq 0$) | $\mathrm{Div}(30)$, $\mathrm{Div}(210)$, $\mathrm{Div}(2310)$, $\mathrm{Div}(30030)$, $\mathrm{Div}(1001)$, $\ldots$ | *empty* |
| **Tensor** (non-squarefree, $\mu = 0$) | $\mathrm{Div}(900)$ profile $(2,2,2)$, $\mathrm{Div}(216)$ profile $(3,3)$, $\mathrm{Div}(44100)$ profile $(2,2,2,2)$, $\ldots$ | $\mathrm{Div}(720)$, $\mathrm{Div}(360)$, $\mathrm{Div}(12)$, $\mathrm{Div}(2520)$, $\ldots$ |

The spinor + strained cell is empty by Lem 9.7c: squarefree $\implies$ constant profile $(1, 1, \ldots, 1) \implies \widehat{f}(-1) = 0$ by character orthogonality at $m \geq 2$. The other three cells are populated. The *single-axis* case ($m = 1$, Lem 9.7c outside its scope) is a separate viewer category and not part of this grid.

**Lemma 9.7o resolves the tensor + closed cell that $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}$ cannot.** $W$ acts non-trivially on every tensor-sector atom (Lem 9.7o), so $W$-action distinguishes spinor + closed from tensor + closed by acting on the latter and not the former. $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}$, by contrast, agrees on the two cells: both have $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} = 0$. Stated geometrically: *winding-only reversal is a strictly finer closure-detector than the apex-defect scalar.* The apex-defect scalar samples one Fourier mode of the multiplicity profile; $W$-action probes the full embedding.

**Canonical witness.** $N = 900 = 2^2 \cdot 3^2 \cdot 5^2$, profile $(2, 2, 2)$:
- $\mu(900) = 0$ — tensor sector;
- $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(900) = 0$ — closed by Lem 9.7c (constant profile);
- $W$ moves **24 of 27 divisors** — non-identity by Lem 9.7o ($n_i = 3$ for every prime, so each helix has three beads with $W$ swapping $e_i = 1 \leftrightarrow e_i = 2$).

The three fixed divisors are the diagonal constant-exponent divisors with exponent vectors $(0,0,0)$, $(1,1,1)$, and $(2,2,2)$ — divisor values $1$, $30$, $900$. The nonzero fixed cases are not per-prime fixed beads: on each $n_i = 3$ helix, $W$ swaps $e_i = 1 \leftrightarrow e_i = 2$. They are fixed only after summing the three equal-profile prime contributions, by the same rotational cancellation that makes the profile closed (Lem 9.7c).

**Reading.** The kernel's existing geometric readout ($\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}$) and algebraic readout (Möbius) for the multiplicity boundary disagree at one cell. $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}$ closes the boundary along the closure axis by character-orthogonality cancellation; the cancellation happens algebraically inside the Fourier coefficient and erases the cell's distinguishability at the scalar level. $W$-action sees what the scalar averages away — it reads multiplicity from the per-prime bead-permutation side rather than from the Fourier-sum side. The two readouts are complementary, and Lemma 9.7o makes that complementarity precise.

> *The scalar $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}$ is a detector, not the operation it samples. Winding-only reversal supplies the geometric operation whose support is the tensor sector; the apex defect supplies the closure scalar whose zero set includes the tensor-closed sub-cell.*

<!-- id: RMK-9.7z-CHI-W-COINCIDENCE | register: remark | status: unconditional (iff; four-state collapse) + interpretive (coupling / closure-crosscut framing) | provenance: original | depends: 9.7, DEF-9.7o-WINDING-ONLY-REVERSAL, 9.7p, 9.7c, 9.7c.1, 9.7y, I.3(chi) -->
**Remark 9.7z (Chirality–Winding Coincidence on the §9.7 Embedding, v3.1.24, new, unconditional).**

In the §9.7 embedding, the winding-only reversal W (Def 9.7o) and the chirality reflection χ
(Part I / I.3, (x,y,z) ↦ (x,−y,z)) induce the **same unlabeled enantiomorph — equivalently, the
four orientation states (W,χ) ∈ {±1}² collapse to two embedded point-multisets — if and only if
the exponent profile is κ-palindromic**, where κ(s) = (−s) mod m is the azimuth seat-reflection in
the canonical ascending-prime seat order (n_{κ(s)} = n_s for all s).

W ≠ χ as labeled maps (Def 9.7o): χ is full conjugation of the phase plane, W flips winding only,
and they move different node sets. The coincidence is at the **unlabeled point-multiset** level.
Sufficiency: under κ-palindromy the seat-reflection coordinate permutation σ_κ (σ_κ e)_t = e_{κ(t)}
satisfies Z(σ_κ e; W=−1) = conj Z(e; W=+1) and preserves Ω, so the W-image equals the χ-image;
hence Wχ is the internal symmetry, and V₄ = ⟨W,χ⟩ acts on the figure through a ℤ/2 quotient with
kernel ⟨Wχ⟩ (crystal ↔ enantiomorph). Necessity: restrict to the Ω=1 shell, where per-seat beads
have magnitude ρ(n) = 2 sin(π/n)/n = (2/π) g(π/n) with g(x) = x sin x, g′ > 0 on (0,π/2], so ρ is
strictly decreasing hence injective; the shell identity then decomposes order-by-order and forces
κ-invariance of the profile.

**Closure-crosscut (governing axis is κ-palindromy, not closure).** κ-palindromy holds for strained
κ-palindromic profiles (δ_apex > 0) and fails for non-κ-palindromic closed profiles, so the
coincidence is not cell-confined. Its bearing on the closure/strain partition (Rmk 9.7e, Lem 9.7c,
Cor 9.7c.1) is the κ-palindromic ∩ closed slice: there the body retains chirality even though
δ_apex = 0 — **apex closure does not imply achirality**. This sharpens Rmk 9.7p: δ_apex = |Â(−1)|²,
and |·|² discards the argument that carries the handedness sign — the same chirality-blindness
Rmk 9.7y records when a chiral pair shares the magnitude spectrum with k ↔ −k exchanged. One
principle: magnitude-spectrum readouts are chirality-blind by construction; the embedded figure
retains the handedness; W and χ toggle it.

**Guards.**
(i) W is a §9.7 embedding probe, not one of the kernel's three ℤ/2 involutions (Def 9.7o closing
    paragraph). This does not advance the deferred χ / w / signed-completion coupling program.
(ii) κ (azimuth reflection s ↦ −s mod m, fixes seat 0) is **not** Rmk 9.7y's necklace reversal
    (s ↦ m−1−s). They agree on constant profiles and differ generally (m=6: κ asks a₁=a₅, a₂=a₄;
    reversal asks a₀=a₅, a₁=a₄, a₂=a₃). Do not conflate.
(iii) κ-palindromy is seat-order-relative (canonical ascending-prime order), not an intrinsic
    prime-label symmetry; it imports no squarefree-automorphism symmetry (Lemma I.5.3 firewall).
(iv) All statements concern the unlabeled embedded point-multiset; divisors keep their prime tags.

**Witnesses.** Constant (2,2,2,2,2,2) = Div(30030²): closed, κ-palindromic, coincidence holds;
fixed locus = reflection plane Im Z = 0 (141 nodes), spine x=y=0 (93 nodes), unit and apex the two
guaranteed anchors. Div(900) profile (2,2,2) is the three-prime instance (Rmk 9.7p witness).
Counter-witnesses (closed, not κ-palindromic, coincidence fails): (4,2,1,4,2,1) [the Cor 9.7c.1
doubled atom], (3,1,2,3,1,2). Cross-witness (strained, κ-palindromic, coincidence holds):
(2,1,0,2,0,1), δ_apex = 1.

**Status.** iff and four→two collapse: unconditional (sufficiency by σ_κ; necessity by the Ω=1
shell, two independent verification routes). Coupling / closure-crosscut framing: interpretive.
Lock: `lock_record_2026-06-09_chi-W-coincidence` (proof + Route A bijection + Route B multiset).

**Kill condition.** A κ-palindromic profile with W-image ≠ χ-image; a non-κ-palindromic profile
with equality; or a flaw in the σ_κ bijection / Ω=1 injectivity step.


<!-- id: LEM-9.7q-CYCLOTOMIC-BOUND | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 9.7b -->
**Lemma 9.7q (Cyclotomic Field-of-Definition Bound for Apex Defect, v3.1.16, new, unconditional).** For any §9.7 atom $N = \prod_{i=0}^{m-1} p_i^{a_i}$ with exponent profile $(a_0, \ldots, a_{m-1})$ and polygon orders $n_k = a_k + 1$, the apex defect $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(N) = |A(N)|^2$ from Def 9.7b satisfies

$$\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(N) \;\in\; \mathbb{Q}(\zeta_L) \cap \mathbb{R}, \qquad L = \mathrm{lcm}(m,\, n_0,\, n_1,\, \ldots,\, n_{m-1}).$$

$L$ is the **ambient conductor bound** for $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(N)$, not necessarily its minimal cyclotomic conductor; the actual conductor of $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(N)$ may be a proper divisor of $L$.

*Proof.* $\omega = e^{2\pi i/m} \in \mathbb{Q}(\zeta_m)$. Each $f(k) = (e^{-2\pi i/n_k} - 1)/n_k \in \mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{n_k})$. Both are contained in $\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_L)$, hence $A(N) = \sum_k f(k) \omega^k \in \mathbb{Q}(\zeta_L)$. Complex conjugation acts on $\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_L)$ as $\zeta_L \mapsto \zeta_L^{-1}$, so $\bar{A}(N) \in \mathbb{Q}(\zeta_L)$, and $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(N) = A(N) \cdot \bar{A}(N) \in \mathbb{Q}(\zeta_L)$. Real-valuedness then descends $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(N)$ to $\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_L) \cap \mathbb{R}$. $\blacksquare$

**Worked instance: SP6# ($\mathrm{Sp}_6$).** For $N = \mathrm{Sp}_6 = 2^6 \cdot 3^5 \cdot 5^4 \cdot 7^3 \cdot 11^2 \cdot 13$, profile $(6,5,4,3,2,1)$, polygon orders $n_k = (7,6,5,4,3,2)$, $m = 6$:

$$L(\mathrm{Sp}_6) \;=\; \mathrm{lcm}(6, 7, 6, 5, 4, 3, 2) \;=\; 420.$$

The closed form (verified at 60 dps in `sp6_apex_defect.py`) is

$$\mathrm{Re}\,A = -\frac{1}{6} - \frac{2\sin^2(\pi/7)}{7} + \frac{2\sin(\pi/5)\cos(\pi/30)}{5}$$
$$\mathrm{Im}\,A = \frac{1+3\sqrt{3}}{4} - \frac{\sin(2\pi/7)}{7} - \frac{2\sin(\pi/5)\sin(\pi/30)}{5}$$
$$\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(\mathrm{Sp}_6) \;=\; (\mathrm{Re}\,A)^2 + (\mathrm{Im}\,A)^2 \;=\; 1.99610291533920158\ldots$$

Each summand's individual conductor divides 420: $\sqrt{3} \in \mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{12})$, $\sin(\pi/5), \cos(\pi/30), \sin(\pi/30) \in \mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{60})$, $\sin^2(\pi/7) \in \mathbb{Q}(\zeta_7)$, $\sin(2\pi/7) \in \mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{28})$. The closeness $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(\mathrm{Sp}_6) \approx 2$ (gap $\approx 3.9 \times 10^{-3}$) is decimal coincidence in this specific cyclotomic combination, not an algebraic identity.

**Staircase ambient-bound table.** For superprimorial staircase profiles $\mathrm{Sp}_n$ (Rmk 9.7l):

| $n$ | polygon-order multiset | $L(\mathrm{Sp}_n)$ |
|---|---|---:|
| 1 | $\{2\}$ | $2$ |
| 2 | $\{2,3\}$ | $6$ |
| 3 | $\{2,3,4\}$ | $12$ |
| 4 | $\{2,3,4,5\}$ | $60$ |
| 5 | $\{2,3,4,5,6\}$ | $60$ |
| 6 | $\{2,3,4,5,6,7\}$ | $420$ |
| 7 | $\{2,3,4,5,6,7,8\}$ | $840$ |
| 8 | $\{2,3,4,5,6,7,8,9\}$ | $2520$ |

**Generalization (bounded).** Any §9.7 invariant of $N$ constructed by rational operations from the vertex coordinates $\mathbf{P}(d)$, the apex displacement $A(N)$, and the polygon-order roots of unity $\omega, e^{2\pi i/n_k}$ lies in $\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_L)$ for $L = \mathrm{lcm}(m, n_0, \ldots, n_{m-1})$. Metric invariants — edge lengths, perimeter, area — may require $\sqrt{\cdot}$ extractions and therefore live in conductor-enlarged fields; the same $L$ bound does not transport automatically to these.

> *The apex defect of a §9.7 atom lies in the real subfield of $\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_L)$, where $L = \mathrm{lcm}(m, n_0, \ldots, n_{m-1})$ — the lcm of the azimuthal order $m$ and the polygon orders $n_k$. The minimal conductor may be a proper divisor of $L$. Metric invariants may enlarge the conductor through $\sqrt{\cdot}$; they do not all share this exact ambient bound.*

<!-- id: RMK-9.7r-CASUS-IRREDUCIBILIS-THRESHOLD | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 9.7b, 9.7l, 9.7q -->
**Remark 9.7r (Casus-Irreducibilis Threshold at $\mathrm{Sp}_6$, v3.1.16, new, interpretive).** The perimeter formula $P(N) = 2\sum_i \sin(\pi/n_i)$ (derived 2026-05-28 session, candidate kernel-additive) lives in $\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{L_P}) \cap \mathbb{R}$ where $L_P = \mathrm{lcm}_i(c_i)$ and $c_i$ is the cyclotomic conductor of $\sin(\pi/n_i)$:
- $c_i = 1$ if $n_i \in \{1, 2, 6\}$ (the sine is rational: $0, 1, 1/2$)
- $c_i = 12$ if $n_i = 3$ ($\sqrt{3}/2$)
- $c_i = 8$ if $n_i = 4$ ($\sqrt{2}/2$)
- $c_i = 4 n_i$ for odd $n_i \geq 5$ (e.g., $c_i = 20$ at $n_i = 5$, $c_i = 28$ at $n_i = 7$)
- $c_i = 2 n_i$ for even $n_i \geq 8$ in general (case-by-case)

The exact ambient conductors $L$ (apex defect, Lemma 9.7q) and $L_P$ (perimeter) **differ in general**. At SP6#: $L = 420$ but $L_P = \mathrm{lcm}(1, 12, 8, 20, 1, 28) = 840$, because $\sin(\pi/4) = \sqrt{2}/2$ requires $\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_8)$ and $8 \nmid 420$.

**General prime-support coincidence is false.** The apex bound $L = \mathrm{lcm}(m, n_0, \ldots, n_{m-1})$ contains the azimuthal order $m$, while the perimeter formula depends only on the polygon orders $n_k$ and some polygon orders contribute rational perimeter terms ($n_k \in \{1, 2, 6\}$). Counterexample: for squarefree $N = p_1 p_2 \cdots p_7$ with $m = 7$ and all $n_k = 2$, the apex ambient bound is $L = \mathrm{lcm}(7, 2) = 14$ — prime support $\{2, 7\}$ — but the perimeter is $P(N) = 2 \cdot 7 \cdot \sin(\pi/2) = 14 \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $L_P = 1$ and prime support $\varnothing$. The two prime supports do not coincide.

**Staircase prime-support threshold.** For staircase profiles $\mathrm{Sp}_n$, however, the casus-irreducibilis threshold for the two invariants does occur at the same atom. The polygon-order multiset is $\{2, \ldots, n+1\}$ (Rmk 9.7l), so prime $7$ first enters the apex ambient bound $L(\mathrm{Sp}_n)$ and the perimeter field $L_P(\mathrm{Sp}_n)$ at $\mathrm{Sp}_6$. $\mathrm{Sp}_6$ is therefore the first staircase whose polygon-order support forces the real $7$-cyclotomic subfield into the paired apex/perimeter discussion. Equivalently, the real $7$-cyclotomic subfield appears, for example through the degree-three generator $2\cos(2\pi/7)$, which satisfies

$$x^3 + x^2 - 2x - 1 \;=\; 0.$$

The SP6# apex closed form additionally contains conductor-$28$ sine terms such as $\sin(2\pi/7)$, beyond the conductor-$7$ content reachable via $\cos(2\pi/7)$.

> *The general prime-support coincidence between apex and perimeter ambient bounds is false. For the staircase family specifically, the casus-irreducibilis threshold — the smallest $\mathrm{Sp}_n$ where prime $7$ first enters both ambient bounds — is $\mathrm{Sp}_6$.*

## II.16 CRT fibered presentation

**Source:** Rmk 9.7h (v2.7.14); Rmk 9.7f general-congruence half (v2.7.13).  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.16.  
**Status:** interpretive / unconditional (per-block).

<!-- id: RMK-9.7h-CRT-FIBERED-PRESENTATION | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 2.2e, 9.7, 9.7a, 9.7f -->
**Remark 9.7h (CRT Fibered Presentation of the Multi-Prime Embedding, v2.7.14, preserved).** Under the §9.7 embedding, $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ is rendered as a Minkowski sum of prime-power fibers. For $N = \prod_i p_i^{a_i}$, the $i$-th prime contributes a regular $n_i$-bead polygonal fiber, where $n_i = a_i + 1$, traced by the exponent coordinate $e_i \in \{0, \ldots, a_i\}$. Choosing any coprime partition of the prime axes as base axes and fiber axes (in the sense of Part I / I.8 Rmk 2.2e), the fiber axes present as translated polygonal fibers over the base. Thus the product decomposition
$$
\mathrm{Div}(N) \;\cong\; \prod_i \mathrm{Div}(p_i^{a_i})
$$
becomes visible as fibered polygon geometry.

For $N = 720 = 2^4 \cdot 3^2 \cdot 5$, the prime-$2$ fiber is a pentagon (the regular $5$-gon traced by $e_2 \in \{0, 1, 2, 3, 4\}$ on cylinder radius $r_2 = 1/5$), translated over the $3 \times 2$ base supplied by primes $3$ and $5$, giving $5 \cdot 3 \cdot 2 = 30$ divisor positions.

> *The §9.7 embedding makes the divisor product structure geometrically explicit: each prime-power factor appears as a translated regular polygon, and the divisor lattice is the Minkowski sum of these fibers. Coprime joins (Part I / I.8 Rmk 2.2e) correspond to fibered restrictions of this geometry.*

This is the geometric face of the CRT/product split already used by the harmonic register; Garcia-mode CRT decomposition operates on the same factorization algebraically, but no Garcia-specific machinery is assumed here.

<!-- id: RMK-9.7f-BASIS-COMPLEMENT-CONGRUENCE | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 2.2e, 9.7, 9.7b -->
**Remark 9.7f (Basis/Complement Crystal Congruence and Joined Objects, v2.7.13, general-congruence half preserved here).**

**Boolean cube congruence.** Two integers with identical multiplicity profile and identical $m$ produce identical embedded crystals under Definition 9.7: the embedding formula sees primes only through $n_i = a_i + 1$ and through their canonical-ordering index $i$ for phase. The general statement is that any two integers $N_1$ and $N_2$ with the same multiplicity profile and the same number of primes $m$ embed as the same crystal, indistinguishable except through prime labeling.

**Joined objects.** When $\gcd(N_1, N_2) = 1$, Rmk 2.2e (Part I / I.8) gives the order-isomorphism $\mathrm{Div}(N_1 N_2) \cong \mathrm{Div}(N_1) \times \mathrm{Div}(N_2)$ as graded posets. Read geometrically under Definition 9.7 with $m = m_1 + m_2$ primes, the embedded figure of $\mathrm{Div}(N_1 N_2)$ is the *Minkowski sum* of two sub-figures: one carrying $N_1$'s multiplicity profile on the first $m_1$ phase positions, one carrying $N_2$'s profile on the remaining $m_2$ positions. Hasse cover edges separate cleanly by side: covers within basis primes and covers within complement primes never mix.

The 720/1001 instantiation — basis cube over $\{2, 3, 5\}$, complement cube over $\{7, 11, 13\}$, joined Minkowski sum in $720720 = 720 \cdot 1001$, and strain non-additivity $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(720720) = 1.864204 \neq 0.892582 + 0$ — lives at Part V / V.4.

## II.17 Crystal-graph readout

**Source:** Rmk 9.7j (v2.7.14); §G companion-spine routing pointer.  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.17.  
**Status:** unconditional / interpretive (per-block).

<!-- id: RMK-9.7j-CRYSTAL-GRAPH-IDENTIFICATION | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 2.2a.1, 9.7, 9.7a, 9.7h -->
**Remark 9.7j (Crystal-Graph Identification of $\mathrm{Div}(N)$, v2.7.14, preserved).** For $N = \prod_{i=1}^{m} p_i^{a_i}$, the divisor lattice $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ carries the structure of the **colored product crystal** of the tensor product
$$
V_{a_1/2} \otimes V_{a_2/2} \otimes \cdots \otimes V_{a_m/2}
$$
under the commuting action of $\mathfrak{sl}_2^{\oplus m}$ (the Levi subalgebra with one $\mathfrak{sl}_2$ factor per prime). Each prime contributes:

- a *crystal factor*: the length-$(a_i + 1)$ chain $|{-a_i/2}\rangle, |{-a_i/2 + 1}\rangle, \ldots, |a_i/2\rangle$, the crystal basis of the spin-$a_i/2$ irreducible representation of $\mathfrak{sl}_2$;
- a *weight identification*: the exponent $e_i \in \{0, 1, \ldots, a_i\}$ corresponds to the weight $m_i = e_i - a_i/2$;
- a pair of *Kashiwara operators* $\tilde{e}_{p_i}, \tilde{f}_{p_i}$ acting along the $i$-th weight string. Multiplication by $p_i$ is the coefficient-free raising operator $\tilde{e}_{p_i}$; division by $p_i$ is the coefficient-free lowering operator $\tilde{f}_{p_i}$.

The total $\Omega$-sum $\Omega(d) = \sum_i e_i(d)$ corresponds to the total weight $M(d) = \Omega(d) - \Omega_{\max}/2$. Divisor $1$ is the lowest-weight state, divisor $N$ is the highest-weight state, $\Omega$-slices are fixed-$M$ subspaces.

> *Each prime contributes a Kashiwara crystal chain of length $a_i + 1$; the divisor lattice is the colored product of these chains, with primes labeling commuting Kashiwara operator families.*

**No-deep-Kashiwara caveat (preserved).** This identification uses Kashiwara's framework (crystal bases, $\tilde{e}_i/\tilde{f}_i$ operators, crystal graphs, weight functions) only in the trivial-tensor-product case where the $m$ $\mathfrak{sl}_2$ actions commute. The non-trivial Kashiwara tensor-product rule (signature-sequence algorithm for diagonal $\mathfrak{sl}_2$-action) is **not** invoked, because the product structure of $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ corresponds to $\mathfrak{sl}_2^{\oplus m}$ acting by commuting factors, not to a single diagonal $\mathfrak{sl}_2$.

**Finite Hasse Hilbert realization.** $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ realizes $H_{\mathrm{Hasse}}(N)=\ell^2(\mathrm{Div}(N)) \cong \bigotimes_i \mathbb{C}^{a_i+1}$ with divisor $d = \prod_i p_i^{e_i}$ corresponding to the exponent-vector basis state $|e_1, \ldots, e_m\rangle$. Each prime $p_i$ contributes one bounded finite representation factor of spin $j_i = a_i/2$ when the optional $\mathfrak{sl}_2$ weighting is declared; the cap $0 \leq e_i \leq a_i$ is finite-dimensional. The same finite sector therefore carries three active addresses for each state: divisor (arithmetic), spin/representation weight (Hilbert/representation), and crystal vertex (combinatorial/operator). It is not a Fock space unless a future project supplies a genuine one-particle space and symmetric-algebra construction.

**Four-register square.** The crystal-register reading parallels Rmk 2.2a.1's difference/slope duality (Part IV / IV.6): coefficient-free Kashiwara moves $\tilde{e}_i, \tilde{f}_i$ play, in the representation register, the same structural role that forward differences play in the Newton register. Passing to $J_\pm$ or $U_q(\mathfrak{sl}_2)$ operator coefficients is a weighted lift, just as passing from Newton differences to standard polynomial coefficients introduces factorial-denominator basis-change costs. Both registers are *fraction-free* at the integer-step level; both pick up weights or denominators only when lifted to a continuous or weighted formulation. This closes a four-register square — Newton/difference, Taylor/slope, Kashiwara/crystal, Hilbert/spin — with coefficient-free native step and weighted lift occupying the same structural position in each pair.

*Status: Unconditional for the technical correspondence between $\mathrm{Div}(N)$'s product structure and the product crystal of $\mathfrak{sl}_2^{\oplus m}$. Interpretive for the framework-bridge: Kashiwara is cited as math-address for an existing structure, not as a source of theorems $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ inherits. No result currently displayed by §9.7 or the helix-lattice trainer depends on a Kashiwara theorem beyond the framework's defining axioms.*

### §G companion-spine routing note (terse, per Robin's File 1 verdict 1)

The §G companion-spine body (Phase 1.a complete, May 2026) carries the formal representation-readout content: $q$-deformation via $U_q(\mathfrak{sl}_2)$, Weyl reflection $w$ declared distinct from §C chirality $\chi$, and the candidate Weyl-reflection-via-$R$-matrix route for §E.4 phase-transport resolution. The §G body remains routed to companion-spine in v3.0; its core finite representation content is covered by Part II / II.17 (Rmk 9.7j crystal-graph) and Part V / V.13 (§8.7 spinor-tensor 720-specific use). The old Part II / II.22 Fock branch is preserved below only as non-normative research/history. Body inclusion is post-v3.0 scope, contingent on Chris register-discipline verdict.

## II.18 Local-comb and position-awareness invariants

**Source:** Rmk 9.7k (v2.7.14); Note 9.7m invariant-check half (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.18.  
**Status:** unconditional / interpretive (per-block).

<!-- id: RMK-9.7k-LOCAL-COMB-INVARIANT | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 9.7, 9.7h, 9.7j -->
**Remark 9.7k (Local-Comb Cardinality Invariant, v2.7.14, preserved, unconditional).** Let $d \in \mathrm{Div}(N)$ with $N = \prod_{i=1}^{m} p_i^{a_i}$. The *local comb* at $d$ is the union of the $m$ prime strands through $d$:
$$
\mathrm{comb}(d) \;=\; \bigcup_{i=1}^{m} \left\{\, d \cdot p_i^{k - e_i(d)} \;:\; 0 \leq k \leq a_i \,\right\}
$$
where $e_i(d)$ is the $i$-th exponent of $d$. Each strand contributes $a_i + 1$ members; the selected divisor $d$ is shared by all $m$ strands, contributing $m - 1$ redundant counts. Hence:
$$
|\mathrm{comb}(d)| \;=\; \sum_{i=1}^{m}(a_i + 1) - (m - 1) \;=\; 1 + \sum_{i=1}^{m} a_i \;=\; 1 + \Omega_{\max}(N).
$$

The local-comb cardinality is **constant across selection**: every divisor in $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ has a local comb of size $1 + \Omega_{\max}$, with only the comb's *orientation* (which strand position $d$ occupies on each strand) varying by selected divisor.

> *Every divisor in $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ has the same local prime-action neighborhood cardinality, $1 + \Omega_{\max}$. The selection determines orientation, not size.*

The multiset of strand orders $\{a_i + 1\}_{i=1}^{m}$ — the polygon-order multiset of Rmk 9.7h (Part II / II.16) — is likewise invariant under selection. Selection changes which vertex of each polygon is the shared anchor, not which polygons appear.

In the crystal-graph language of Rmk 9.7j (Part II / II.17), the local comb is the orbit-closure of $d$ under the union of single-prime Kashiwara operator subgroups:
$$
\mathrm{comb}(d) \;=\; \bigcup_{i=1}^{m} \langle \tilde{e}_{p_i},\, \tilde{f}_{p_i} \rangle \cdot d.
$$
Its cardinality $1 + \Omega_{\max}$ is the size of the single-factor-orbit closure for any basis vertex of the colored product crystal — a property of the product structure independent of the chosen vertex.

<!-- id: NOTE-9.7m-POSITION-AWARENESS | register: note | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 9.7, 9.7h, 9.7j, 9.7k, 9.7l -->
**Note 9.7m (Position-Awareness as Anti-Hallucination Property, v2.7.14, preserved — invariant-check half).** The structural facts of Rmk 9.7h (polygon-order multiset, Part II / II.16), Rmk 9.7k (constant local-comb cardinality $1 + \Omega_{\max}$, above), and Rmk 9.7l (characteristic signatures for distinguished families, Part III / III.7) together supply *position-awareness*: a computational or interpretive agent situated at a divisor $d \in \mathrm{Div}(N)$ can verify its claimed location against known local invariants.

Three locally-checkable invariants are available for any $d \in \mathrm{Div}(N)$:

- **Cardinality**: $|\mathrm{comb}(d)| = 1 + \Omega_{\max}$, identical for every selection in the same lattice.
- **Polygon-order multiset**: $\{a_i + 1\}_{i=1}^{m}$, identical for every selection in the same lattice — a lattice-level signature.
- **Strand-position vector**: $(e_i(d))_{i=1}^{m}$, distinctive to $d$ — locating $d$ uniquely within the polygon assembly.

A system that claims to be at divisor $d$ but reports a local neighborhood inconsistent with these invariants has demonstrably misidentified its position. The first two invariants check that the system is in the correct lattice; the third checks that it is at the correct divisor within that lattice. Each check is $O(m)$ in the number of primes, not $O(\tau(N))$ — local-comb cardinality, polygon-order multiset, and strand-position vector are all readable from atom-local data.

The *anti-hallucination application* of this invariant check — proactive position-verification by AI agents operating on FVLT-substrate data — lives at Part VI / VI.13.

<!-- id: NOTE-C3eII1-PRIME-RANK-REGISTER | register: note | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 9.7l, K.2 -->
**Note C3e-II.1 (The Prime-Rank Register, v3.1.4, new).** The *profile register* records the unordered exponent multiset $\{a_i\}$ — what a single unweighted divisor-lattice shape delivers, invariant under permutation of which prime carries which exponent, since $G_N \cong P_{a_1+1} \,\square\, \cdots \,\square\, P_{a_m+1}$ and Cartesian product is commutative (C3e; Thm K.2). The *prime-identity register* records the full labeled exponent assignment — $N$ itself — equivalently prime-rank together with *prime-support*, the bare name-set $\{p_1, \ldots, p_m\}$ (cf. Rmk 9.7l, Part III / III.7). Between them lies the *prime-rank register*: exponent behavior against prime-size order $p_1 < p_2 < \cdots$, retaining rank position without requiring transport of named prime identity. C3e rules out recovering prime identity from a single unweighted lattice shape; it does not rule out aggregate rank effects. In random-integer ensembles, $\mathbb{E}[v_p(n)] = 1/(p-1)$, so exponent mass decays with prime size. In extremal families — highly composite numbers and the superprimorial staircase — this pressure appears as non-increasing exponents on initial prime segments, the anti-correlation rule already used in Rmk 9.7l's scope-of-characterization. Prime-rank and prime-support are independent refinements of profile, with prime-identity their join: a diagnostic sensitive to descending exponent structure should be audited as a rank-register readout, not mistaken for an identity-register readout, and may transport along rank-order tasks even where identity-transport fails.

> *The profile register is the unordered exponent multiset; the prime-identity register is the labeled integer. The prime-rank register lies between them — it remembers order by prime size without remembering labels — and naming it prevents the false binary that a readout is either profile-level or identity-level.*

This note bounds the transport claim of Part VI / VI.6 §10.8(c): profile-class transport carries the profile register and stops at the prime-rank register, the first finer layer.

## II.19 Closure-of-description readout

**Source:** Rmk 1.5b closure-half (v2.7.10); Rmk 1.6b (v2.7.10).  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.19.  
**Status:** interpretive.

The closure-of-description readout is the readout-side companion of the coordinate-substrate / closure-substrate distinction (Part I / I.10). Where I.10 names the substrate-level dichotomy, here we install the readout machinery.

<!-- id: RMK-1.5b-CLOSURE-SUBSTRATE | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 1.4, 2.2 -->
**Remark 1.5b (Closure-of-Description Substrate, v2.7.10, preserved — closure-half).** The closure-of-description substrate $\Lambda_{\mathrm{desc}}(O, \Sigma, I)$ arises from a Galois connection on derivation operators. Given:

- $O$ — a set of *objects* (e.g., divisors, walk visits, walk transitions);
- $\Sigma$ — a set of *predicates / derivations* describing properties of objects;
- $I \subseteq O \times \Sigma$ — the *incidence relation*: $oI\sigma$ iff object $o$ satisfies predicate $\sigma$;

the Galois connection
$$
A' \;=\; \{\sigma \in \Sigma : \forall o \in A,\; oI\sigma\}, \qquad B' \;=\; \{o \in O : \forall \sigma \in B,\; oI\sigma\}
$$
maps subsets of $O$ to subsets of $\Sigma$ and back. The closure $A \mapsto A''$ on subsets of $O$ defines the *closure-of-description substrate* $\Lambda_{\mathrm{desc}}(O, \Sigma, I)$ as the lattice of closed sets.

**Canonical recovery.** When $\Sigma$ is the set of prime-power divisibility predicates over the join-irreducibles of $\mathrm{Div}(N)$, the closure substrate $\Lambda_{\mathrm{desc}}$ is isomorphic to $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ — the order-ideal representation theorem [Bir40] recovers the coordinate substrate from its description.

**Non-canonical extensions.** When $\Sigma$ is observation-derived — walker history, lens state, atom/jump/zero classification (Part IV / IV.4 Rmk 9.3f), per-visit attributes (Rmk 1.6b below) — $\Lambda_{\mathrm{desc}}$ diverges from $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ and provides genuinely new structure. Naming follows the framework's principle of description over prescription: the closure substrate describes the structure of *what can be said about* the objects, not a prescription about what to do with them.

<!-- id: RMK-1.6b-THREE-OBSERVATION-CONTEXTS | register: interpretive | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 1.5, RMK-1.5b-CLOSURE-SUBSTRATE, 2.2c, 9.3f -->
**Remark 1.6b (Three Observation Contexts, v2.7.10, preserved).** Trainer-derived attributes attach at three distinct levels of primitive object. Each level supports its own $\Lambda_{\mathrm{desc}}$. Conflating the levels yields invalid closure structure.

- **Node context.** $O_{\mathrm{node}} := \mathrm{Div}(N)$. Attributes: intrinsic structural properties (domain class, $\omega(n)$, $\Omega(n)$, pairCoords, lensStatus($L$)($n$), and predicates definable over the lattice structure). Persistent across walks. The closure lattice $\Lambda_{\mathrm{desc}}(O_{\mathrm{node}}, \Sigma_{\mathrm{node}}, I_{\mathrm{node}})$ captures which sets of nodes cohere under shared structural descriptions.
- **Visit context.** $O_{\mathrm{visit}} := $ ordered pairs $(k, n_k)$ drawn from a trajectory $T = (n_0, n_1, \ldots, n_T)$. Attributes: per-event properties (was-target, was-revisit-of-prior-value, lens-state-at-visit, $k$th-revisit-of-this-value, time-since-last-visit-of-this-value). Specific to a single trajectory.
- **Transition context.** $O_{\mathrm{trans}} := $ ordered triples $(k, n_k, n_{k+1})$. Attributes: per-move properties (layer ∈ {atom, jump, zero} from Rmk 9.3f, prime-used, polynomial expression, sign-flip). Specific to a single trajectory.

**Three questions answered:**
- (a) What *structural classes of nodes* exist (intrinsic, trajectory-independent)?
- (b) What kinds of *visit-events* recur within a walk (per-trajectory)?
- (c) What kinds of *moves* recur within a walk (per-trajectory)?

Each induces its own $\Lambda_{\mathrm{desc}}$ when computed. Cross-context queries require constructing a fourth context whose objects span a chosen domain and whose attributes are pulled across levels; merging existing ones is not permitted.

**Data-structure separation.** LatticeNode carries node-context attributes (computed at lattice construction), WalkerVisit carries visit-context attributes (populated during walking), WalkerTransition carries transition-context attributes (populated per step). The reserved attributes slots in the v0.2 walker schema (WalkerVisit.attributes, WalkerTransition.attributes) are the kernel-grounded entry point for $\Lambda_{\mathrm{desc}}$ overlay computation in subsequent trainer versions.

## II.20 Monoidal functor structure

**Source:** §11, Def 11.1, Def 11.2, Thm 11.3, Rmk 11.4, Cor 11.5 (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part II / II.20.  
**Status:** definitional / research-scoped (v3.2.0 correction).

<!-- id: DEF-11.1-CATEGORY-SEQ | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: none -->
**Definition 11.1 (Category Seq, preserved).** **Seq** has: objects — finite sequences $X = (x_0, x_1, \ldots, x_{L-1})$ over $\mathbb{R}$ or $\mathbb{C}$; morphisms — length-preserving maps; monoidal product — concatenation $X \otimes X' = (x_0, \ldots, x_{L-1}, x_0', \ldots, x_{L'-1}')$; unit — the empty sequence $\varepsilon$.

<!-- id: DEF-11.2-CATEGORY-TRITRAJ | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: none -->
**Definition 11.2 (Category TriTraj, preserved).** **TriTraj** has: objects — trilattice trajectories $T = (c(0), c(1), \ldots, c(L-1))$ where $c(t) = (c_{719}(t), c_{720}(t), c_{721}(t))$; monoidal product — trajectory concatenation with phase continuity.

<!-- id: THM-11.3-FVLT-AS-STRICT-MONOIDAL-FUNCTOR | register: proposed | status: research | provenance: original | depends: none -->
**Research Note 11.3 (Strict Monoidal Functor Claim, v3.2.0 downgraded).** The legacy claim that $F: \mathrm{Seq} \to \mathrm{TriTraj}$ is strict monoidal is not active theorem authority without fully declared hypotheses for anchoring, normalization, and concatenation indexing. A restricted absolute-index feature map can be studied by declaring those choices explicitly; arbitrary windowed energies do not inherit boundary independence merely from the formula for $c_q(t)$.

*Status:* research/provisional. The previous proof sketch ignored sample inclusion, reanchoring, normalization, and concatenation index shifts.

<!-- id: RMK-11.4-CONTRAST-WITH-DFT | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: none -->
**Remark 11.4 (Contrast with DFT, v3.2.0 scoped).** The DFT depends on the total sequence length $N$, and windowing choices matter. FVLT's absolute-position Ramanujan features can avoid some DFT-style spectral-leakage mechanisms under declared support and indexing conventions, but the v3.2.0 baseline does not claim that FVLT windowed energies avoid all boundary artifacts or never require tapering.

<!-- id: COR-11.5-WINDOW-BOUNDARY-INDEPENDENCE | register: superseded | status: superseded-as-stated | provenance: original | depends: 5.5f -->
**Windowed Energy Contract 11.5 (v3.2.0 correction; old corollary superseded as stated).** A windowed statistic must be written $E_{q,W}^{\mathrm{line}}$ or $E_{q,W}^{\mathrm{full}}$ and must declare: support $W$; whether sample indices use absolute coordinates or are reanchored at the window origin; normalization; and taper policy, including the choice of no taper if that is intended. Universal boundary-independence, window-size-insensitivity, and no-taper claims are not active theorems in this baseline.
