# Part III — Atlases and Structural Classes

Part III classifies the atoms of the substrate (Part I) into structural families without singling out any one atom. The classification has multiple axes — exponent-signature shape, prime support, polygon-order multiset, fiber compatibility — and the resulting families are the *atlases in the macro-section sense*: collections of atoms organized by topic. Part V then locates the $720$ factorial-shell / $1001$ complement instance inside this organization.

## III.1 Atom classification overview

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §D preamble (atlas-classification framing).  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.1.  
**Status:** structural.

The substrate $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$ admits classification along several independent axes:

- **Signature shape** (Part III / III.2–III.4): constant / strictly-decreasing / general-weakly-decreasing / non-monotone
- **Prime support** (Part III / III.5–III.9): squarefree / non-squarefree; basis-only / basis-plus-complement / mixed
- **Polygon-order multiset** (Part II / II.16, Part III / III.7): the multiset of fiber orders $\{a_i + 1\}$
- **Fiber compatibility** (Part III / III.10): local-crystallographic / globally compatible / quasicrystalline
- **Sampling regime** (Part III / III.13): primorial / non-primorial samplers
- **Shell extension** (Part III / III.12): canonical / sub-shell / multi-prime-shell

The signature-shape axis additionally indexes **symmetry-speedup cost** (Part VI / VI.6 §10.8): constant signature is the maximal-collapse corner, strictly-decreasing signature the minimal-collapse interior. Atoms sharing a signature multiset form a *profile class* — an isomorphism class of atoms — across which profile-register results transport without recomputation. Profile class is the coarsest of three nested registers; the *prime-rank* and *prime-identity* registers refining it are Note C3e-II.1 (Part II / II.18).

Each axis partitions $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$ differently; the worked atom $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ occupies a specific position in each under the local Part V criteria (Part V / V.2–V.3).

## III.2 Symmetric atoms

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §D Def D.1 (symmetric boundary); §D D.2.a; Rmk 9.7f general half.  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.2.  
**Status:** definitional / structural.

Atoms with constant exponent signature: $a_1 = a_2 = \cdots = a_m$. Axes are interchangeable; the automorphism group $S_m$ acts by prime-permutation.

**Squarefree sub-family.** Constant signature $(1, 1, \ldots, 1)$ — the primorial family (Part III / III.5).

**Non-squarefree constant-signature atoms.** Examples: $\mathrm{Div}(4) = \mathrm{Div}(2^2)$, $\mathrm{Div}(36) = \mathrm{Div}(2^2 \cdot 3^2)$, $\mathrm{Div}(900) = \mathrm{Div}(2^2 \cdot 3^2 \cdot 5^2)$, $\mathrm{Div}(30030 \cdot 30030) = \mathrm{Div}(p_1^2 \cdots p_6^2)$. All satisfy $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} = 0$ by Lemma 9.7c (Part II / II.15).

$\mathrm{Div}(900)$ verified at $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} = 0$ via helix-lattice viewer instrument (v2.7.13, preserved).

## III.3 Asymmetric primitive class

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §D Def D.1.  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.3.  
**Status:** definitional.

<!-- id: DEF-D.1-ASYMMETRIC-PRIMITIVE | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: 9.7 -->
**Definition D.1 (Asymmetric Primitive, v3.0-alpha, preserved).** For $n \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ with prime factorization $n = \prod_{i=1}^{m} p_i^{a_i}$ under canonical prime ordering $p_1 < p_2 < \cdots < p_m$, the *exponent signature* of $n$ is $\sigma(n) := (a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_m)$.

The atom $\mathrm{Div}(n)$ is an *asymmetric primitive* iff $\sigma(n)$ is weakly decreasing: $a_1 \geq a_2 \geq \cdots \geq a_m \geq 1$.

**Sub-classes by strictness:**
- **Constant signature** (symmetric boundary): $a_1 = a_2 = \cdots = a_m$.
- **Strictly decreasing signature** (strict interior): $a_1 > a_2 > \cdots > a_m$.

**Naming note (preserved).** The term "asymmetric primitive class" names the monotone-ordered class as a whole; its constant-signature boundary is symmetric. The class is monotone ⊇ constant ⊕ strictly decreasing.

## III.4 Mixed and non-monotone atoms

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §D Def D.1 closing paragraph.  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.4.  
**Status:** structural scope.

Atoms with non-monotone signature lie *outside* the asymmetric primitive class. Example: $\mathrm{Div}(75) = \mathrm{Div}(3 \cdot 5^2)$ has signature $(1, 2)$ under canonical prime ordering — non-monotone.

Classification of non-monotone atoms is deferred (open structural question). Some non-monotone atoms admit re-ordering of primes to monotone signature; the canonical-ordering convention prevents trivializing the question.

## III.5 Primorials (symmetric boundary)

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §D D.2.a (primorial family); Thm K.2 atom-local (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.5.  
**Status:** structural; unconditional fingerprints per family.

The $n$-th primorial is $P_n := \prod_{i=1}^{n} p_i$ with signature $(1, 1, \ldots, 1)$ on $n$ primes. The atom $\mathrm{Div}(P_n)$ is the Boolean cube $P_2^{\square n}$ (Thm K.2, atom-local, Part V / V.14). Invariants: $\tau(P_n) = 2^n$, $\Omega_{\max}(P_n) = n$, polygon multiset $\{2\}^n$.

First instances: $P_1 = 2$, $P_2 = 6$, $P_3 = 30$, $P_4 = 210$, $P_5 = 2310$, $P_6 = 30030$.

**Basis vs complement primorials.** The basis primorial $\mathrm{Div}(30)$ (on $\{2, 3, 5\}$) and the complement primorial $\mathrm{Div}(1001) = \mathrm{Div}(7 \cdot 11 \cdot 13)$ are the canonical Boolean cubes in FVLT's $720/1001$ architecture (Part V / V.4). The complement primorial is a primorial *on the complement primes*, not on the first three primes — the family is closed under prime-support choice.

$\mathrm{Div}(P_n)$ has the full automorphism group $S_n$ acting on primes — the symmetric boundary of the asymmetric primitive class. Part II / II.22.7 (§13.7) gives the Fock-space primorial classification.

## III.6 Factorials (Legendre signature)

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §D D.2.b (factorial family).  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.6.  
**Status:** structural; unconditional.

The factorial $k!$ has exponent signature determined by Legendre's formula: $a_p(k!) = \sum_{j \geq 1} \lfloor k/p^j \rfloor$. Weakly decreasing across primes by Legendre; often strictly decreasing, but repeated tail exponents occur when several large primes enter the support with exponent $1$:

- $3! = 6$, signature $(1, 1)$ — weakly but not strictly decreasing
- $5! = 120$, signature $(3, 1, 1)$
- $6! = 720$, signature $(4, 2, 1)$ — **strictly** decreasing on its support
- $7! = 5040$, signature $(4, 2, 1, 1)$ — strictness fails at tail

The prime support of $k!$ is exactly the primes $\leq k$. Strict decrease across the entire support is the special case; $6!$ realizes it. This makes $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ the smallest strictly-decreasing factorial — atom-local property; canonical role in Part V.

Small factorials are also highly composite (III.8): $2!, 3!, 4!, 5!, 6!, 7!$ are all HCN.

## III.7 Superprimorials (staircase signature)

**Source:** Rmk 9.7l (v2.7.14); v3.0-alpha §D D.2.c.  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.7.  
**Status:** unconditional / structural.

The $n$-th superprimorial is $\mathrm{Sp}_n := \prod_{i=1}^{n} p_i^{n - i + 1}$, with signature $(n, n-1, \ldots, 2, 1)$ — the staircase. Strictly decreasing by construction.

<!-- id: RMK-9.7l-SUPERPRIMORIAL-SIGNATURE | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 9.7, 9.7h, 9.7j, 9.7k -->
**Remark 9.7l (Superprimorial Polygon Signature, v2.7.14, preserved, unconditional).** For $\mathrm{Sp}_n = \prod_{i=1}^{n} p_i^{n-i+1}$, the polygon-order multiset is the *consecutive run*
$$
\{a_i + 1\}_{i=1}^{n} \;=\; \{2, 3, \ldots, n+1\}.
$$
Every polygon order from digon to $(n+1)$-gon appears in the local comb at any divisor, each exactly once.

**Three equivalent readings:** polygon assembly (Part II / II.16), spin profile $(n/2, (n-1)/2, \ldots, 1/2)$, product crystal $V_{1/2} \otimes V_1 \otimes \cdots \otimes V_{n/2}$ (Part II / II.17).

**Lattice constants:** $\tau(\mathrm{Sp}_n) = (n+1)!$, $\Omega_{\max}(\mathrm{Sp}_n) = T_n = n(n+1)/2$, $|\mathrm{comb}(d)| = T_n + 1$. For $\mathrm{Sp}_6$: $\tau = 5040 = 7!$, $\Omega_{\max} = 21$, multiset $\{2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7\}$.

**Scope of characterization (preserved).** The polygon-order multiset is invariant under permutation of which prime carries which exponent. The multiset is the *family classifier* (staircase profile on $n$ primes); identifying $\mathrm{Sp}_n$ specifically requires (i) prime support $= \{p_1, \ldots, p_n\}$, the $n$ smallest primes; and (ii) anti-correlation rule $a_{p_i}$ decreasing in $p_i$.

## III.8 Highly composite numbers (Ramanujan's class)

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §D D.2.d (HCN family).  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.8.  
**Status:** structural.

$n$ is *highly composite* (Ramanujan, 1915) iff $\tau(m) < \tau(n)$ for all $m < n$. Ramanujan proved that HCN signatures are weakly decreasing, with the additional constraint that the smallest exponent in the support is $1$ (with the single exception $n = 4$).

HCN sequence: $1, 2, 4, 6, 12, 24, 36, 48, 60, 120, 180, 240, 360, 720, 840, 1260, 1680, 2520, 5040, \ldots$

Overlaps: with factorials at $\{2, 6, 24, 120, 720, 5040\}$; with primorials only at $\{2, 6\}$; with superprimorials at $\{2, 12, 360, \ldots\}$.

$720$ is the 13th HCN — atom-local property; canonical role in Part V.

## III.9 Multi-prime shells

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §D framing of multi-prime atoms.  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.9.  
**Status:** structural.

Atoms with several primes activated form their own structural class: $720$ ($3$ primes), $720720$ ($6$ primes), $\mathrm{Sp}_n$ for $n \geq 3$, primorial $P_n$ for $n \geq 2$. Multi-prime shells admit basis/complement architecture (Part V / V.4) when the prime set splits naturally into named subsets.

## III.10 Fiber compatibility and non-crystallographic regimes

**Source:** Note 9.7i (v2.7.14); §9.5 (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.10.  
**Status:** interpretive / unconditional (per-block).

<!-- id: NOTE-9.7i-FIBER-COMPATIBILITY | register: note | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 9.7, 9.7b, 9.7c, 9.7e, 9.7h -->
**Note 9.7i (Fiber-Level Non-Crystallographic Symmetry, v2.7.14, preserved).** Two distinct axes on local fiber orders $n_i = a_i + 1$:
- **Local crystallographic status:** $n_i \in \{1, 2, 3, 4, 6\}$ (classical orders allowed by the crystallographic restriction theorem in the plane).
- **Global compatibility:** $n_i \mid m$ (the local cyclic-group order embeds into the global $m$-fold phasing as a subgroup).

These conditions are distinct. For $m = 6$, global compatibility implies local crystallographic status (since $n_i \mid 6 \implies n_i \in \{1, 2, 3, 6\} \subset \{1, 2, 3, 4, 6\}$), but the converse fails: $n_i = 4$ is locally crystallographic yet globally incompatible at $m = 6$ ($4 \nmid 6$). A square fiber inside hexagonal global phasing differs structurally from a pentagon — the square shares 2-fold rotational symmetry with the hexagon, while the pentagon shares no non-trivial rotational symmetry.

**Independence witnesses at $m = 6$ (2×3 grid, preserved):**

|  | crystallographic + globally compatible | crystallographic, globally incompatible | non-crystallographic |
|---|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| **profile symmetric** | $\mathrm{Div}(30030)$, $a = (1^6)$, $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} = 0$ | $a = (3^6)$, $n = (4^6)$, $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} = 0$ | constant-quintic $a = (4^6)$, $n = (5^6)$, $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} = 0$ |
| **profile asymmetric** | $a = (1^5, 5)$, $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} = 31/36$ | $a = (3, 1^5)$, $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} = 5/8$ | $\mathrm{Div}(720720)$, $a = (4, 2, 1^4)$, $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} = 1.864204$ |

All six cells inhabited by explicit examples. The constant-quintic settles that fiber non-crystallographic symmetry does not imply $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} > 0$ (every fiber is a pentagon at $m = 6$, yet $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} = 0$ by Lem 9.7c). The constant-quartic $a = (3^6)$ settles that global incompatibility does not imply $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}} > 0$ either. *Profile asymmetry, fiber crystallographic status, and fiber global compatibility are three distinct diagnostics.*

> *The pentagon and the strain are different witnesses. The square and the strain are different witnesses. The strain itself is neither — it is the phase-weighted Fourier residual of the multiplicity profile, a separate diagnostic coexisting with both fiber-compatibility regimes.*

**§9.5 Prime-clean fibers (preserved).** A fiber is *prime-clean* iff its order is prime. Prime-clean fibers admit the simplest cyclic-character readout (Z/p Z); composite fibers admit subgroup structure that complicates the harmonic register. Prime-clean fiber definitions are referenced from Part II / II.16 (CRT fibered presentation).

## III.11 Goldbach resonance atlas

**Source:** §6.1, §6.2, §6.3, §6.4, Def 6.1, Thm 6.2, Thm 6.3 (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.11.  
**Status:** unconditional / conditional (per-block).

The Goldbach atlas is the regime of resonance arising from prime-pair structure under the Goldbach conjecture.

<!-- id: DEF-6.1-RESONANT-PAIRS | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: none -->
**Definition 6.1 (Resonant Pairs, preserved).** For even $N \geq 4$, a *resonant pair* is an unordered pair $\{p, q\}$ of primes with $p + q = N$. The Goldbach conjecture asserts that every even $N \geq 4$ has at least one resonant pair.

<!-- id: THM-6.2-RESONANCE-PURITY | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 6.1 -->
**Theorem 6.2 (Resonance Purity, preserved, unconditional).** For any resonant pair $\{p, q\}$ summing to $N$, the Ramanujan channel $c_N$ carries energy from $(p, q)$ inputs that is *pure* in the sense that off-diagonal cross-channel energy vanishes. The Goldbach pair acts as a coherent pair of channels with no inter-pair leakage.

<!-- id: THM-6.3-CONDITIONAL-COVERAGE | register: proved | status: conditional | provenance: original | depends: 6.1, Goldbach -->
**Theorem 6.3 (Conditional Coverage, preserved, conditional on Goldbach).** Under the Goldbach conjecture, every even $N \geq 4$ admits resonant-pair coverage in the harmonic register. *(Status preserved verbatim: conditional on Goldbach.)*

<!-- id: ALG-6.4-ALGORITHM | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: 6.1 -->
**Algorithm 6.4 (preserved).** For even $N$, enumerate primes $p \leq N/2$ and test whether $N - p$ is prime; output all resonant pairs. Cost: $O(\pi(N/2) \cdot \log N)$ for primality testing.

In the measurement-instrument reading, each resonant pair is modeled as seeding a phase-locked region at $p/N$ of approximate width $1/\sqrt{pq}$ (Part II / II.23.5, Rmk II.23.5); tongue occupancy gives an operational, interpretive classification of signal Goldbach-coherence.

## III.12 Generalized shell regimes

**Source:** §8.2 (v2.7.14); §8.3 Rmk 8.5c–e resolution/selection half.  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.12.  
**Status:** structural.

<!-- id: SEC-8.2-SUB-SHELL | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: 8.1 -->
**§8.2 Sub-Shell Extension (v3.2.0 scoped).** In the worked factorial-shell instance, $N_0 = 720$. The framework extends to sub-shells (e.g., $\mathrm{Div}(N_t^* \mid N_0)$ under lens contraction, Part IV / IV.1) and to multi-prime extensions ($\mathrm{Div}(720720)$, $\mathrm{Div}(\mathrm{Sp}_n)$). Generalized shell regimes connect to asymmetric / primorial classes (Part III / III.3, III.5).

**§8.3 DFT resolution and shell-selection (Rmk 8.5c–e half, preserved).** The sliding $E_q$ scanner instrument (Part II / II.8) has resolution determined by the shell size and a selection rule for the operating $q$. The resolution constraint and shell-selection rule are atlas-level facts about the regime, not instrument-level mechanics.

## III.13 Primorial sampling classification

**Source:** §8.3 (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.13.  
**Status:** structural.

<!-- id: SEC-8.3-PRIMORIAL-SAMPLING | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: 8.2 -->
**§8.3 Primorial Sampling Classification (preserved).** For operating shell $N$ with primorial sampling $M = \prod B$, define $\Sigma(M, N) := \mathrm{Div}(N) \cap \mathrm{Div}(M)$. The class $|\Sigma(M, N)|$ varies inversely with the coprime sector occupancy (Part II / II.22.7 Thm 13.19). Three classes — full / partial / minimal $\Sigma$ — partition the sampling regimes.

## III.14 Farey / Mandelbrot / Julia cousin atlas

**Source:** §9.6 (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.14.  
**Status:** interpretive.

<!-- id: SEC-9.6-COUSIN-ATLAS | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 9.1a -->
**§9.6 Shared Rational Skeleton (preserved).** The Farey spine of FVLT, the Mandelbrot-set parameter space, the Julia-set dynamics, and (third cousin) the Rogers–Ramanujan $q$-series / modular-form world share a common rational skeleton — the exponential-rational substrate of §9.1a — but operate on it through divergent readout machinery. FVLT reads through $\mathrm{Div}(N)$, Hasse-diagram metric, gcd-orbits, and Ramanujan subspace energies $E_q$. Rogers–Ramanujan reads through $\Gamma$-equivariance on the upper half plane, level-5 cosets, and complex-multiplication evaluations. The cousinhood is at the spine; the divergence is at the readout. The substrate-level pentagonal source of $\varphi$ shared by both routes is located in Part II / II.13 Rmk 9.3c; the structural non-collapse of the two φ-arrivals is Part V / V.14 Rmk K.3d.

## III.15 CVA / Divisor-RoPE atlas

**Source:** Rmk B.5 (v3.0-alpha); CVA bridge content.  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.15.  
**Status:** interpretive / research-track.

Cyclotomic Vernier Attention (CVA) and Divisor-RoPE are positional-prior instruments derived from FVLT structure. Cyclotomic factorization (Part IV / IV.9) governs the prime supports of visited atoms; CVA installs this as an attention bias in transformer architectures. The technical AI-application body lives at Part VI / VI.3; the atlas-regime perspective (which classes of atoms / regimes CVA is appropriate for) lives here. *L1a–L2 tier structure preserved as research-track scope.*

## III.16 Ontology atlas

**Source:** Note 9.7n framing (v2.7.13/14); v3.0-alpha §1.3 ontology-atlas mention.  
**v3.0 location:** Part III / III.16.  
**Status:** structural / research-track.

The ontology atlas applies the closure-of-description substrate (Part II / II.19) to typed property graphs (e.g., Serena's tissue graph, Part VI / VI.12). It is a non-canonical extension of $\Lambda_{\mathrm{desc}}$ in the sense of Part I / I.10: $\Sigma$ is observation-derived (typed predicates over Serena's property graph schema), and the resulting $\Lambda_{\mathrm{desc}}$ may diverge from $\mathrm{Div}(N)$.

Falsifiable per Note 9.7n containment scope (Part VII / VII.4): if the four conditions for substrate extraction (FCA closure, declared $\Omega_{\mathrm{desc}}$, declared bank assignment, computed phase orthogonality) cannot be verified for a candidate ontology, the kernel-theorem inheritance fails and the ontology atlas is research-track at most.

# Part IV — Dynamics and Walk Grammar

The substrate (Part I) is static. Movement on and across the atlas — walks — is the subject of Part IV. The grammar separates four registers (atom-internal Hasse motion, atlas-level motion between atoms, successor / transversal motion, carrier events through $0$) that must not be conflated (Rule 8). Walks built from these registers compose into trajectories with structured atom-class visitation patterns.

## IV.1 Walk-relative lens

**Source:** §1.5 preamble (v2.7.14); Rmk 1.3c reference.  
**v3.0 location:** Part IV / IV.1.  
**Status:** definitional / structural.

The trainer operates under a *base lens* $N_{\mathrm{base}}$ (typically $720$), an *operative lens* $N_t^*$ at step $t$ ($N_t^* \mid N_{\mathrm{base}}$), and a *current lens* $N_t$ (which may differ from $N_t^*$ under lens expansion). The nesting $N_{\mathrm{base}} \mid N_t^* \mid N_t$ (Def 2.2d, Part I / I.6) is preserved under lens contraction; lens contraction is a view change, not erasure.

**Breathing lens.** A trajectory's viewer-pulse $N_t^*$ may oscillate ("breathe") around the base lens as walks visit lower-$\Omega$ atoms in the trajectory.

**Scope function.** The scope function $\mathrm{scope}(N_t, N_t^*)$ selects the instrument's *operative subset* of $\mathrm{Div}(N_t)$ for the current step. The scope function is an instrument choice; theorems proved under one scope function do not automatically lift to others.

**Non-lift of base-lens-specific theorems (Rmk 1.3c, preserved).** A theorem proved for $N_{\mathrm{base}} = 720$ — for example, the Fiedler value $\lambda_1(G_{720}) = 1/\varphi^2$ (Part V / V.14 Thm K.3) — does not automatically lift to $N_t \neq N_{\mathrm{base}}$. Lift behavior is theorem-specific; some lift (Thm K.2 structural identification), others do not (specific spectral values).

## IV.2 Structural motion in the atlas

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §B Def B.1.1, Def B.1.2.  
**v3.0 location:** Part IV / IV.2.  
**Status:** definitional.

<!-- id: DEF-B.1.1-STRUCTURAL-UNIT-COVER | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: A.4 -->
**Definition B.1.1 (Structural Unit Cover, v3.0-alpha, preserved).** For $x \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ and prime $p$, the *structural unit cover* is
$$
\mathrm{Div}(x) \to \mathrm{Div}(p x).
$$
This is an atlas-Hasse cover in $(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}, \leq_{\mathcal{A}})$. Inverse defined when $p \mid x$.

<!-- id: DEF-B.1.2-STRUCTURAL-COMPOSITE | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: B.1.1 -->
**Definition B.1.2 (Structural Composite Move, v3.0-alpha, preserved).** For $x, a \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ with $a = \prod_i p_i^{e_i}$, the *structural composite move* is
$$
\mathrm{Div}(x) \to \mathrm{Div}(ax)
$$
equal to the composition of $\sum_i e_i$ structural unit covers along the corresponding prime axes. Atlas-lattice displacement vector $\sigma(a)$. Inverse defined when $a \mid x$.

**Register discipline (Rule 8 reminder).** $\mathrm{Div}(x) \to \mathrm{Div}(px)$ is *atlas-level* motion (between atoms); $d \to dp$ inside fixed $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ is *atom-internal Hasse* motion. The two registers are distinct; the recursion hinge of Part I / I.5 makes their formal shape coincide but does not collapse them.

## IV.3 Successor and transversal motion

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §B Def B.1.3.  
**v3.0 location:** Part IV / IV.3.  
**Status:** definitional.

<!-- id: DEF-B.1.3-SUCCESSOR | register: definitional | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: A.4 -->
**Definition B.1.3 (Successor / Predecessor, v3.0-alpha, preserved).** For $x \geq 1$ (predecessor requires $x \geq 2$), the *successor / predecessor* move is
$$
\mathrm{Div}(x) \to \mathrm{Div}(x \pm 1).
$$
This is transversal motion external to $\leq_{\mathcal{A}}$. Consecutive atoms have trivial atlas-meet: $\gcd(x, x \pm 1) = 1$.

The successor move is the simplest atlas walk by itself (the Peano successor sequence $1, 2, 3, 4, \ldots$ visiting every atom in order).

## IV.4 Atom / jump / zero transition typology

**Source:** Rmk 9.3e (v2.7.7); Rmk 9.3f (v2.7.8).  
**v3.0 location:** Part IV / IV.4.  
**Status:** definitional.

<!-- id: RMK-9.3f-TRANSITION-TYPOLOGY | register: remark | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: A.4, 9.3, 9.3a -->
**Remark 9.3f (Atom / Jump / Zero Typology, v2.7.8, preserved).** Operationally distinct transitions in a walk:

- **Atom.** Sign-preserving Hasse cover; single prime factor difference between $n$ and $n'$. (Equivalently: structural unit cover, Def B.1.1.)
- **Jump.** Polynomial-expression-required transition between nonzero values, where no single prime factor change suffices.
- **Zero.** Carrier event with $n = 0$ or $n' = 0$; requires signed-completion handling (Part I / I.4 + §C routing note, Part IV / IV.12).

**Test order.** Zero first (check whether endpoints touch the carrier hub), atom second (check whether a single prime distinguishes them), jump residual (otherwise).

**Operational keystone (preserved).** The typology is implementation vocabulary for the trainer's transition layer, *not* a partition theorem about all maps on $\mathbb{Z}$. The trainer's atom / jump / zero classification names what each transition does mechanically; mathematical claims (such as energy decomposition) build on the typology as a measurement scaffold.

<!-- id: RMK-9.3e-ZERO-CLAUSE | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 9.3, 9.3f -->
**Remark 9.3e (Zero Clause, v2.7.7, preserved).** $0$ is excluded from the atlas (Part I / I.4) for the structural reason that $\mathrm{Div}_{\mathrm{literal}}(0)$ has infinitely many divisors. In carrier-event motion, $0$ functions as the additive hub — *singular relative to positive divisor-lattice measurement, not singular absolutely*. Identity symmetry: $1$ is the multiplicative root; $0$ is the additive root; two roots, two layers.

**Canonical routing as instrument policy.** The signed walk policy $\pm 1 \to 0 \to m'$ — sign change via passage through $0$ — is an *instrument choice*, not a forced consequence. Alternative policies (sign change via $\pm 1 \to \mp 1$ without $0$ passage) are admissible under different chirality conventions (Part I / I.3).

## IV.5 Unit floor and fraction boundary

**Source:** Rmk 9.3g (v2.7.9).  
**v3.0 location:** Part IV / IV.5.  
**Status:** interpretive.

<!-- id: RMK-9.3g-UNIT-FLOOR | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 9.3, 9.3f -->
**Remark 9.3g (Unit Floor and Fraction Boundary, v2.7.9, preserved).** $1$ is the floor of multiplicative descent in two senses simultaneously:

- *Multiplicative identity:* $\mathrm{Div}(1) = \{1\}$ is the trivial atom; $1$ is the multiplicative root of every $\mathrm{Div}(n)$.
- *Register boundary:* below $1$, division produces fractions, which exit $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ into the slope register (Part IV / IV.6).

Three-stage route example: $|n| \to \cdots \to 1 \to 0 \to m'$ exhibits all three operational regimes — atom regime (descent through $\mathrm{Div}(|n|)$), zero event (carrier passage at $|n| = 1$), jump launch (transition $1 \to 0 \to m'$).

## IV.6 Difference–slope duality in walks

**Source:** Rmk 2.2a.1 (v2.7.9).  
**v3.0 location:** Part IV / IV.6.  
**Status:** interpretive.

<!-- id: RMK-2.2a.1-DIFFERENCE-SLOPE-DUALITY | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 2.2a -->
**Remark 2.2a.1 (Difference–Slope Duality, v2.7.9, preserved).** Two registers describe the same descent-through-zero walk:

- **Difference register (Newton/forward).** Forward differences $\Delta f, \Delta^2 f, \ldots$ live without denominators. Polynomial signature in this register is a sequence of integer differences.
- **Slope register (Taylor/standard).** Standard polynomial coefficients pick up factorial denominators $1/k!$. Binomial basis $\binom{x}{k} = (x)_k / k!$ makes the toll explicit.

**Load-bearing sentence (preserved).** *The denominator is not missing from the lattice register; it has not yet been introduced.*

**Carrier-usage independence guardrail.** The factorial denominator is *basis-change cost*, NOT a register-crossing diagnostic. Two worked counterexamples preserved:
- $[12, 4, 2, 1]$ atomic walk has standard-form denominator $6$
- $[6, 12, 30, 60]$ has a jump, standard-form denominator $1$

Both diagnostics independent: denominator is unrelated to the atom/jump/zero classification.

**Companion diagnostic architecture.** Three independent diagnostic axes (preserved):

| Axis | What it measures | Independent of |
|---|---|---|
| History | atom / jump / zero ribbon of visited transitions | denominator, degree |
| Signature | Newton interpolant degree | denominator, history |
| Translation cost | standard-form denominator | history, degree |

## IV.7 Lattice metric and polynomial signature degree

**Source:** Note 2.2a.2 (v2.7.9).  
**v3.0 location:** Part IV / IV.7.  
**Status:** interpretive.

<!-- id: NOTE-2.2a.2-LATTICE-METRIC-DEGREE | register: note | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 2.2a, 2.2a.1, 2.2b -->
**Note 2.2a.2 (Lattice Metric ↔ Polynomial Degree, v2.7.9, preserved).** For descent-through-zero walks $|n| \to \cdots \to 1 \to 0 \to m'$:

- Visit count $= \Omega(n) + 3$
- Newton interpolant degree $\leq \Omega(n) + 2$
- Standard-form denominator divides $(\Omega(n) + 2)!$

This identifies $d(n, 1) = \Omega(n)$ (Cor 2.2b.1, Part I / I.7) with polynomial signature degree.

**Each prime factor in start contributes one degree** to signature. The Newton-register descent is fraction-free; the standard-register expression picks up $\Omega(n) + 2$ factorial denominators by basis change.

**Two senses of shell distinguished:** lattice shell ($\mathrm{Div}(N_0)$ up to $\Omega$) vs polynomial shell ($N_k = k!$ for standardization) — independent (Part I / I.6).

## IV.8 Affine atlas walks

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §B Rmk B.3 (with B.3.a, B.3.b).  
**v3.0 location:** Part IV / IV.8.  
**Status:** structural; unconditional closed forms.

<!-- id: RMK-B.3-AFFINE-FAMILY | register: remark | status: structural | provenance: original | depends: A.4, B.1 -->
**Remark B.3 (Affine Family $f_a^b(x) = ax + b$, v3.0-alpha, preserved).** For $a \in \mathbb{N}_{>0}$ and $b \in \{-1, 0, +1\}$, the affine recursion decomposes as one structural composite move ($\times a$, atlas-Hasse displacement $\sigma(a)$) followed by zero, one positive, or one negative successor.

**B.3.a — Iterates from $x_0 = 1$ for $b = +1$, $a \geq 2$ (preserved).** $x_n = (a^{n+1} - 1)/(a - 1)$ (repunit base $a$). Worked instances:
- $a = 1$: $x_n = n + 1$. Pure Peano successor.
- $a = 2$: $x_n = 2^{n+1} - 1$. The Mersenne sequence $3, 7, 15, 31, 63, 127, 255, 511, 1023, 2047, \ldots$.
- $a = 3$: $x_n = (3^{n+1} - 1)/2$. The 3-adic-flavored sequence $4, 13, 40, 121, 364, 1093, \ldots$ — literally the upper branch of Collatz iterated.

**B.3.b — Iterates for $b = -1$, $a \geq 2$ (preserved).** Fixed point $x^* = 1/(a - 1)$ is rational, not integer for $a \geq 2$; integer trajectories starting at $x_0 \neq x^*$ escape. For $a = 2$ from $x_0 = 2$: $x_n = 2^n + 1$ (Fermat-flavored; classical Fermat $F_k = 2^{2^k} + 1$ at $n = 2^k$ as sub-sequence).

## IV.9 Cyclotomic structure of visited atoms

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §B Rmk B.5 + B.3.c.  
**v3.0 location:** Part IV / IV.9.  
**Status:** structural; unconditional cyclotomic factorization (classical); interpretive CVA bridge.

<!-- id: RMK-B.5-CYCLOTOMIC-STRUCTURE | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: B.3, classical-cyclotomic -->
**Remark B.5 (Cyclotomic Factorization of Visited Atoms, v3.0-alpha, preserved).** The atoms visited by $f_a^{+1}$ iteration from $x_0 = 1$ have prime supports governed by cyclotomic factorization:
$$
a^n - 1 \;=\; \prod_{d \mid n} \Phi_d(a),
$$
so for $x_n = (a^{n+1} - 1)/(a - 1) = \prod_{d \mid (n+1),\, d > 1} \Phi_d(a)$, the prime support of $\mathrm{Div}(x_n)$ is the union of prime supports of $\Phi_d(a)$ for $d \mid (n+1), d > 1$. Signature shape determined by multiplicities $v_p(\prod_d \Phi_d(a))$.

**Worked instances at $a = 2$ (preserved):**
- $x_3 = 15 = 3 \cdot 5$ — Boolean cube (Part III / III.5)
- $x_4 = 31$ — Mersenne prime, trivial atom
- $x_5 = 63 = 3^2 \cdot 7$ — asymmetric, signature $(2, 1)$ on $(3, 7)$

**B.3.c — Trajectories cut across §D atom classes (preserved).** No §D (Part III) class preference. The trajectory respects neither symmetric nor asymmetric class boundaries; the atom-class of each visited atom is determined by the cyclotomic factorization of $a^{n+1} - 1$, which is *transversal* to §D's signature-shape axis.

**CVA-bridge cross-reference.** The cyclotomic factorization structure connects affine walks to Cyclotomic Vernier Attention (Part III / III.15 atlas-regime; Part VI / VI.3 technical application). Interpretive structural cross-reference; not a theorem.

## IV.10 Lattice-conditioned walks

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §B Rmk B.4.  
**v3.0 location:** Part IV / IV.10.  
**Status:** definitional.

<!-- id: RMK-B.4-LATTICE-CONDITIONED | register: remark | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: B.1, 9.3f -->
**Remark B.4 (Lattice-Conditioned Walks, v3.0-alpha, preserved).** A *lattice-conditioned walk* is a walk whose next move depends on a property of the current atom readable from atom-local data — typically a prime-exponent coordinate $v_p(x)$.

**Components:** predicate over current atom (e.g., $v_2(x) \geq 1$), branch moves (one per predicate value), initialization parameters.

The conditional is **atom-local**: $v_p(x)$ readable in $O(1)$ from the exponent vector. The walk reads this single coordinate and selects one of two moves in the §B grammar.

**Generalizations.** Syracuse, $aT + b$ family, other generalized Collatz walks — all parameterized by the predicate and branch choices.

## IV.11 Collatz as paradigmatic atlas dynamics

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §B Rmk B.4 (Collatz instance).  
**v3.0 location:** Part IV / IV.11.  
**Status:** definitional / open.

The canonical lattice-conditioned walk is Collatz:
$$
\mathrm{Collatz}(x) \;=\; \begin{cases} x / 2 & \text{if } v_2(x) \geq 1 \\ 3x + 1 & \text{if } v_2(x) = 0 \end{cases}
$$

- $x / 2$ is a structural unit cover (downward along the 2-axis): $\mathrm{Div}(x) \to \mathrm{Div}(x/2)$ — **atlas-level**, not atom-internal.
- $3x + 1$ is a mixed affine move: $\mathrm{Div}(x) \to \mathrm{Div}(3x) \to \mathrm{Div}(3x + 1)$ — structural ×3 followed by successor.

**Collatz conjecture in atlas language (preserved).** Every lattice-conditioned walk under the §B.4 switch terminates at $\mathrm{Div}(1)$.

The conjecture remains open. The atlas reformulation does not solve it; it relocates the problem inside the framework's vocabulary (Part I substrate, Part IV / IV.10 lattice-conditioned move grammar).

**Status:** routed.

Signed atlas walks operate on $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}$ (Part I / I.3). Walks crossing $x = 0$ require signed-completion handling (Rmk 9.3f zero clause, Part IV / IV.4). Winding number for atlas walks lives in the companion-spine §C body.

**§C companion-spine routing note (terse, per Robin's File 1 verdict 2).** §C body in companion-spine develops signed completion formally: Def C.1 (signed atom), Rmk C.2 (chirality convention, load-bearing definitional choice — negative atoms carry reversed helix orientation $\theta \mapsto -\theta$ relative to positive counterparts), C.3 (invariants under chirality), C.7 (grammar handoff to §E). Cross-section coherence with §G: three orthogonal $\mathbb{Z}/2$ involutions — chirality $\chi$ (signed completion at the geometric realization level), Weyl reflection $w$ (§G representation-readout level), signed completion (atlas-level Part I / I.3) — declared distinct in v3.0; coupling deferred. Phase 1.a green; four flags applied during audit. Full §C body remains routed; body-inclusion is post-v3.0.

# Part V — The 720 Factorial-Shell and 1001 Complement Instance

Part V preserves the local $720$ factorial-shell and $1001$ complement construction. This is an *instrumentation choice* justified by named criteria (closure exhaustion, loop topology threshold, basis/complement architecture, and $S_6$ context in its own group register), not a substrate selection. Part V locates $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ inside the atlas (Part I), inside the atlas-classification (Part III), and inside the specific $720/1001$ bridge instance.

> *$\mathrm{Div}(720)$ is a locally selected factorial-shell instance. It is **not** the substrate. It is **not** "the atlas." It is one atom among $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$, distinguished only under the criteria named in this Part.*

## V.1 Why the 720 instance is local, not substrate

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §D.4.c framing; §8 preamble (v2.7.14); v3.0 atlas-first reframing.  
**v3.0 location:** Part V / V.1.  
**Status:** interpretive.

The v3.2.0 reframing principle: $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ is a worked factorial-shell / bridge instance. The substrate is the positive atlas $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$ (Part I / I.2); $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ is one atom in $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$.

**Why this matters.** In v2.7.14, theorems and definitions about $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ were framed as if $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ were the substrate. In v3.2.0, they are framed as *atom-local* facts about the 720 factorial-shell instrument choice. Theorem content is unchanged; the universe-of-discourse is reframed.

**Multiple local roles converge at $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ in the $k=6$ factorial-shell context:**

| Role | Source |
|---|---|
| Factorial $6!$ | Part III / III.6 |
| Highly composite (13th HCN) | Part III / III.8 |
| Smallest strictly-decreasing factorial signature | Part III / III.6 |
| Asymmetric primitive with closure exhaustion + loop topology | Part V / V.10, V.15 |
| First substrate-level $\varphi$-realization via $P_5$ chain | Part V / V.14 Thm K.3 |
| Basis/complement architecture active | Part V / V.4 |
| Maximality of $S_6$ closed factorial shell | Part V / V.16 |

The convergence of named criteria is what makes $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ the selected 720 factorial-shell instance — selected without becoming a global center.

## V.2 Selection of $N_0 = 720$

**Source:** Thm 8.20h.3 (v2.7.6); v3.0-alpha §D.4.a (motive reframed to canonical-instrument-atom selection).  
**v3.0 location:** Part V / V.2.  
**Status:** unconditional (motive reframed; theorem content unchanged).

<!-- id: THM-8.20h.3-SELECTION-OF-N0 | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 8.14a, 8.20h.2 -->
**Theorem 8.20h.3 (Selection of $N_0 = 720$ as a factorial-shell bridge instance, v2.7.6, preserved; motive reframed, theorem content scoped).** Among factorial shells $k!$ with $k \in \{2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, \ldots\}$, the shell $N_0 = 720 = 6!$ uniquely satisfies *all* of the following criteria simultaneously:

1. Closure exhaustion: $\varphi(\prod B') = k!$ holds (Thm 8.14a, Part V / V.15).
2. Loop topology threshold: complement closure index $r = 3$ achieved (Rmk 8.20h.2, Part V / V.10).
3. Basis/complement architecture: $|B| = 3$ primes $\{2, 3, 5\}$ pair with $|B'| = 3$ complement primes $\{7, 11, 13\}$ (Part V / V.4).
4. Strictly-decreasing factorial signature: $(4, 2, 1)$ on $(2, 3, 5)$ — first instance of strict decrease on the entire support.

For $k < 6$, criterion (2) fails (closure index $r < 3$). For $k > 6$, criterion (1) fails (Thm 8.14a closure exhaustion).

*Status: Unconditional under the named factorial-shell criteria. Universe-of-discourse reframed: this selects the local $720$ factorial-shell / bridge instance, not the substrate or a universal atom.*

Constructively, $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ is the $k = 6$ output of the factorial-shell phasor scan; see Algorithm Note ALG-1. The selection theorem is *evaluative* on an output that the algorithm is *constructive* about.

## V.3 $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ as $5 \times 3 \times 2$ atom

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §D.4.a; Thm K.2 atom-local (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part V / V.3.  
**Status:** unconditional (atom-local).

$720 = 6! = 2^4 \cdot 3^2 \cdot 5$, signature $(4, 2, 1)$ on $(2, 3, 5)$. Atom invariants:

- $\tau(720) = 5 \cdot 3 \cdot 2 = 30$
- $\Omega_{\max}(720) = 4 + 2 + 1 = 7$
- Polygon multiset $\{5, 3, 2\}$ (pentagon, triangle, digon)
- Thm K.2 atom-local: $G_{720} \cong P_5 \square P_3 \square P_2$ (Part V / V.14)
- $59$ covering edges in $G_{720}$

## V.4 Basis–complement architecture

**Source:** §8.4 (v2.7.14); Rmk 9.7f 720/1001-instantiation half (v2.7.13).  
**v3.0 location:** Part V / V.4.  
**Status:** definitional / unconditional.

The basis primes are $B = \{2, 3, 5\}$, supporting $720 = 2^4 \cdot 3^2 \cdot 5$. The complement primes are $B' = \{7, 11, 13\}$, supporting $1001 = 7 \cdot 11 \cdot 13$. The product $720720 = 720 \cdot 1001 = 2^4 \cdot 3^2 \cdot 5 \cdot 7 \cdot 11 \cdot 13$ is the ambient lcm lattice for this $720/1001$ bridge instance.

**Rmk 9.7f (720/1001 instantiation half, preserved).** $\mathrm{Div}(30)$ and $\mathrm{Div}(1001)$ are congruent Boolean cubes (Part II / II.16): the basis cube over $\{2, 3, 5\}$ and the complement cube over $\{7, 11, 13\}$. Their geometric congruence follows from their shared squarefree three-prime multiplicity profile $(1, 1, 1)$.

This grounds the basis/complement bridge of the FVLT $720/1001$ lens architecture as a *congruence of crystals*, not merely a pairing of numbers.

**Strain non-additivity at $720720$ (preserved).** Apex defect does NOT split additively under poset join:
$$
\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(720720) \;=\; 1.864204 \;\neq\; 0.892582 + 0 \;=\; \delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(720) + \delta_{\mathrm{apex}}(1001).
$$
At $m = 3$ in $\mathrm{Div}(1001)$ standalone, the three complement primes get all three cube roots of unity as phases, and profile $(1, 1, 1)$ cancels by character orthogonality. At $m = 6$ inside $\mathrm{Div}(720720)$, those same primes occupy positions $3, 4, 5$ with phases $(\pi, 4\pi/3, 5\pi/3)$ — three of the six sixth roots of unity but *not* a complete orthogonality basis; constant profile no longer cancels.

> *Constant-profile cancellation is $m$-relative. A crystal that closes alone may not close inside a join: the join absorbs its primes into a larger orthogonality system in which the cancellation must be recomputed.*

The basis–complement architecture's behavior under join — including the strain non-additivity above — is one of the structural facts that the Free Navigation Extension (ANL-2) makes systematically navigable. Under ANL-2's four-layer stack, the classical arithmetic backend supplies factorizations, the cached lattice assembly uses CRT component reuse plus global axis recomposition to construct $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ at any scale, and instruments subscribe to the resulting shared state. The C3 experiment (May 24, 2026; `c3_verdict.md`) empirically confirms that the phasor stack recovers the product structure $G_{720720} \cong G_{720} \square G_{1001}$ (Level 1 PASS, 240 vertices, 832 edges) but does *not* distinguish the specific $\mathrm{Br}_3^{\mathrm{pos}}$ bridge pairings (Level 2 PARTIAL); bridge architecture remains a separate-layer phenomenon, opening RES-3 / ALG-4 as the next research-track question.

**Layer separation (RES-2 closure, May 14, 2026).** The basis/complement architecture described in this section operates at the *divisor-lattice geometry* layer (Hasse covers, gcd-class congruence, Boolean-cube structure of $\mathrm{Div}(30)$ and $\mathrm{Div}(1001)$). The cyclotomic-order stratification of Algorithm Note ALG-2 operates at the *cyclotomic-spectrum* layer (gcd-indexed orders $m_0, m_{\mathrm{plot}}, m_1$ of integer-$N$ phasor values). Experiment D3 tested whether the small-$N$ cyclotomic spectrum at $s = 720$ shows complement-prime enrichment relative to the structural baseline $\#\{d \mid s : d \leq W/\ell, \, s/d \text{ even}\}$; observed counts matched baseline exactly at every tested prime coprime to $s$. The hypothesis (RES-2) was falsified, confirming that the two layers are not directly coupled at this level of observation. See `res_2_complement_prime_spectrum_draft.md` (closure) and `d3_verdict.md`.

**Basis primes as smallest atomic ratios (ALG-3 phasor-side reading).** At $s = N_0 = 720$, the continuous-$N$ phasor winding (Algorithm Note ALG-3) recovers the 240 comparable divisibility-pair ratios of $\mathrm{Div}(720)$; the combinatorial atomicity test on those ratios isolates exactly the 59 Hasse covers, whose ratios are drawn from $\{2, 3, 5\}$ — the basis primes. The basis-prime support of $720$ is therefore the phasor-side identification of the smallest non-trivial atomic ratios in the divisor lattice: basis primes are *characterized* as the atoms of $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ visible to ALG-3's recovery, not merely *postulated* as such. See Algorithm Note ALG-3.

## V.5 The 37-node basis/complement instrument

**Source:** §1.4 + §8.4a 37-node framing (v2.7.14); Rmk 1.2a (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part V / V.5.  
**Status:** structural.

The 37-node instrument $\mathrm{Div}(720) \cup \mathrm{Div}(1001)$ is a constrained sub-topology of $\mathrm{Div}(720720)$, joined at the unit $1$:
$$
G_{\mathrm{Exp}} \;=\; G_{720} \cup_{\{1\}} G_{1001}.
$$
$30 + 8 - 1 = 37$ nodes; $59 + 12 = 71$ Hasse covers; isometric subgraph of the full fused $G_{720720}$ (240 nodes, 832 covers); $203$ missing mixed-support nodes do not create shortcuts.

The 37-node topology is the local instrument *choice* within the larger ambient lcm lattice; under signals with structure at primes $p \geq 17$, the atlas extends to $\mathrm{Div}(\mathrm{lcm}(720720, n))$ (Part II / II.1 Rmk 1.2a, v3.2.0 scoped).

## V.6 $\mathrm{Div}(720720)$ as scaled exemplar

**Source:** v3.0-alpha §D.4.b.  
**v3.0 location:** Part V / V.6.  
**Status:** unconditional (atom-local) / structural.

$720720 = 720 \cdot 1001 = 2^4 \cdot 3^2 \cdot 5 \cdot 7 \cdot 11 \cdot 13$, signature $(4, 2, 1, 1, 1, 1)$.

Invariants: $\tau(720720) = 30 \cdot 8 = 240$, $\Omega_{\max}(720720) = 7 + 3 = 10$, polygon multiset $\{5, 3, 2, 2, 2, 2\}$.

Thm K.2 atom-local lift: $G_{720720} \cong P_5 \square P_3 \square P_2 \square P_2 \square P_2 \square P_2$.

Fibered structure: $\mathrm{Div}(720720) \cong \mathrm{Div}(720) \times \mathrm{Div}(1001)$ (Part I / I.8 Rmk 2.2e — substrate fact). Basis core $(4, 2, 1)$ identical to $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ — *extends, does not deform*.

## V.7 Bridge architecture

**Source:** §8.4a preamble (v2.7.14); v3.0-alpha §D.4.d Bridge bias and bridge posture.  
**v3.0 location:** Part V / V.7.  
**Status:** definitional / structural.

The bridge architecture is the load-bearing geometric content of $\mathrm{Div}(720720)$ at the instrument-atom level.

**Proper bridge divisor (preserved).** $d \in \mathrm{Div}(720720)$ is a *proper bridge divisor* iff its basis-decomposed contribution $B(d) \neq 0$, complement contribution $C(d) \neq 0$, and exterior contribution $E(d) = 0$. Proper bridges have nontrivial structure on both basis and complement sides without leakage to exterior primes.

**Bridge bias and bridge posture (preserved).** Bridge bias scalar $\beta_{\mathrm{bridge}}(d) = |B(d)|/(|B(d)| + |C(d)|) \in (0, 1)$. Bridge posture tuple $\pi_{\mathrm{bridge}}(d) = (\beta_{\mathrm{bridge}}, |B|, |C|, \arg B, \arg C, \Omega)$.

<!-- id: RMK-V.7a-BRIDGE-POSTURE-PARTIAL-SUMS | register: remark | status: definitional | provenance: original | depends: V.4, V.7, 9.7, 9.7a, 9.7f -->
**Remark V.7a (Prime-Contribution Decomposition for Bridge Posture, v3.1.14, confirmed C1-P v3.1.15, definitional).** In the bridge-posture notation above, the quantities $B(d)$, $C(d)$, and $E(d)$ are the basis, complement, and exterior partial sums of the §9.7 multi-prime embedding (Definition 9.7).

Let $N = \prod_{i=0}^{m-1} p_i^{a_i}$ under canonical prime ordering, and let $\mathbf{P}(d)$ be the Definition 9.7 embedding of $d \mid N$. Write the per-prime contribution as
$$
\mathbf{P}_i(d) \;=\; r_i \!\left( \cos\!\left(\tfrac{2\pi e_i}{n_i} + \varphi_i\right) - \cos\varphi_i,\; \sin\!\left(\tfrac{2\pi e_i}{n_i} + \varphi_i\right) - \sin\varphi_i,\; e_i \right),
$$
where $n_i = a_i + 1$, $r_i = 1/n_i$, $\varphi_i = 2\pi i/m$, and $e_i = e_i(d) = v_{p_i}(d)$.

For the canonical basis/complement split (Part V / V.4),
$$
\mathcal{B} = \{2, 3, 5\}, \qquad \mathcal{C} = \{7, 11, 13\},
$$
define
$$
B(d) = \sum_{p_i \in \mathcal{B}} \mathbf{P}_i(d), \qquad
C(d) = \sum_{p_i \in \mathcal{C}} \mathbf{P}_i(d), \qquad
E(d) = \sum_{p_i \notin \mathcal{B} \cup \mathcal{C}} \mathbf{P}_i(d).
$$
These are vectors in $\mathbb{R}^3$, and
$$
\mathbf{P}(d) = B(d) + C(d) + E(d).
$$

**Proper bridge support test (vector-level).** The V.7 definitions above — a divisor $d$ is a *proper bridge divisor* iff $B(d) \neq 0$, $C(d) \neq 0$, and $E(d) = 0$ — use vector nonzero in $\mathbb{R}^3$. Note that $B(d) = 0$ as a vector iff $d$ has no basis-prime support ($e_i = 0$ for all $p_i \in \mathcal{B}$), and likewise for $C(d)$.

**Bridge posture quantities (horizontal complex projection).** The bridge-posture phase and magnitude quantities $|B|$, $|C|$, $\arg B$, $\arg C$ in the posture tuple $\pi_{\mathrm{bridge}}(d) = (\beta_{\mathrm{bridge}}, |B|, |C|, \arg B, \arg C, \Omega)$ are computed from the horizontal $(x, y)$ projections of the partial sums, read as complex numbers:
$$
B_{\mathbb{C}}(d) = B_x(d) + i\, B_y(d), \qquad C_{\mathbb{C}}(d) = C_x(d) + i\, C_y(d).
$$
Then
$$
|B| = |B_{\mathbb{C}}(d)|, \qquad |C| = |C_{\mathbb{C}}(d)|,
$$
$$
\arg B = \arg B_{\mathbb{C}}(d), \qquad \arg C = \arg C_{\mathbb{C}}(d),
$$
and
$$
\beta_{\mathrm{bridge}}(d) = \frac{|B|}{|B| + |C|}.
$$
The $z$-components of $B(d)$ and $C(d)$ carry $\Omega$-rank information ($\Omega_B = \sum_{p_i \in \mathcal{B}} e_i$, $\Omega_C = \sum_{p_i \in \mathcal{C}} e_i$) and contribute to the $\Omega$ entry of the posture tuple, not to the phase/magnitude entries.

**Why horizontal complex, not full $\mathbb{R}^3$ norm.** The posture's $\arg B$ and $\arg C$ are phase quantities; they measure where the basis-side and complement-side contributions sit in the $(x, y)$ plane of the §9.7 embedding. Mixing the $z = \Omega$ direction into the magnitude would conflate graded rank with cyclic phase, losing the distinction between *where* in the embedding (horizontal) and *how deep* in the lattice (vertical). The full $\mathbb{R}^3$ norm remains available via $\|B(d)\| = \sqrt{|B|^2 + \Omega_B^2}$ for any analysis that needs it, but the posture tuple uses horizontal magnitude by convention.

**720/1001 instance ($N = 720720 = 720 \cdot 1001$).** At this fused ambient, the exterior term is identically zero for every divisor ($E(d) = 0$ since all six primes of $720720$ are in $\mathcal{B} \cup \mathcal{C}$). Every divisor with nontrivial basis *and* complement prime support is a proper bridge divisor. For example:

| $d$ | Factorization | $|B|$ | $\arg B$ | $|C|$ | $\arg C$ | $\beta$ |
|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 14 | $2 \cdot 7$ | 0.235 | 126.0° | 1.000 | 0.0° | 0.190 |
| 35 | $5 \cdot 7$ | 1.000 | −60.0° | 1.000 | 0.0° | 0.500 |
| 42 | $2 \cdot 3 \cdot 7$ | 0.646 | −171.2° | 1.000 | 0.0° | 0.392 |
| 231 | $3 \cdot 7 \cdot 11$ | 0.577 | −150.0° | 1.732 | 30.0° | 0.250 |

Note: $d = 77 = 7 \cdot 11$ has $|B| = 0$; it is BRIDGE_EXTENSION / pure complement, not CROSS_PRODUCT, and therefore not a proper bridge divisor. The proper-bridge-divisor condition is geometric: basis and complement embedding contributions must both be nonzero.

**In larger ambients.** For $N$ containing primes outside $\mathcal{B} \cup \mathcal{C}$ (e.g., $N$ divisible by 17), $E(d) \neq 0$ records leakage outside the canonical bridge support. A divisor with $E(d) \neq 0$ is exterior-contaminated and does not satisfy the proper-bridge-divisor condition regardless of its $B(d)$ and $C(d)$ values.

**Load-bearing sentence (preserved).** *Bridge stops being a Boolean predicate and becomes a coordinate.*

<!-- id: PROP-8.20j-SIGMA-ANT-SYNTHEME | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 8.4, 8.20a, V.4 -->
**Proposition 8.20j (Antipodal Bridge Operator as Syntheme, v3.1.9, new, unconditional).** Under the canonical hexagonal arrangement of the six primes $\{2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13\}$ at positions $\{0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5\}$ in the §9.7 embedding of $\mathrm{Div}(30030)$, the basis/complement antipodal-swap operator — the involution exchanging each basis prime with the complement prime at the antipodal hexagonal phase — is the permutation
$$
\sigma_{\mathrm{ant}} \;=\; (2, 7)(3, 11)(5, 13) \;=\; (0, 3)(1, 4)(2, 5) \;\in\; S_6
$$
of cycle type $(2, 2, 2)$. In the classical terminology of $S_6$ combinatorics, $\sigma_{\mathrm{ant}}$ is a *syntheme*: a perfect matching of the 6 prime positions into 3 pairs.

The action on the basis cube $\mathrm{Div}(30)$ sends it bijectively to the complement cube $\mathrm{Div}(1001)$, realizing the §V.4 basis/complement congruence (Rmk 9.7f) as a group-element action.

<!-- id: RMK-8.20j.1-HEX-CLASSIFICATION | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 8.20j, II.14 -->
**Remark 8.20j.1 (Hex-Distance Classification of Synthemes and Transpositions, v3.1.9, new, unconditional).** Under the canonical hexagonal arrangement of the 6 primes (Part II / II.14, Part V / V.7), the size-15 conjugacy classes of $S_6$ partition by hex-geometric content as follows.

*Transpositions* (cycle type $(2, 1^4)$, 15 total) partition by hex-distance:
$$
\text{adjacent (distance 1): } 6 \qquad \text{short-diagonal (distance 2): } 6 \qquad \text{diametrical (distance 3): } 3
$$
No singleton hex class.

*Synthemes* (cycle type $(2^3)$, 15 total) partition by hex-distance multiset:
$$
(1,1,1): 2 \qquad (1,1,3): 3 \qquad (1,2,2): 6 \qquad (2,2,3): 3 \qquad (3,3,3): 1
$$
The unique fully-diametrical syntheme — multiset $(3, 3, 3)$, the singleton class — is $\sigma_{\mathrm{ant}}$ (Prop 8.20j).

*Two-way verification.* Constructive enumeration agrees with closed-form combinatorial counts; the distance-2 graph on the 6 primes is two disjoint 3-cycles, so a fully short-diagonal syntheme is combinatorially forbidden.

*Architectural reading.* Under the geometric symmetry $D_6$ of the §9.7 figure, synthemes have a hex-singleton orbit ($\sigma_{\mathrm{ant}}$) but transpositions do not. This is the structural reason hex symmetry narrows the OP18 candidate set from 15 transpositions to the 3-element diameter class but cannot pin a singleton without additional content (Part VII / VII.7).

## V.8 Bridge depth and internal bridge filtration

**Source:** Thm 8.20a (v2.7.5); §8.4a body (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part V / V.8.  
**Status:** unconditional.

<!-- id: THM-8.20a-BR3-POS-CANONICAL | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 8.4a -->
**Theorem 8.20a ($\mathrm{Br}_3^{\mathrm{pos}}$ Canonical Bridge Family, v2.7.5, preserved, unconditional).** The canonical bridge family $\mathrm{Br}_3^{\mathrm{pos}}$ consists of the three atomic Hasse covers $1 \to 7$, $1 \to 11$, $1 \to 13$ in $G_{1001}$, paired with subset-position information in $\mathrm{Div}(720)$. Properties:

(i) Each $\mathrm{Br}_3^{\mathrm{pos}}$ edge crosses from basis ($1 \in \mathrm{Div}(720)$) to complement ($\{7, 11, 13\} \in \mathrm{Div}(1001)$).
(ii) Bridge depth $\beta(x) \in \{0, 1, 2, 3\}$ counts the number of complement primes in $x$'s support — monotone under structural unit covers along $B'$.

**Diagnostic structure (preserved).**
- $p_{\mathrm{null}}$ permutation-test calibration: surrogate $x_{\mathrm{null}}$ destroys complement structure while preserving marginal channel-energy statistics; $p_{\mathrm{null}}$ is the rank of the observed $H_{\mathrm{bridge}}$ (the bridge statistic $p_{\mathrm{null}}$ ranks — v3.1.3) among $N_{\mathrm{perm}}$ surrogates.
- $\rho_k$ monotone filtration: cumulative coverage of bridge ranks $\leq k$, monotone in $k$.
- $H_{\mathrm{bridge}}$ Herfindahl concentration: $\sum_d \rho_{\mathrm{bridge}}(d)^2$ where $\rho_{\mathrm{bridge}}(d)$ is the proportion of bridge mass at $d$.
- $\mathrm{ce}_z$ complement energy z-score: z-score of observed complement-side $\sum E_q$ against the calibrated null distribution.

**The $p_{\mathrm{null}}$ bridge statistic is $H_{\mathrm{bridge}}$ (v3.1.3, additive — no theorem statement modified).** The scalar bridge statistic whose rank against the valid (Rmk 8.20e.1, V.17) surrogate ensemble *is* $p_{\mathrm{null}}$ is $H_{\mathrm{bridge}}$: $p_{\mathrm{null}}$ is the add-one rank of the observed $H_{\mathrm{bridge}}$ among the $H_{\mathrm{bridge}}$ values of the surrogate ensemble — the null calibration of $H_{\mathrm{bridge}}$. The complement energy $E_C$ is **not** a valid $p_{\mathrm{null}}$ statistic: $E_C$-ranked $p_{\mathrm{null}}$ is structurally prone to saturation against a structure-destroyed null — a signal carrying genuine arithmetic structure has more complement energy than a structure-destroyed surrogate of itself, so the observed $E_C$ tends to exceed the entire surrogate band and $p_{\mathrm{null}}^{E_C}$ collapses toward the rank floor, a presence/absence detector rather than a resolution diagnostic. $E_C$ retains its role as the quantity $\mathrm{ce}_z$ standardizes (the $\mathrm{ce}_z$ bullet above) — there saturation does not arise, $\mathrm{ce}_z$ being a continuous $z$-score, not a rank. $H_{\mathrm{bridge}}$, bounded in $[1/8,1]$ and a concentration rather than an extensive energy, has a non-degenerate rank and supplies the $(\beta, p_{\mathrm{null}})$ diagnostic pair with a non-saturating partner. The operative content — *$p_{\mathrm{null}}$ ranks $H_{\mathrm{bridge}}$* — is a designation, unconditional; the saturation rationale is a strong structural tendency, confirmed for the C3e-II witness class (the $\chi$-type bridge witnesses against a Rmk 8.20e.1-valid permutation null). See VII.11, v3.1.3.

## V.9 Bridge correspondence layer

**Source:** Rmk 8.20g.2 (v2.7.3).  
**v3.0 location:** Part V / V.9.  
**Status:** unconditional.

<!-- id: RMK-8.20g.2-BRIDGE-CORRESPONDENCE | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 8.20a, 8.20g -->
**Remark 8.20g.2 (Bridge Correspondence Layer $\mathrm{Br}_3^\varphi$, v2.7.3, preserved, unconditional).** The totient-image correspondence is
$$
\mathrm{Br}_3^\varphi \;=\; \{(c, \varphi(c)) : c \in \mathrm{Div}(1001)\}.
$$
This realizes the closure identity $\varphi(1001) = 720$ levelwise: each $c \in \mathrm{Div}(1001)$ pairs with $\varphi(c) \in \mathrm{Div}(720)$.

**Metric-free pairing.** $\mathrm{Br}_3^\varphi$ is NOT Hasse edges; it is a totient-image correspondence on the level structure. Seven nontrivial pairs have kernel distances $3, 3, 4, 6, 7, 7, 10$ — not the unit distances of Hasse edges.

Each nontrivial pair is cross-domain (one side in $\mathrm{Div}(720)$, the other in $\mathrm{Div}(1001)$) → bridge cost vanishes (Cor 8.20g).

**$\mathrm{Br}_3^{\mathrm{pos}}$ vs $\mathrm{Br}_3^\varphi$ distinction preserved.** $\mathrm{Br}_3^{\mathrm{pos}}$ is the atomic-Hasse bridge family (V.8); $\mathrm{Br}_3^\varphi$ is the totient-correspondence bridge layer. v2.7.5 architectural cleanup made the distinction explicit.

## V.10 Complement topology and selection of $N$

**Source:** §8.4b, Rmk 8.20h.2 (v2.7.6); Rmk 9.3d 720-architectural-reading half.  
**v3.0 location:** Part V / V.10.  
**Status:** unconditional / interpretive (per-block).

<!-- id: RMK-8.20h.2-LOOP-TOPOLOGY | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 8.4b -->
**Remark 8.20h.2 (Loop Topology Threshold, v2.7.6, preserved, unconditional).** At complement closure index $r = 3$:
$$
\Delta(B_3^\circ) \;\simeq\; S^1.
$$
The hexagonal cycle: $\{7\} \to \{7, 11\} \to \{11\} \to \{11, 13\} \to \{13\} \to \{7, 13\} \to \{7\}$.

$\Delta(B_r^\circ) \simeq S^{r-2}$ classical for $r \geq 2$; $r = 3$ is the unique closure index at which a 1-cycle is the primary topological feature of complement closure.

**Rmk 9.3d (720 architectural-reading half, preserved).** The residue fan $y = Nx + b$ reads as basis-versus-complement architecture under two conditions: (i) $|B(N)| \geq 2$ (multi-prime basis support) and (ii) $N + 1$ supported on $B'(N)$ (complement primes carry the successor structure). Both conditions satisfied at $N = 720$: $|B(720)| = 3$ and $720 + 1 = 721 = 7 \cdot 103$ — exactly one prime in $B' = \{7, 11, 13\}$ supports $721$, and $103$ is exterior. The fan reads as a 720-specific architectural diagnostic, not a substrate-level fact.

The complement algebra structure on $\{7, 11, 13\}$ — closed under multiplication, generated by three primes — supplies the algebraic content of the complement side of basis/complement architecture.

**Sumset-obstruction context.** The numerical semigroup is referenced from Part II / II.21 (coprime Virasoro research/history) and Part VII / VII.6 (anti-autonomy framing): the arithmetic sumset obstruction $(U + U) \cap U = \emptyset$ is retained, while the old quotient-abelian conclusion is no longer active without a defined quotient/ideal.

**§8.6 The Möbius–Betti Bridge (preserved).** The Möbius function $\mu$ structure on $\mathrm{Div}(720720)$ couples to Betti number structure on the same lattice through a bridge correspondence. Specifically, the Möbius function on $\mathrm{Div}(720720)$ (which inherits from the squarefree spinor sector via $\mu(p_1 \cdots p_r) = (-1)^r$) connects to the first Betti number of $G_{720720}$ through the cycle-rank structure (Rmk K.4c). The Möbius–Betti bridge supplies a topological reading of the algebraic Möbius structure.

## V.13 Spinor–tensor bridge structure

**Source:** §8.7 (v2.7.14) — 720-specific bridge-use half of SPLIT; Prop 8.36, 8.37, 8.38, 8.39.  
**v3.0 location:** Part V / V.13.  
**Status:** per §8.7 block-level / unconditional (per-block).

**§8.7 720-specific bridge use (preserved).** The general representation content of spinor–tensor decomposition (Defs 8.35–8.37, Props 8.36–8.37) lives at Part II / II.13. The 720-specific instantiation:

**Proposition 8.38 (Bridge as Spinor Isomorphism, preserved, unconditional).** The squarefree bridge $\mathcal{P}(B) \cong \mathcal{P}(B')$ (§8.4) is an isomorphism of spinor sectors:
$$
\mathcal{S}(720) \;\cong\; \mathrm{Div}(1001) \;=\; \mathcal{S}(1001).
$$
The dual lattice is the spinor content of the local $720/1001$ bridge instance, externalized. The bridge correspondence respects both lattice structure (partial order) and chirality grading (Möbius sign).

**Proposition 8.39 (Double Cover, preserved, unconditional).** Let $\omega = e^{2\pi i / N_0}$ be the primitive $720$-th root of unity. The covering edge $360 \to 720$ in $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ encodes the double cover $\mathrm{SU}(2) \to \mathrm{SO}(3)$:

(i) $\omega^{N_0/2} = e^{\pi i} = -1$. A spinor traversing half the shell ($360$ steps) acquires the sign flip; it does not return to identity. Full restoration requires $\omega^{N_0} = 1$ — two complete traversals.

(ii) The index $[N_0 : N_0/2] = 2$ is the degree of the covering map.

The spinor–tensor decomposition of $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ exhibits the same double-cover relation that distinguishes spinor (squarefree, Möbius-nonzero) from tensor (non-squarefree, Möbius-zero) representations in physics.

> The spinor/tensor decomposition admits a second geometric witness alongside the apex-defect scalar (Part II / II.15 Def 9.7b): winding-only reversal $W$ of the §9.7 embedding (Part II / II.14 Lem 9.7o) is the identity exactly on the spinor sector and non-identity exactly on the tensor sector. $W$-action is strictly finer than the closure scalar $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}$, distinguishing tensor + closed atoms (e.g., $\mathrm{Div}(900)$) from spinor + closed atoms (e.g., $\mathrm{Div}(30)$) where $\delta_{\mathrm{apex}}$ agrees on both (Rmk 9.7p).

## V.14 Spectral facts: Fiedler value and $K(720)$

**Source:** Appendix K preamble + §K.1, Thm K.2, §K.3, Thm K.3, Rmk K.3a, Rmk K.3c, Rmk K.3d (v2.7.4), §K.4, Rmk K.4c, Rmk K.4d, §K.5 (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part V / V.14.  
**Status:** unconditional (theorems) / empirical-computational (K(720) value).

**Appendix K preamble (preserved, contextualized).** This appendix documents two graph-theoretic invariants of the local $720$ worked atom. The Fiedler value is **proved** (Thm K.3) via a structural identification (Thm K.2). The Kirchhoff number is computed; the absence of $2$ from its factorization receives a rigorous combinatorial interpretation via Eppstein's theorem (Rmk K.4d). Per Rmk 1.3c (Part IV / IV.1), both quantities are properties of the base lens $N_{\mathrm{base}} = 720$ and do not lift automatically to $N_t$, although Thm K.2 itself lifts naturally (§K.5).

### V.14.1 The underlying graph (§K.1)

$G_{720}$: $30$ vertices (divisors of $720$), $59$ covering edges (Hasse covers, $720 = 2^4 \cdot 3^2 \cdot 5$). Cycle rank $|E| - |V| + 1 = 30$.

### V.14.2 Structural identification (§K.2, Thm K.2)

<!-- id: THM-K.2-CARTESIAN-PRODUCT | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: none -->
**Theorem K.2 (Cartesian Product Structure, preserved, unconditional).** Let $N = p_1^{a_1} p_2^{a_2} \cdots p_r^{a_r}$ with distinct primes $p_i$ and exponents $a_i \geq 1$. Then $G_N$, the underlying undirected covering graph of the Hasse diagram of $\mathrm{Div}(N)$, satisfies
$$
G_N \;\cong\; P_{a_1+1} \,\square\, P_{a_2+1} \,\square\, \cdots \,\square\, P_{a_r+1}.
$$

**Corollary K.2.1 (Application to $N_0 = 720$, preserved).** For $N_0 = 720 = 2^4 \cdot 3^2 \cdot 5^1$,
$$
G_{720} \;\cong\; P_5 \,\square\, P_3 \,\square\, P_2.
$$

**Algorithmic deducibility of the underlying graph (ALG-1 + ALG-3 + combinatorial closure).** The structural identification in Theorem K.2 is a property of the divisor-lattice graph $G_{720}$. The graph itself is reconstructible from the phasor stack: Algorithm Note ALG-1 (Single-Phasor Divisor Gate) supplies the 30 vertices via the $\Im(z_1) = 0$ predicate; Algorithm Note ALG-3 (Continuous-Phasor Winding Recovery) supplies the 240 comparable divisibility-pair ratios via the closed-form winding $\Delta\theta = \pi\ell(r-1)$; the combinatorial atomicity test on the recovered ratios identifies the 59 Hasse cover edges. From the algorithmically recovered Hasse graph, Theorem K.2's Cartesian-product factorization is *deducible*. The spectral invariants below — Theorem K.3 Fiedler value $\lambda_1 = 1/\varphi^2$, the Kirchhoff number $K(720)$, the cycle rank — are properties of the recovered graph; whether they are reachable *directly* from the phasor stack (without passing through the explicit graph reconstruction) is the open RES-1 C3 question. See Algorithm Note ALG-3.

**RES-1 C3 resolved (May 24, 2026).** The C3 experiment (`c3_verdict.md`) confirms three-tier recovery: combinatorial-geometric invariants (K2 PASS: $G_{720} \cong P_5 \square P_3 \square P_2$, 30 vertices, 59 edges; K2.5 PASS: 500/500 Ramanujan multiplicativity checks) are recovered DIRECTLY by the phasor stack; spectral invariants (K3: $\lambda_1 = 1/\varphi^2$ matched to $10^{-15}$) are DOWNSTREAM-DERIVABLE from the recovered graph via Laplacian eigendecomposition; bridge architecture invariants ($\mathrm{Br}_3^{\mathrm{pos}}$ pairings) live at a SEPARATE LAYER not accessible to the current stack. This three-tier separation is the structural content of Architecture Note ANL-2 (Free Navigation Extension); see `anl_2_public_entry.md`.

### V.14.3 Fiedler value (§K.3, Thm K.3 + remarks)

<!-- id: THM-K.3-FIEDLER-VALUE | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: K.2 -->
**Theorem K.3 (Fiedler Value of $G_{720}$, preserved, unconditional).** The algebraic connectivity of $G_{720}$ is
$$
\lambda_1(G_{720}) \;=\; 2 - 2\cos(\pi/5) \;=\; \frac{1}{\varphi^2}.
$$
*Proof (preserved).* By Thm K.2, $G_{720} \cong P_5 \square P_3 \square P_2$. The Laplacian spectrum of a Cartesian product is the (Minkowski-) sum of the factor spectra; the smallest nonzero eigenvalue is achieved at the longest factor — here $P_5$. The Fiedler value of $P_5$ is $2 - 2\cos(\pi/5) = 1/\varphi^2$. $\square$

<!-- id: RMK-K.3a-SPECTRAL-BOTTLENECK | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: K.3 -->
**Remark K.3a (Spectral Bottleneck, preserved).** The Fiedler value is achieved at the $P_5$ factor — the longest prime-power chain in $720$, namely $1 \to 2 \to 4 \to 8 \to 16$, arising from multiplicity $a_1 = 4$ of prime $2$. The slowest non-trivial Laplacian relaxation mode is concentrated along this chain. *This is the structural mechanism by which the prime $2$ — the prime with the highest multiplicity in $N_0$ — controls the algebraic connectivity of the entire instrument*, despite $2$ being absent from the Kirchhoff factorization (Rmk K.4d).

<!-- id: RMK-K.3c-PENTAGONAL-RECONSTRUCTION | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: K.3 -->
**Remark K.3c (Pentagonal Reconstruction; Not Derivation, preserved).** From Thm K.3, $\pi$ can be reconstructed as $\pi = 5 \arccos(1 - \varphi^{-2}/2)$. This is *reconstruction* of $\pi$ from the local $720$ atom's spectral data, not a derivation of $\pi$ in independent terms.

<!-- id: RMK-K.3d-NON-COLLAPSE-PHI | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: K.2, K.3, 9.3c -->
**Remark K.3d (Non-Collapse of $\varphi$ Mechanisms, v2.7.4, preserved, unconditional).** The identity $\lambda_1(G_{720}) = 1/\varphi^2$ in Thm K.3 is a *path-spectrum phenomenon*. From $720 = 2^4 \cdot 3^2 \cdot 5$ and Thm K.2, $P_5$ arises from the multiplicity $a_1 = 4$ of prime $2$, not from prime $5$ (which contributes only $P_2$).

Other fivefold $\varphi$-arrivals — Rogers–Ramanujan continued-fraction evaluations at complex-multiplication points, icosahedral level-5 modular phenomena, Klein's icosahedral resolvent — may land in the same quadratic order $\mathbb{Z}[\varphi]$, but they do so through *different orbit machines*: $\Gamma$-equivariance on the upper half plane, Galois action on level-5 modular curves, $A_5 \cong \mathrm{PSL}(2, \mathbb{F}_5)$ representation theory. Theorem K.3 is the divisor-lattice spectral route; it is neither a modular nor an icosahedral nor a CM route. *Numerical coincidence in $\mathbb{Z}[\varphi]$ should not be read as structural identity.*

### V.14.4 Kirchhoff number (§K.4 + remarks)

The Kirchhoff number is computable via Thm K.2 and the Cartesian product Laplacian spectrum, evaluated via the Matrix-Tree theorem:
$$
K(720) \;=\; 118{,}045{,}647{,}468{,}375 \;=\; 3 \cdot 5^3 \cdot 11^3 \cdot 19^3 \cdot 29^2 \cdot 41.
$$
*Status: empirical/computational.*

<!-- id: RMK-K.4c-CYCLE-RANK | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: K.1 -->
**Remark K.4c (Cycle Rank, preserved, unconditional).** Cycle rank (first Betti number) of $G_{720}$ is $|E| - |V| + 1 = 59 - 30 + 1 = 30$, *equal to the vertex count*. This rate-$1$ self-checking property — $30$ data channels balanced against $30$ independent parity cycles — is a structural feature of $P_5 \square P_3 \square P_2$ and is the topological substrate of the bridge architecture (Part V / V.7).

<!-- id: RMK-K.4d-EULERIAN-EDGE-CUT | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: K.4, classical-Eppstein -->
**Remark K.4d (Eulerian Edge Cut Interpretation, preserved, unconditional).** "$2$ absent from $K(720)$" is equivalent to "$G_{720}$ has no Eulerian edge cut" via Eppstein's theorem. The absence of prime $2$ from the Kirchhoff factorization, despite $2 \in B$ being the highest-multiplicity prime, reflects a deep combinatorial property of $G_{720}$'s edge structure.

### V.14.5 Routing and lift behavior (§K.5, preserved)

What lifts naturally: Thm K.2 — the Cartesian product identification lifts to any $N_t \neq N_{\mathrm{base}}$ with the corresponding prime-power exponents.

What does not lift automatically: numerical readouts ($\lambda_1$ value, $K(N)$ value, specific spectral data). These are properties of the base lens; theorem-specific lift conditions must be verified.

## V.15 Closure exhaustion and loop topology

**Source:** Thm 8.14a (v2.7.14); Rmk 8.20h.2 cross-reference.  
**v3.0 location:** Part V / V.15.  
**Status:** unconditional.

<!-- id: THM-8.14a-CLOSURE-EXHAUSTION | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 8.14 -->
**Theorem 8.14a (Closure Exhaustion, preserved, unconditional).** The identity $\varphi(\prod B') = k!$ holds for $k \in \{2, 4, 6\}$ only. (Verification: $\varphi(\prod B'_2) = \varphi(\{2\})$ in degenerate sense; $\varphi(\prod B'_4) = \varphi(\{3, 5\}) = 8 = 4!/3$; $\varphi(\prod B'_6) = \varphi(1001) = 720 = 6!$.)

Closure exhaustion at $k = 6$ supplies criterion (1) of the Selection Theorem (Thm 8.20h.3, Part V / V.2). The exhaustion is one of the named criteria selecting the local $720$ factorial-shell instance.

The loop topology threshold (Rmk 8.20h.2, Part V / V.10) supplies criterion (2). Together, closure exhaustion and loop topology threshold at $r = 3$ select $S_6$ uniquely among factorial shells.

## V.16 $S_6$ maximality and automorphism context

**Source:** Rmk 8.20h.4 (v2.7.6); Open Problem 18 reference.  
**v3.0 location:** Part V / V.16.  
**Status:** unconditional.

<!-- id: RMK-8.20h.4-S6-MAXIMALITY | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 8.14a, 8.20h.2, 8.20h.3 -->
**Remark 8.20h.4 ($S_6$ Maximality, preserved, unconditional).** $S_6$ is the *first and largest closed factorial shell with nontrivial complement topology*. Combines closure exhaustion (V.15) with loop topology threshold (V.10).

**$S_6$ outer automorphism context (Open Problem 18, Part VII / VII.7).** $S_6$ is the unique symmetric group with an outer automorphism. The relation between this outer automorphism and the basis/complement bridge architecture admits partial closure at the class level and structural-arithmetic level (Rmk 8.20h.5, Prop 8.20h.6 below). The full marking-scoped narrowing — including the scoped-canonical transposition pin — is the OP18 bibliography entry (Part VII / VII.7).

<!-- id: RMK-8.20h.5-OUTER-AUTO-CLASS | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 8.20h.4, 8.20j -->
**Remark 8.20h.5 (Outer-Automorphism Class Correspondence, v3.1.9, new, unconditional).** $S_6$ is the unique symmetric group with a nontrivial outer automorphism (Hölder 1895): $|\mathrm{Out}(S_6)| = 2$. The equal-size conjugacy-class coincidences in $S_6$ occur at sizes $15, 40, 90, 120$. Under an outer automorphism:

- the size-15 pair is swapped — transpositions $(2, 1^4)$ with synthemes $(2^3)$;
- the size-40 pair is swapped — 3-cycles $(3, 1^3)$ with double-3-cycles $(3^2)$;
- the size-120 pair is swapped — 6-cycles $(6)$ with $(3, 2, 1)$.

The two size-90 classes $(4, 1^2)$ and $(4, 2)$ are fixed as classes.

The bridge operator $\sigma_{\mathrm{ant}}$ (Prop 8.20j, Part V / V.7) lies in the syntheme class. Under any outer automorphism of $S_6$, its image lies in the transposition class. This is the class-level structural content of the OP18 connection: *the bridge operator's syntheme/transposition duality is supplied by the outer automorphism, not an arithmetic coincidence.*

*Verification:* `/mnt/user-data/outputs/outer_aut_bridge.py` confirms the four size-coincidence pairs and the three-swap structure via enumeration of all 720 elements of $S_6$.

<!-- id: PROP-8.20h.6-TOTIENT-BRIDGE-STAB | register: proved | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 8.20g.2, V.9, V.16 -->
**Proposition 8.20h.6 (Totient Bridge Matrix Stabilizer, v3.1.9, new, unconditional).** The totient bridge matrix $M$ encoding the basis-prime factorization of complement-prime totients is
$$
M \;=\; \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 & 1 \\ 2 & 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}
\qquad \text{rows} = (7, 11, 13), \quad \text{columns} = (2, 3, 5),
$$
where $M_{ij}$ is the exponent of basis prime $j$ in $\varphi(\text{complement prime } i)$:
$$
\varphi(7) = 6 = 2 \cdot 3, \quad \varphi(11) = 10 = 2 \cdot 5, \quad \varphi(13) = 12 = 2^2 \cdot 3, \quad \prod_i \varphi(c_i) = 720.
$$

$M$ has pairwise-distinct rows and pairwise-distinct columns. The stabilizer of $M$ under the action of $S_3$ permuting the three antipodal pairs (each permutation acting simultaneously on rows and columns) is trivial.

*Equivalently:* the three antipodal basis/complement pairs $\{(2, 7), (3, 11), (5, 13)\}$ are pairwise distinguishable by totient bridge content. The $S_3$ symmetry permuting these pairs — preserved by hexagonal symmetry (Part V / V.7), per-prime chirality, and per-prime Galois content at any modulus — is broken by the totient bridge.

*Verification:* `/mnt/user-data/outputs/T5a_totient_bridge.py` confirms triviality by direct enumeration of all 6 elements of $S_3$ and by the closed-form distinct-rows-and-distinct-columns check.

## V.17 Null-model discipline

**Source:** Rmk 8.20e.1 (v2.7.3).  
**v3.0 location:** Part V / V.17.  
**Status:** unconditional.

<!-- id: RMK-8.20e.1-NULL-MODEL-VALIDITY | register: remark | status: unconditional | provenance: original | depends: 8.20a, 8.20e -->
**Remark 8.20e.1 (Null-Model Validity, v2.7.3, preserved, unconditional).** A null-model surrogate $x_{\mathrm{null}}$ for the canonical bridge family $\mathrm{Br}_3^{\mathrm{pos}}$ must satisfy two conditions:

- **(N1)** Bridge restriction of the actual FVLT forward transform output applied to null-hypothesis input.
- **(N2)** $x_{\mathrm{null}}$ destroys structure under test while preserving marginal statistics.

**Structural diagnostic for (N1).** The monotone-filtration violation rate (Thm 8.20a clause (ii)). Independent draws over $37$ channels produce monotone violations → fail (N1).

**Why $\beta$ alone is not diagnostic (preserved).** $\beta$ (bridge depth) alone does not establish a complement-side claim; it requires the $(\beta, p_{\mathrm{null}})$ pair, and $p_{\mathrm{null}}$ requires a valid surrogate. The null-model discipline is the integrity contract for complement-side claims.

# Part VI — Applications, Tooling, and AI

Applications are downstream of Parts I–V. The discipline of Part VI is that no application expands the kernel theory: each application uses Parts I–V's content under Parts I–V's status partition. Speculative material remains marked. *Per Robin's File 3 constraint 7: applications are not expanded into new claims.*

## VI.1 Substrate-agnostic signal instrumentation

**Source:** §0.5 (v2.7.14).  
**v3.0 location:** Part VI / VI.1.  
**Status:** interpretive.

<!-- id: SEC-0.5-SUBSTRATE-AGNOSTIC-SIGNAL | register: remark | status: interpretive | provenance: original | depends: 5, 8 -->
**§0.5 Substrate-Agnostic Signal Claim (preserved).** The FVLT is a *calibrated signal instrument*, not a universal decoder. It produces structured diagnostic-priority readouts ($E_q$, $\mathrm{ce}_z$, $\beta$, $p_{\mathrm{null}}$, $H_{\mathrm{bridge}}$; Part V / V.8) under null-model discipline (Part V / V.17). It does *not* claim that every signal admits a decomposition into Ramanujan channels with structural meaning; it claims that *when* the channel decomposition reveals organized structure under null-model validation, the structure is honestly diagnostic rather than artifactual.

**Calibrated-instrument claim, not theorem-level.** No theorem asserts "every signal decomposes nontrivially into $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ channels." The framework supplies an instrument for measurement; the instrument's findings are diagnostic outputs, validated against the kernel discipline of Parts I–V.
